#10556. Aibo, Sony’s robot dog, now has a second life in hospitals

The people at Sony don’t use words like “product” or “reboot” when they talk about Aibo, the Japanese electronics and entertainment company’s robotic dog.

Aibo robots, which first went on sale in 1999, are all sold out and no longer in production. But some owners who had to part with their Aibo have donated the robots to Tokyo-based Sony Group Corp. for an “adoption program,” where they get treated to repairs and upgrades.

Those robot pets now have new homes in hospitals and other facilities, bringing solace and smiles to patients and staff.

Sony does not disclose the pricing or the number of Aibo robots that have been sold.

Packed with sensors, digital cameras and an intelligence of sorts, Aibo has been programmed to remember its name, the owner’s face and its surroundings — details of everyday life that make for a disarming illusion of a personality.

The robot barks, cocks its head, moves its ears, waddles toward you, acts happy when petted and goes to “sleep” — in short it simulates a puppy, except this one can take snapshots and monitors its own mechanical dysfunctions.

“The owners feel as though they are sending off what has become to them like their own child,” said Sony official Yoshiyuki Katsuno, who oversees the program.

Aibo gets people talking to each other, and can offer tired souls a perk to pick themselves up and keep going, according to Katsuno.

“We hope to spread the emotional healing and human interaction that Aibo has to offer,” he said.

At the St. Marianna University School of Medicine in Kawasaki, on the outskirts of Tokyo, a nurse had to do some looking to find a wandering Aibo during a recent visit.

But Aibo has helped with the long waits for visiting patients and succeeded in persuading reluctant children to get examined, says Dr. Manabu Komori of the department of otolaryngology.

That field involves diagnosing and treating sicknesses related to the ear, nose and throat, with many child patients.

“It helps motivate the kids,” Komori said. “Going to a hospital is basically not very pleasant.”

The hospital also uses real dogs to help patients feel better, though the animals require care and food and can be temperamental. That means a robot dog offers some advantages, although Komori was careful not to say they were better than real dogs.

Cuteness is seen as desirable in Japanese culture, where being “kawaii,” or “cute,” is not a sign of weakness or inferiority, but an endearing trait that encourages harmony and communication — both valued in Japanese society.

And so, if the need is for a soulmate, it’s better to have that cute look, be it a dog, cat or even a duck, said Jerry Wang, Executive Chairman of FF EAI Robotics Ecosystem, a U.S. company that offers more foreboding-looking four-legged patrol robots.

“If you want some emotional attachment, you want a cute shape,” he said.

Yuri Kageyama is on Threads: https://www.threads.com/@yurikageyama

—Yuri Kageyama, AP Business Writer

#10554. Elon Musk is giving away nearly $18 billion in SpaceX stock. Here’s who can get it

Your kid is becoming quite the capitalist.

Every U.S. child with an active Trump account will soon receive two shares of SpaceX, the space exploration company run by Elon Musk. This donation marks the latest in a string of philanthropic initiatives focused on these new types of tax-advantaged investment accounts.

SpaceX announced Wednesday that the company will donate one share of its Class A common stock—currently trading for more than $130 per share—to every currently active Trump account. In turn, CEO Musk will match that donation, which he later confirmed on X.

“Every child in America will receive two SpaceX shares, one from the company and one from me personally,” Musk posted. 

In its official announcement, SpaceX touted its future-focused mission of building new infrastructure that could achieve multiplanetary living. “We believe ownership of the company creating these systems should extend to the next generation of Americans for their long-term benefit.”

Here’s what we know about the donations.

MILLIONS OF NEW SHAREHOLDERS

The portfolios of the country’s youngest generation of investors keep getting fatter. Launched earlier this year, Trump accounts allow deposits of up to $5,000 per year for each child until the age of 18 and various billionaires have even thrown some money into the mix.

Musk now joins the likes of Michael Dell and Ray Dalio, who have similarly pledged donations to Trump accounts. The U.S. Treasury Department, too, will make a one-time $1,000 deposit to every child born between Jan. 1, 2025, and Dec. 31, 2028.

The SpaceX shares work a little differently in that the company will transfer one share of its stock to active Trump accounts in December, according to Musk’s post. Nearly 70 million accounts have been created thus far, the White House reported last month, though that’s largely a result of a change by the Treasury to allow for automatic enrollment. 

Musk’s matching donation will come later, he said on X, sometime after mid-June 2027. That’s when a 366-day lockup expires that he agreed to as part of the SpaceX initial public offering earlier this year.

The Starbase, Texas-based company noted that Gwynne Shotwell, its president and chief operating officer, previously pledged with her husband to donate more than 2 million shares to Trump accounts. But there are strict eligibility requirements associated with those donations, which parents can check online.

Combined, the gifts from the company, Musk, and Shotwell could have a value of more than $18 billion given the stock’s current price and if all eligible Trump accounts have been claimed. These donations also do something that’s delving into new territory: They guarantee that potentially millions of Americans will become long-term shareholders of SpaceX. 

YOU GET A STOCK!

Just as Oprah famously announced a new car to every person in her studio audience back in the early 2000s, Musk and other billionaires are saying the same to kids now: “You get a stock!”

But that could create a conundrum of sorts for parents as more and more money is likely to flow into these accounts from billionaires. 

The Treasury recently implemented new rules that allow companies and other donors to gift individual stocks to these accounts, while also limiting trading activity in them, as The Wall Street Journal reported. Children and their parents can neither pick nor reject stocks that are donated to a child’s account—and they can’t sell them for five years.

And yet, there’s free money on the line. If parents don’t claim an account on behalf of their kid, their kid misses out on a potentially lucrative portfolio. Despite the move to automatic enrollment, some manual steps are still required, which means parents need to claim an account by downloading an app from the Trump accounts website. 

Musk couldn’t help but tout the prospects of what that could mean to millions of prospective shareholders—something that resonated with investors, too, as the stock has jumped nearly 5% in the past two days of trading.

“If SpaceX succeeds in making life multiplanetary and harnessing even a millionth of the Sun’s energy, it will be worth about a million times the current global economy,” Musk posted. “These are obviously absurdly difficult goals, but there is a path to achieving them. I recommend holding on to your shares.”

#10553. Which 2026 Samsung Galaxy Tab should you buy?

Samsung has a huge lineup of Galaxy tablets to choose from right now, and they’re all a little different.

The Galaxy Tab S12+ is the Samsung tablet to choose for a premium mix of entertainment, creativity, and productivity. The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra gives you an even bigger canvas, while the Galaxy Tab S11 brings flagship features to a more portable size. For everyday value, start with the Galaxy Tab S10 FE series or the affordable Galaxy Tab A11+.

Samsung’s tablet lineup covers everything from watching a show on the couch to sketching your next project or getting work done between meetings. The right choice comes down to what you want that extra screen to do.

With options across a wide range of price points, Samsung has a Galaxy tablet for different needs and budgets this holiday season.

Here’s a list of the main Galaxy Tab options available in 2026, from family-friendly entertainment to Samsung’s largest creative workspace:

Screen size is only one part of the whole story. The Galaxy Tab S11, S12+, and S12 Ultra have AMOLED displays that offer rich contrast for movies and photos, while the FE models provide useful everyday features at a more accessible price.

The Galaxy Tab S12+ is the sweet spot if you want a serious upgrade over a basic tablet without moving to Samsung’s biggest screen.

As we covered in our Galaxy Tab S12 launch story, it combines a 12.6-inch 120Hz AMOLED display with a MediaTek Dimensity 9500 processor, 12GB of RAM, and an included S Pen, with four months of complimentary Adobe Photoshop access and a six-month Notability trial, both of which support the stylus.

The S12+ is easy to use and appreciate in daily life. It puts reference material beside your notes during a course, sketch ideas for a presentation, while making it easy to settle into a movie after finishing your email. It has room to spread out without requiring the Ultra’s larger footprint.

The Galaxy Tab S12+ also offers a 5G version, making it worth considering if you regularly work away from Wi-Fi.

Buy the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra when the screen itself is the upgrade you’ll enjoy every day.

The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra is made for creative professionals and families seeking premium entertainment. It moves naturally between focused creative work and immersive movie nights at home.

Its 14.6-inch AMOLED display gives photos, drawings, and multiple app windows more breathing room. Picture reviewing a document while keeping your handwritten notes visible, or drawing with reference images alongside your canvas.

The Ultra shares the S12+’s Dimensity 9500 processor but offers configurations up to 16GB of RAM and 1TB of storage. It’s available for $1,399.99, but last year’s Tab S11 Ultra is on sale during Black Friday for just $899.99.

Both S12 tablets ship with Android 17-based One UI 9 and seven years of OS and security updates. Photoshop comes pre-installed with a four-month access offer, giving you a way to explore S Pen-assisted photo editing.

The Ultra is especially appealing with a keyboard at a desk; Samsung’s Ultra Book Cover Keyboard Slim is the perfect companion to the S12 Ultra, and is on sale for $146.99 during Best Buy’s Black Friday Deal event.

Yes, the Galaxy Tab S11 is the premium Galaxy tablet to choose when portability comes first.

Our Galaxy Tab S11 launch coverage highlighted the return of Samsung’s smaller flagship tablet, with a Dimensity 9400+ processor, 12GB of RAM, and an included S Pen. Its 11-inch AMOLED screen makes it an attractive companion for reading, travel, and handwritten notes.

The Galaxy Tab S11 is the sort of tablet you can bring to a meeting or pull out on a train without needing a large work surface. You still get premium display quality, just in a size that’s easier to carry, and it comes in at a lower price.

A discounted Tab S11 Ultra can also be worth considering if you want a 14.6-inch screen. Compare the actual price with the S12 Ultra rather than dismissing an older flagship automatically.

The Galaxy Tab S10 FE and FE+ are compelling choices for people who want an included S Pen without flagship pricing.

Choose the 10.9-inch FE for a study companion that travels easily. Choose the 13.1-inch FE+ when you’d rather put a lecture, document, or recipe beside your notes. Both offer IP68 water and dust resistance, so they’re great kitchen companions.

Galaxy Tab S10 Lite is for students, children, and emerging creators. It is a practical, encouraging gift for handwritten learning, organization, drawing, and experimentation.

The Galaxy Tab A11+ is the straightforward entertainment choice. At $199, it’s the cheapest of the Galaxy Tab options, but it doesn’t support the S Pen, so choose an S-series model if handwriting matters.

Choose the Galaxy Tab S12+ for a premium all-rounder, the S12 Ultra for a spacious creative workspace, or the S11 for flagship features in a travel-friendly size.

For school, home projects, and everyday notes, the FE series delivers the features many people actually need. For family streaming and browsing, the A11+ keeps things simple and affordable.

The best Galaxy Tab isn’t automatically the biggest or newest. It’s the one that makes the things you already love doing easier — and gives you a few new possibilities along the way.

#10552. Build Live Video Chat With WebRTC: From First Call to Production Debugging

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Adding real-time video to a web app used to mean plugins, proprietary SDKs and a lot of money. Today every modern browser ships with WebRTC, a set of APIs that lets two browsers exchange audio, video and data directly. The catch is that WebRTC gives you building blocks, not a finished feature. This guide walks through those blocks in the order you actually need them, with the exact API calls you need, and then covers the production issues that tutorials usually skip.

A browser-to-browser call has four moving parts:

Start with the local preview. Browsers only allow camera access on HTTPS or localhost, so serve your page accordingly.

1.     Request a stream with navigator.mediaDevices.getUserMedia(), passing video constraints (an ideal width of 1280 and height of 720 is a sensible default) and audio constraints with echoCancellation and noiseSuppression enabled.

2.     Assign the returned MediaStream to the srcObject property of your local video element.

3.     Keep a reference to the stream, because you will add its tracks to the peer connection later.

Set the muted and playsinline attributes on the local video element. Without muted users hear themselves, and without playsinline iOS Safari opens the video full screen.

The peers need a way to find each other. A tiny Node.js server using the ws package is enough for development. Its whole job is to relay messages between the peers in a room:

1.     Create a WebSocket server (for example with the ws package) listening on its own port.

2.     Keep a map of room names to the set of sockets in each room.

3.     When a client sends a join message, store the room name on the socket and add it to that room's set.

4.     For every other message type, forward the raw message to the other sockets in the same room, and never echo it back to the sender.

5.     On disconnect, remove the socket from its room so stale peers do not receive messages.

In production you would add authentication, limit room size to two for one-to-one calls, and validate message types, but the shape stays the same.

Both sides create an RTCPeerConnection, add their local tracks and listen for the remote ones:

1.     Create a new RTCPeerConnection with an iceServers list that contains at least one STUN server.

2.     Loop over the local stream's tracks and call addTrack() for each one.

3.     In the ontrack handler, attach the first remote stream to your remote video element.

4.     In the onicecandidate handler, send every new candidate to the other peer through your signalling channel as a candidate message.

One peer creates an offer, the other answers, and both apply each other's descriptions. Candidates arrive asynchronously in the background.

1.     The caller runs createOffer(), applies it with setLocalDescription() and sends the description as an offer message.

2.     The callee applies the offer with setRemoteDescription(), runs createAnswer(), applies it locally and sends it back as an answer.

3.     The caller applies the answer with setRemoteDescription().

4.     Both sides pass every incoming candidate message to addIceCandidate().

If both peers might start a call at the same time, look up the "perfect negotiation" pattern on MDN. It assigns one side as polite and resolves offer collisions cleanly instead of leaving both peers stuck.

On a home network, STUN alone often works. On corporate Wi-Fi, mobile carriers and symmetric NATs it frequently does not, and the call silently fails to connect. A TURN server relays media when a direct path is impossible. The open-source coturn server is the usual choice.

In practice, your iceServers list should contain your own STUN URL plus a TURN URL with a username and credential, so the browser can fall back to the relay automatically when direct connectivity fails.

Generate short-lived TURN credentials on your backend rather than hard-coding them, and budget for bandwidth: relayed calls pass every frame through your server.

Users deny camera access, unplug headsets and switch from Wi-Fi to 4G mid-call. Catch NotAllowedError and show a clear explanation, listen for devicechange events, and use RTCRtpSender.replaceTrack() to swap cameras without renegotiating the whole call.

Listen to pc.onconnectionstatechange. When the state becomes disconnected, wait a few seconds before giving up, because it often recovers. On failed, call pc.restartIce() to try new network paths.

Use RTCRtpSender.setParameters() to cap the bitrate on weak connections, and read pc.getStats() to monitor packet loss and round-trip time. Showing a small "poor connection" indicator does more for perceived quality than any codec tweak.

Mesh calls, where every participant connects to every other, work for two or three people. Beyond that, upload bandwidth explodes. Group calls need a Selective Forwarding Unit (SFU) such as mediasoup, LiveKit or Janus, which receives each stream once and forwards it to the others.

Tutorials end at a working demo, but the fastest way to understand what production WebRTC looks like is to watch a real call from the inside. Chrome ships an internal diagnostics page that shows every peer connection the browser opens, and it works on any site that uses WebRTC.

1.     Open chrome://webrtc-internals in one tab and keep it open.

2.     In another tab, start a call on your own demo and find its RTCPeerConnection entry.

3.     Expand that entry and look at the ICE candidate pairs: you will see whether the call went direct (host or srflx) or through a TURN relay (relay).

4.     Open the stats graphs for the outbound video stream and watch the bitrate, frame rate and resolution adapt when you throttle the network in DevTools.

5.     Check the negotiated codec and how quickly the connection recovers when you switch Wi-Fi off and back on.

Once you can read your own call, repeat the exercise on a production service. Any browser-based product works, whether a meeting tool or a live video chat that pairs two visitors, because webrtc-internals shows the same data for every site. Expect differences from your demo: more candidate pairs, a relay candidate in the list and a bitrate that adapts faster. Seeing them makes the earlier sections concrete, from why TURN matters to how bandwidth estimation reacts to packet loss.

The one-to-one call above is the core of many products, from telehealth consultations and tutoring to customer support. The engineering challenges grow with the use case: random matching needs queueing and presence on the signalling server, public platforms need moderation and reporting, and global audiences need TURN servers in several regions.

Start with the minimal version, get it working across Chrome, Firefox and Safari on both desktop and mobile, and add TURN before real users arrive. Once the call is reliable, every other feature becomes a much easier problem.

#10551. A popular password manager was hacked. Here’s what you need to know

Password managers are the safest way short of secret sticky notes to keep track of the hundreds of logins in your life, but even they don’t have perfect security.

Popular Password manager 1Password disclosed this week that hackers breached its systems this month and accessed the software’s source code. While no breach is good news, the attackers were not able to access 1Password users’ most sensitive data, including their passwords, secret keys, and information stored in user vaults. 

“At no time were customer vault contents accessed by, or available to, the attacker,” the company emphasized in a blog post on the incident. The security of the software maker’s apps and browser extensions was also not affected by the breach.

The attackers were able to burrow into 1Password’s system after gaining access to an employee’s corporate GitHub account. By pushing malicious code into its backend systems, the attackers made off with administrative data on a subset of 1Password customers, including customer names, email addresses, and “non-secret internal metadata” about accounts. 

Anyone who uses 1Password doesn’t need to take extra steps right now, but they should be extra vigilant for phishing attempts in the future. That includes being skeptical of “unexpected emails, texts or calls that mention 1Password, invitations, or account recovery attempts,” according to the company. The password manager will never ask its customers for an account password or a secret key.

In response, 1Password says that it has disabled the compromised GitHub account, rotated and revoked exposed internal credentials, and brought in external cybersecurity experts to advise the software maker on its response, among other measures.

Security for everybody

The world of cybersecurity moves fast – and now that cycle is speeding up even in light of AI. Security companies are leveraging artificial intelligence to detect threats at the same time that malicious actors are putting it to work cracking into accounts at scale.

1Password CTO Nancy Wang described the unique challenges and opportunities the company faces in light of widespread AI adoption in a recent interview with Fast Company. While a sea of vibe-coded apps have introduced security holes human software makers would have caught, AI can also scan massive chunks of code for vulnerabilities. “AI’s been a mixed bag, just because that work has been so gnarly and technical,” Wang said.

Aside from the millions of people who rely on 1Password, more than 200,000 businesses use the password manager to keep track of their credentials. That number includes nearly a third of the largest companies in the U.S., according to 1Password. Fast Company named the software maker to its 2025 list of Brands That Matter for its user-friendly approach to security. 

“1Password was built on the belief that security should be accessible to everyone,” 1Password CEO David Faugno said last year. “In an industry that too often leads with fear and complexity, we’ve built our foundation on trust, usability, and the human experience.”

#10547. The 2026 Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade isn’t canceled. Here’s when you can watch it this weekend

Thanksgiving may be over, but one of the holiday’s biggest traditions is still to come.

For the first time in its history, the Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade is taking place on a day other than Thanksgiving. On Wednesday, Macy’s, NBC, and New York City officials made the unprecedented decision to postpone the parade until Saturday, November 28, after forecasts called for dangerous winds, heavy rain, and coastal flooding.

While the parade was canceled from 1942 through 1944 during World War II, weather has never before forced organizers to move the event to another day.

The timing is particularly noteworthy given that this year marks the parade’s 100th edition, a milestone Macy’s is celebrating with an expanded lineup of balloons, floats, musical performances, and celebrity appearances.

NBC aired a replay of the 2025 parade on Thanksgiving morning instead. The live celebration will take place Saturday, although the last-minute schedule change has forced some performers to adjust their plans.

Here’s what to expect from the rescheduled parade and how to watch it.

Who is performing at the 2026 Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade?

Savannah Guthrie, Hoda Kotb, and Al Roker are slated to return as hosts of NBC’s broadcast.

The originally announced musical lineup includes RAYE, Teddy Swims, Megan Moroney, Natasha Bedingfield, Queen Latifah, Jimmy Fallon and The Roots, JoJo, Smash Mouth, and Keke Palmer, among others.

A special Broadway performance featuring a 10-minute medley from nine musicals, including Hamilton, Wicked, and Rent, is still expected to take place. The Radio City Rockettes are also on the announced lineup.

However, not everyone who planned to participate on Thursday will be able to stick around for Saturday.

The University of Alabama’s Million Dollar Band, for instance, is returning home to perform at Saturday’s Iron Bowl football game. Macy’s Great American Marching Band is also unable to participate in the rescheduled event.

Other groups have turned to crowdfunding to cover the unexpected cost of extending their stays in New York. A high school marching band from Pearland, Texas, raised $350,000 in less than 24 hours to enable more than 500 students and staff members to stay for the parade.

How to watch the 2026 Macy’s Thanksgiving Day Parade

The rescheduled parade will step off at 8:30 a.m. ET on Saturday, November 28, and is expected to conclude around noon.

NBC will televise the parade, while cord-cutters can stream it on Peacock. A Spanish-language broadcast will also be available on Telemundo. Viewers with live TV streaming subscriptions that carry NBC, including YouTube TV, Hulu + Live TV, and Fubo, can also tune in.

If you’d rather sleep in on Saturday morning, NBC is scheduled to air an encore presentation at 2 p.m. ET/PT.

#10546. Claude rolling out updated Android app with Material 3 Expressive redesign

Anthropic is rolling out a new Claude app for Android that picks up a Material 3 Expressive redesign.

Confirmed on Twitter/X by Anthropic Product Manager Robert Bye, Claude is now rolling out a Material 3 Expressive redesign for its Android app that, while not a full embrace of Google’s design language, does make a clear attempt to better fit in on Android.

The updated design picks up a bottom-bar UI as opposed to relying fully on the old hamburger menu – it still exists, but it’s mainly for chat history now. You can see M3 elements throughout the app, including in the model switcher and the background of chats. It’s clearly themed to match Anthropic’s aesthetic, and there’s no obvious implementation of Material You colors, but it all fits within the general Material 3 guidelines – much more so than the original app.

The one flaw we spotted was the predictive back implementation, which gets a neat animation to move the search bar but is supposed to show a preview of the previous screen, but that’s just nit-picking, really.

The new Claude app for Android is rolling out now.

A quick, easy and cool way to turn off your rig

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

You've bought the big stuff: the SSDs, the RAM, the graphics card. Those are the obvious Black Friday deals for a PC gamer, but this is the perfect opportunity to step back and look at the gadgets and tools that you wouldn't normally think about, or even know about. That's where this SinLoon external power button comes in, on sale for just $11.19.

We'd normally run through all of the big, important specs here, but we don't really need to do that here because, well, there isn't any. You're buying an external power button. It connects to your PC. It turns it off, it turns it on, it restarts it (if you want it to). It has a two-meter cable that you can use to connect it to your PC, either using your PC's front panel with the supplied splitter or directly to your motherboard. It's also, by design, got a pair of mechanical keyboard keys: these'll be the ones you press to turn your rig on or off.

This gaming-friendly external power button has a 2-meter (78.74 inch) cable, along with a splitter that lets you connect both buttons to your case's front panel buttons. It also includes adhesive pads and zip-ties.

Simple enough, then, but that's not to downplay what you're getting here, or how it's been made. Mechanical keyboard keys are an interesting choice, because they look like they've been plucked straight from a top-spec gaming keyboard, especially with the RGB lighting underneath. It's a showstopper by design. SinLoon could have just gone for a big, round button, but by going for a design that uses mechanical keyboard keys, they're making sure this button has longevity.

That's why SinLoon reckons these keys are good for no less than five million keystrokes. You can switch your PC on or off five million times before this board wears out. That's a lot of restarts. The two keys themselves sit in a smooth, black plastic case. The wiring is black, too, and sheathed (mostly) in glossy plastic, extending from the back. It does eventually split into different colored cables, though, each labeled so you know their purpose and where you'll need to connect them.

While you will need to open up your PC to get this installed, it's an easy enough process, as long as your case lets you get close to your PC's front panel headers. PC builders won't have much trouble with this, as you've probably already dealt with these cables when you've hooked it up to your motherboard. It's the same process for these cables, and it comes with connectors to handle the power and disk access LEDs, too.

You don't have to sacrifice your PC's power buttons, either. This SinLoon power button comes with a 1-to-2 splitter, a physical adapter that lets you connect these cables alongside the cables handling your PC's power and reset buttons. It's a physical adapter, so if the pinout doesn't quite match up, you can just move the header cables around until it works. You can also totally bypass your PC's power button, if you prefer, by just connecting the cables up to your motherboard without the splitter.

You may not need this, but for $11, it's the ultimate buy for a techie's desk. You might not have ever thought about a desktop power button, but look: it's cool, and it's useful. It's not just a pointless desk ornament, but a practical tool that'll help you turn your rig on or off. If you've got your PC in a place that's difficult to reach, it solves an obvious problem, too.

You'll also find it useful if you're a maker or builder. For instance, open test rigs (like the ones we use at Tom's Hardware) might not have a case, just a motherboard on a desk with a bunch of parts attached. Rather than shorting the header pins to start your rig (not the best idea), you can attach this SinLoon button instead to make the whole process safer and easier.

The $11.19 sale price for this SinLoon external power button is cheap enough to make it easy to justify. It'll quickly improve a quality-of-life issue at your desk and it'll look cool doing it, especially if you've already got RGB peripherals on your desk to match. Whether it's for yourself or as a gift for a PC builder in your life, grab this cool desk item on sale while the discount lasts.

#10542. Hands-on: Baseus’ new PicoGo Pro USB-C charger is clever, cute, and on sale

USB-C chargers are pretty standardized at this point, but there’s a fun new trend of adding displays to your power brick that I’ve been very much enjoying, and the new PicoGo Pro from Baseus is a great example with three ports, optimized charging, and a cute little character to top it off – and for Black Friday, it’s pretty deeply discounted, too.

The Baseus PicoGo Pro (AH22) is a 100W, 3-port USB-C charger designed to top off your laptop, phone, and/or tablet, even all at the same time. For anyone who travels often or just has a lot of devices on hand, chargers like this are a must. Having something you don’t have to think twice about when it comes to charging rates is just so nice, and these have come a long way, too.

Using GaN tech, PicoGo Pro is very compact at about 2 inches tall and a little over an inch wide. I remember having single-port chargers that could barely fast-charge my phone that were twice this size just a few years ago. GaN just absolutely rules, and Baseus is using a special version that reduces heat and improves efficiency.

But the two reasons I really like this charger aren’t strictly to do with GaN.

The first is the built-in display. Again, that’s a trend throughout the industry. Anker has several chargers out with built-in displays as the company helped to really make this popular – the Anker Prime 160W brick is really similar to PicoGo Pro (at a higher price), and it’s the only I’ve been using for months before trying out this Baseus model.

Baseus has a cute little animated character that accompanies your charging, with little eyes when the charger isn’t active and some fun animations to show your charging rate in real-time. Neat! You can “press” the button to move through the UI and even see the specific charging rate of each connected device. You’ll also see that the brick can recognize the device you have plugged in, though Android devices just show a generic “SAMSUNG” or whatever brand you plug in – Anker, again, had a similar set of features in a brick released earlier in 2026, but some of those features were iPhone-only, and that brick was only a 1-port, 45W charger anyway.

But, like that Anker brick, Baseus supports “smart” charging that adjusts to your device. Plugging in a Galaxy Z Fold 8 that was around 65% full, I saw the brick adjust its charging speed to tailor to the phone, initially going up to full speed before settling down around 28W~. You can manually adjust this with “Turbo” or “Gentle” modes, the former maxing out the charging rate and the latter settling in around 15W for a slower charge. That flexibility is great because it means you can adjust the charging rate to what you need in that moment. Full-speed for a quick top-off on a busy day, slower charging overnight to preserve battery health.

Baseus isn’t doing much new here, though the GaN tech mentioned earlier is enhanced by some architecture and algorithm adjustments the company has made behind the scenes, but it’s a really good implementation.

Beyond that, it’s also priced pretty well.

Baseus PicoGo Pro’s full price is $59.99, but you’ll probably rarely see it there. Technically, today is its launch date, and it’s already down by over 30% at $39.70 for Black Friday. Value-wise, this one is hard to beat.

#10541. Building Email Integrations: APIs, Webhooks, Automation, and Deliverability

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Email remains an important communication channel for web applications. From account verification and password resets to product announcements and customer notifications, many applications depend on email to keep users informed.

For developers, however, implementing email functionality involves more than connecting a service and sending a message. A reliable email system needs to handle authentication, dynamic content, delivery failures, user preferences, event tracking, and integrations with other application components.

Using an email service provider (ESP) can simplify this process. Most modern providers offer REST APIs, SMTP relays, templates, automation features, and reporting tools that developers can integrate into existing applications.

This guide explores the technical considerations involved in building email integrations, including API-based sending, webhook handling, automation workflows, and deliverability.

There are two common ways to connect an application to an email delivery service: SMTP and REST APIs.

SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) is a standard protocol used to transfer email messages between servers. Developers can connect an application to an SMTP server using credentials and configuration settings.

REST APIs provide another approach. Instead of communicating through an email server protocol, an application sends HTTP requests to an endpoint. The provider processes the request and returns a response.

Both methods can support transactional and application-generated emails, but their implementation differs.

An application already supports SMTP configuration.

Developers want a relatively straightforward integration.

Existing frameworks or libraries provide built-in SMTP support.

An application needs programmatic control over email operations.

Developers want structured responses and error handling.

Email functionality needs to integrate with other application services.

The system requires event tracking or additional API capabilities.

The right approach depends on the application's architecture, expected email volume, and integration requirements.

An API integration allows an application to trigger emails in response to specific events.

For example, an ecommerce application might send an order confirmation after a successful payment. A SaaS application could send an account verification message after registration.

A typical integration follows this process:

The backend prepares the recipient and message data.

The backend authenticates with the email provider.

The application records the response and handles any errors.

Consider a Node.js application that needs to trigger an email campaign through an API.

Some platforms, including Mailmodo, provide REST API functionality for triggering campaigns and integrating external applications with email workflows.

The following example demonstrates the general structure of an HTTP request using a campaign-triggering endpoint:

This example uses the documented Mailmodo trigger endpoint and request structure. The campaign ID and API key must be replaced with the values from the relevant account.

Notice that the API key is loaded from an environment variable rather than written directly into the source code.

This is an important security practice. Credentials should never be exposed in frontend JavaScript, public repositories, or client-side application bundles.

Not every email provider offers the same integration capabilities. Developers should evaluate providers based on their API documentation, authentication methods, supported endpoints, event notifications, and integration flexibility.

For example, Elastic Email provides a REST API that developers can use to send emails and manage different email operations from an application.

Its API uses HTTPS requests and API-key authentication. Developers can use the available endpoints to send messages, manage campaigns, retrieve information, and configure webhook notifications.

When evaluating an email API, consider these technical requirements:

Authentication: Check how API keys are generated, stored, restricted, and rotated.

Documentation: Look for clear endpoint descriptions, request examples, response formats, and error codes.

SDK availability: Official libraries can reduce the amount of integration code required.

Permission management: Separate credentials for sending emails and accessing account information can reduce unnecessary exposure.

API limitations: Review connection limits, request timeouts, rate limits, and account-level restrictions before deploying an integration.

A provider with extensive features is not necessarily the best option for every project. The important consideration is whether its API supports the application's actual requirements.

Sending an email is only one part of the communication process. Applications may also need to know what happens after a message is submitted.

A webhook allows an external service to send an HTTP notification to an application when a particular event occurs.

Depending on the provider, events may include:

For example, an application could receive a delivery-failure notification and update the corresponding email record in its database.

A basic Express.js webhook endpoint might look like this:

This is a simplified example rather than a complete provider-specific webhook implementation. A production endpoint should validate the provider's signature or other authentication mechanism and follow its documented payload structure.

It should also handle duplicate events safely. Webhook providers may retry notifications when an application does not respond successfully.

An idempotent event handler prevents the same notification from creating duplicate database updates or triggering repeated actions.

Email automation connects application events with predefined communication sequences.

Instead of manually sending every message, developers can create workflows that respond to user activity.

Consider a SaaS application with the following registration process:

If the user does not complete onboarding, a follow-up message may be triggered.

A simplified workflow can be represented as:

In a production application, email delivery should generally be handled through a background job queue rather than blocking the registration request.

A queue also makes it easier to retry temporary failures, manage sending volume, and prevent email delays from affecting the main application.

Developers should consider tools such as Redis-backed queues or framework-specific job processors when building larger systems.

Automation platforms can also help manage more complex journeys. For instance, a developer can connect application events to an external email workflow instead of implementing every communication sequence independently.

The key is to keep business logic in the application while using the email service to manage message delivery and relevant campaign operations.

A successful API response does not necessarily mean that an email has reached the recipient's inbox.

Email deliverability depends on several technical factors, including domain authentication, sender reputation, message quality, and recipient engagement.

Developers should pay attention to the following configurations.

Sender Policy Framework identifies the mail servers authorized to send messages on behalf of a domain.

A correctly configured SPF record helps receiving servers evaluate whether an email originates from an authorized source.

DomainKeys Identified Mail adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages. Receiving servers can use the corresponding public key in DNS to verify the signature.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance builds on SPF and DKIM. It allows domain owners to publish policies for handling messages that fail authentication checks and receive reports about authentication activity.

These three mechanisms should be configured carefully, especially when an application sends emails through multiple providers.

Email systems generate useful operational data. However, developers should avoid treating every available metric as an accurate measure of user engagement.

More reliable application-level signals can include:

These events can be connected with email campaign data to understand how communication affects application activity.

Developers should also maintain a clear separation between email delivery records and user account records. This makes it easier to investigate delivery issues without unnecessarily modifying customer data.

Before deploying an email integration, developers should review the following areas:

API security: Store credentials in environment variables or a dedicated secrets manager.

Domain authentication: Configure SPF, DKIM, and DMARC.

Error handling: Account for failed requests, invalid recipients, and temporary provider errors.

Background processing: Use queues for asynchronous email delivery.

Webhook validation: Verify incoming notifications and handle duplicate events.

Monitoring: Track delivery failures, bounce rates, and application-level outcomes.

Testing: Test email templates across different clients and devices before production deployment.

These practices help create an email system that is easier to maintain, troubleshoot, and scale.

Email integration is an important part of modern application development, but a reliable implementation requires more than connecting an SMTP server or calling an API endpoint.

Developers need to consider authentication, asynchronous processing, webhook security, automation logic, domain configuration, and event monitoring.

REST APIs are useful for applications that need programmatic control over email operations, while SMTP remains a practical option for systems that already support standard mail-server connections. Webhooks add another layer by allowing applications to respond to delivery events and update their internal records.

By choosing an integration method that fits the application architecture and following sound security and deliverability practices, developers can build email functionality that supports both immediate communication needs and long-term application growth.

You don't always need to spend big to get a killer gaming laptop

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Getting a gaming laptop this Black Friday doesn't have to be a hassle. In case you're after the best deals that we've found thus far across a range of configurations, in addition to explanations of why these models in particular should be on your radar. Getting a gaming laptop is pretty tough — different systems can tout similar specs, but in reality, their performance can vary wildly. This is down to different power ratings across mobile GPUs, which can muddy the waters when you're looking on storefronts. It's often obfuscated in listings or omitted entirely.

So we're here to cut through the noise and nonsense to bring you our picks of the best deals we've found thus far. We also have our Black Friday Gaming Laptop Deals Live Blog in action, so check that out if you want more options.

The Asus ROG Strix G16 comes with a 16-inch screen, with an 1900 x 1200 pixel resolution, a 16:10 aspect ratio, and refresh and response rates of 165Hz and 3ms, respectively. Hardware inside this gaming laptop includes Nvidia's GeForce RTX 5070 Ti laptop GPU, an AMD Ryzen 9 8940HX processor, 16GB of DDR5 RAM, and a 1TB SSD for storage.

This Asus ROG Strix G16 gaming laptop offers a solid price-to-performance ratio and comes with AMD's Ryzen 9 8940HX CPU, sporting 16 cores and 32 threads. Packing in the gaming punch is the included RTX 5070 Ti 12GB, with GDDR7 memory and a total graphics power of 115W; this is the full-fat variant of the chip, and dynamic boost can also see this rise to up to 140W. When paired with a relatively modest screen resolution and display, it certainly bodes well for getting solid performance from modern titles. And you should be able to saturate the display's refresh rate when turning on features like Multi Frame Generation in single-player titles.

One thing to keep in mind here is that the laptop does sacrifice display quality, and its CPU isn't on the latest Zen 5 architecture; it's based on AMD's Zen 4 Dragon Range refresh chips. But it's still mighty fast and should suit both gaming and productivity workloads.

Get a 16-inch gaming laptop with Intel Core Ultra 7 255HX, RTX 5070 Ti with 12GB of VRAM, 16GB of DDR5 RAM, and a 512GB SSD.

Coming in slightly cheaper than the above Asus ROG Strix system, this MSI Vector 16 HX AI laptop from Newegg offers much of the same specifications: right down to the TGP of the GPU. MSI's 5070 Ti mobile chip carries the same 115W TGP, in addition to the screen resolution. It also moves CPUs to Intel's 20-core, 20-thread Core Ultra 7 255HX, which is a newer architecture compared to the ROG Strix G16's Dragon Range offering. However, you get a lower screen refresh rate at 144Hz and half the storage included. It does trade blows effectively with the Asus model in many respects, though, including offering dual Thunderbolt 5 ports and a 2.5GbE port over the ROG Strix's 1GbE port. With the sizes of modern games, though, it's tough to outright tell you to run out and purchase a system with only 512 GB of storage, so this system remains our alternative pick for most people.

The MSI Crosshair sports a 16-inch, 240Hz display with a 2560 x 1600 resolution that's powered by an 115W max TGP Nvidia RTX 5060 graphics with 8GB of GDDR7 VRAM. Other specs include an AMD Ryzen 7 8840HX processor, 16GB of memory, and a 1TB SSD.

This MSI Crosshair 16 deal at Walmart sports a tricked-out 115W RTX 5060 GPU at its core, alongside a 12-core, 24-thread AMD Ryzen 7 8840HX Dragon Range CPU that can boost up to 5.1GHz. Sporting 1TB of NVMe storage and 16GB of DDR5 RAM, this more entry-level system can offer solid computing power while staying within a limited budget. Something that prospective buyers should watch out for is the display's resolution: you get a 16:10, 2560 x 1600 screen resolution and 240Hz refresh rate on this system, meaning that the laptop will likely struggle in modern titles, especially at high settings. Turning on upscaling tech like DLSS might help smooth things over somewhat, but given that you'll be doing things other than gaming on this laptop, it makes for a comfortable everyday system that offers a sharp display.

Lenovo's Legion 5i sports a 15.3-inch, 3K display that's powered by an Intel Core 7 245HX processor, an Nvidia GeForce RTX 5060 graphics card, and 16GB of RAM. For storage, there's a 512GB SSD.

Our alternative budget pick here is the Lenovo Legion 5i gaming laptop, which is discounted by $700 at Best Buy. You also get the same 16:10 aspect ratio and 2560 x 1600 resolution, but on a 165Hz OLED panel. If you care about getting the best contrast ratios and response time, this might be the pick for you, as saturating a 240Hz display with an RTX 5060 mobile variant will be a pretty tall task. However, this comes with the downside that you once again sacrifice the amount of storage available on the system here, with only 512GB available here.

Get an HP Omen Max laptop with an RTX 5080 GPU, Ryzen 9 HX 375, a 1TB SSD, and 32GB of RAM, all packed into a 16-inch chassis with a 2560 x 1600 240Hz display.

If you're looking to spend in the high-end, then this RTX 5080-based system with a full-fat wattage of up to 175W (under dynamic boost), paired with an AMD Ryzen AI 9 HX 375, which offers 12 cores and 24 threads, with a max boost clock of up to 5.1GHz should be able to chew up most workloads you throw its way. The 2560 x 1600, 240Hz display makes much more sense here, and with full VRR support and 32GB of DDR5 in tow, this is a bona fide powerhouse, and you'll pay for the privilege at almost $3,000. With that said, you get 1TB of storage here, which is slightly disappointing, but given how NAND pricing is going, it's par for the course right now.

The hardware included inside the Gigabyte Gaming A16 Pro includes Nvidia's RTX 5080 laptop graphics card with 16GB of VRAM, an Intel Core 7 240H processor, 32GB of RAM, and a 1TB SSD.

Coming in at just over $2,000, the Gigabyte Gaming A16 Pro deal at Walmart offers a 10-core, 16-thread Core Ultra 7 240H CPU and an RTX 5080 GPU with 16GB of GDDR7 VRAM. The difference between the Omen 16 Max and this Gigabyte model is that this particular RTX 5080 has a lower TGP of 115W, but maintains the same amount of VRAM. In real-world terms, your framerates will undoubtedly be slower, and saturating its 16-inch, 2560 x 1600 display will be a lot more difficult. However, that neutered power limit also puts it in contention with the RTX 5070 Ti-based systems we've listed, but with more VRAM. If that 4GB difference matters to you, and you are willing to pay the difference, then this deal offers a solid amount of silicon for your cash.

Get an HP Omen Max laptop with an RTX 5090 GPU, a Core Ultra 9 290HX Plus CPU, a 2TB SSD, and 32GB of RAM, all packed into a 16-inch chassis with a 2560 x 1600 240Hz OLED display.

If you're gunning for a gaming behemoth this Black Friday, this desktop-replacement-grade system is worth a look. Sporting an RTX 5090 GPU with 24GB of GDDR7 memory, paired with Intel's Core Ultra 290 HX Plus, with a total of 24 cores and 24 threads (8 P-Cores, 16 E-Cores), and a boost clock of up to 5.5GHz, this monstrous system is paired with 32GB of DDR5-6400 memory and 2TB of PCIe Gen 5 storage. You're also flush with connectivity options, including Wi-Fi 7 support, a pair of Thunderbolt 4 ports, and 2.5GB Ethernet. You also get a rapid 240Hz OLED display that gets plenty bright to boot. If you're spending north of $3,000 on a laptop, this should be it.

Get a great deal on this flagship-class ROG Strix Scar 16 gaming laptop from Amazon. You get the Core Ultra 9 275HX 8p/16e (24 threads) processor, 32GB dual-channel DDR5-5600 RAM, 2TB M.2 PCIe 4.0 x4 NVMe SSD, a 16-inch Mini-LED display (2560x1600) with a fast 240 Hz refresh rate, and the pièce de résistance: a dedicated RTX 5090 24GB laptop GPU. You'll be able to play most games at high frame rates or even run your own local AI models.

At MSRP pricing, the ROG Strix Scar is a better buy every other day. But given that it's black friday, the value offered by the HP Omen Max 16 slightly edges out this equally performant ROG Strix Scar 16 deal at Newegg. Expect the same RTX 5090 laptop GPU with 24GB of GDDR7 memory, but swap in an Intel Core Ultra 275HX with a total of 24 cores and 24 threads with 8 P-Cores and 16 E-Cores with boost clocks of up to 5.4GHz. Additionally, this system offers slower DDR5-5600 memory and slower storage, running at PCIe 4.0 speeds. However, you do get dual Thunderbolt 5 ports, here, and a matte finish on the display. It's still an incredible laptop by all rights, however HP's option edges things out on value, and spec-wise.

#10529. Nothing confirms ‘aggressive’ smartphone release schedule for 2027 with US focus

Alongside a teaser for the new Ear (note) recorder, Nothing CEO Carl Pei has confirmed the company’s plans for a big 2027 in terms of smartphone releases, as well as a focus on the US market.

Speaking to Bloomberg, Carl Pei says that Nothing currently has “tens of millions of devices in use,” and that there are big plans for 2027.

This includes “more attention” to the US market for Nothing, with the US being Nothing’s fastest-growing market for audio devices. In 2026, Nothing launched its Ear (3a) earbuds, multiple CMF-branded devices, and two pairs of over-ear headphones including its high-end Headphone (1) Pro in September, all of which are sold in the US (and on sale during Black Friday).

Pei further said that Nothing would have a more “aggressive” smartphone release schedule in 2027. Previously, it was said that Nothing would launch six new smartphones next year, following just three new smartphones in 2026.

Pei said Nothing now has “tens of millions of devices in use” across its smartphones, earbuds and other products. The company is also planning to devote more attention to the US market, while pursuing a more aggressive smartphone release schedule next year than it did in 2026.

The company said it has generated more than $2 billion in revenue since its first product release in 2022 and that the US is its fastest growing market for audio gear.

Nothing doesn’t have a strict schedule for new product launches, but Nothing Phone (4a) debuted in March 2026, with Phone (3) launching in the Summer of the year prior. With six new devices supposedly in the works, it’s anyone’s guess as to when they might release.

#10528. How to Test WCAG 2.2 in CI: What axe-core Misses

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Your accessibility check in CI is green. axe-core ran, zero violations, and the badge in the README looks great. Your site can still fail WCAG 2.2.

That isn't a bug in axe. WCAG 2.2 became a W3C Recommendation in October 2023. It added nine success criteria and dropped one (4.1.1 Parsing), and most of the new ones can't be judged by reading the DOM. Deque said as much when it shipped the first WCAG 2.2 support in axe-core 4.5: the target-size rule would probably be the only new-criterion rule they added, because the others can't be automated without a lot of false positives.

So the rest is on you. This article covers the one criterion axe can check and three it can't (focus not obscured, dragging movements, accessible authentication), with Playwright tests you can drop into a project. I ran all of them against a small test page that fails every criterion on purpose, and against a fixed version of the same page. If you want a plain-language rundown of every change first, WCAG 2.2 vs 2.1 goes through them. Here I only care about what you can put in a pipeline.

Level A and AA are what most contracts and laws point at, so those are the ones that matter first. The AAA criteria are still worth knowing about.

That's four AA criteria with something you can script, one of which axe already handles. The rest of this article goes through those four.

The AA rule is smaller than most people expect: a pointer target should be at least 24 by 24 CSS pixels, or sit far enough from its neighbours that a 24px circle centred on it wouldn't overlap another target or that target's own circle. There are exceptions, including inline links in a sentence, and targets whose size the browser controls.

axe-core has a target-size rule for this. One catch: it isn't part of a default run. In my setup (axe-core 4.14.1 through @axe-core/playwright 4.14.0), a plain analyze() on the broken page reported nothing at all. The rule only showed up once I asked for it:

On the broken page this lists the three 16px toolbar buttons by selector. On the fixed page, with 32px buttons, it passes. If you've been running axe for years and never seen a target-size result, this is why.

Some context on how common the problem is. In the scan data behind my last SitePoint article, 34.5% of the 1,025 sites had controls below a stricter 44px threshold. That is a tougher standard than the 24px that AA asks for, so don't read it as "a third of sites fail 2.5.8". Plenty of those would pass. But it does show how many production sites ship tiny controls.

If you'd like a quick look at one page before wiring anything up, browser tools such as axe DevTools, WAVE, or a WCAG checker will show the same thing.

axe can't test it, because you have to press Tab and look at where the browser scrolled. Two details caught me out building this:

The check below samples a grid of points inside the visible part of the focused element and asks the browser what's on top at each one. If none of the points land on the element or its children, it's covered.

On my broken page it reported nine hits: links hidden behind the footer going forward ("Section 4 link", "Section 10 link", "Section 16 link") and the toolbar buttons and a few links hidden behind the header coming back. The output names the element and the key, which is what you want when you're staring at a failed build.

The fix is one CSS rule, sized to your sticky elements:

That tells the browser to leave room when it scrolls a focused element into view. With it in place the same test passes.

Be aware of what this can't see: a modal that opens over the page, or a chat widget that only appears after a delay, will not show up unless your test opens or waits for them. Adrian Roselli's piece on 2.4.11 and adversarial conformance is worth reading for the awkward cases.

Anything you can do by dragging needs a way to do it with a single click or tap, unless dragging is essential. Sortable lists, sliders, kanban cards, file reorderers: all of them.

You can't prove a drag alternative works from a script. But you can catch the obvious mistake, which is a draggable item with no button anywhere near it:

The limit is real. It only recognises the native draggable attribute. Libraries built on pointer events, such as dnd-kit, don't set it, so this test will walk right past them. If you use one of those, replace the selector with whatever your component renders, or add a test per drag component. Treat it as a tripwire for the common case, not a verdict.

The fix, in the fixed page, is a pair of "Move up" and "Move down" buttons on each item, each with an accessible name that says which item it moves.

You can't make someone solve a cognitive test to log in (remember a password, transcribe a code) unless there's an alternative or a mechanism that helps. Password managers count as that mechanism, which means the two ways sites break it are blocking paste and omitting the right autocomplete values.

This dispatches a synthetic paste event and checks whether anything called preventDefault() on it. It catches the old onpaste="return false" trick, and it catches script-based blockers too. It does not tell you whether your login has a CAPTCHA that needs an alternative, or an emailed one-time code you must retype. Those need a person.

On a pull request, a plain GitHub Actions job is enough:

Point PAGE_URL at a running preview, and run the tests against the pages that matter most: sign-in, checkout, anything with a sticky element. Don't try to crawl everything on the first pass. A test that fails for reasons nobody owns gets deleted within a month.

Four things I haven't tried to automate, and wouldn't:

Automation will keep the regressions out. It won't tell you the work is finished. A green build and a conformant site are different things, and WCAG 2.2 widened the gap between them.

The tests and both test pages are small enough to read in one sitting. Copy what's useful, change the selectors, and keep the broken page around: it's a handy way to check that a test fails when it should.

About the author: [name] builds WCAGsafe, an accessibility scanner. The study data above comes from its scans; the code in this article is independent of it and runs against any page.

#10526. Nvidia proves it’s too big to fail

Good morning. Today I’m deciding whether the final Avengers: Doomsday trailer passes the “people just standing around” test, trying the new Material 3 Expressive Claude app on my Android devices, and stocking up on discounted XTEINK e-readers for Christmas gifts.

Inbox is written by Daniel Bader. Read previous issues here, and then catch up with Ben Schoon’s excellent Weekender column published every Sunday.

Eleven days after leaving Nvidia, Jensen Huang is back as president, CEO, and a board director. The board voted unanimously to reinstate him on Monday, saying it was satisfied with his candor and ability to lead following a series of meetings. It also said input from senior levels of the US government left it confident that his reinstatement poses no national security risk.

I didn’t see this coming. Huang was dishonest to the public and to his board. He admitted to having affairs not only with an alleged Chinese spy, but also with an employee who directly reported to him. He is accused of facilitating the sale of Nvidia AI chips to companies like Megaspeed that knowingly purchased them with the intention of smuggling them into China, a contravention of US trade restrictions.

As recently as November 17th, the board chair, Harvey Jones, reiterated that the company had no intention of bringing back Huang as CEO. But things changed quickly on Sunday as President Trump reportedly reassured the board that Huang’s reinstatement posed no security risk to US intelligence, something that the new Democratic-led Congress will be sure to test via repeated subpoenas in the new year.

As Huang walked into the atrium at Nvidia’s headquarters, staff gathered around to cheer his return, and he addressed the crowd by saying he would be a “better CEO the second time around,” as if 11 days of unemployment had given him sufficient time to evaluate his past behavior.

Nvidia’s stock was up over 30% on Monday, rebounding from a disastrous end to last week that wiped out $4 trillion in Nasdaq value. It’s hard not to see Huang’s return as a direct response to this short-term disruption, which also hinted at a much more serious correction in the stock market. Nvidia’s stock had already fallen 24% between the day Huang resigned and Friday’s crunch, and with looming regulation from Congress in 2027, a rudderless AI industry could have been disastrous for the US economy writ large.

Though things may appear to be back to normal, or as normal as possible given the insanity of the past couple of weeks, Huang has returned to a very different trade environment. While he was away, Nvidia’s board formed a National Security and Export Control Committee to investigate whether the company’s actions violated any US embargoes. And as part of Huang’s return, Nvidia has committed to hiring its first chief compliance officer, an executive who will ostensibly not report directly to the CEO.

I was pretty gobsmacked when I saw the news of Huang’s return come across the wire at 9am ET yesterday. I’m still wrapping my head around it. Is Nvidia too big to fail, or just a symptom of a larger interdependency between the US executive branch and Big Tech? Huang promises to be a better leader this time around, but to whom: his employees, his customers, or his shareholders? Because he appears to be a very different person to each of those groups.

Better-tasting water for your morning coffee, everyday cooking, or a house full of holiday guests? Waterdrop Filter’s Black Friday sale has options for all three, from high-capacity under-sink reverse osmosis systems to installation-free countertop models. Upgrade your kitchen with the X12 under-sink system for $899, save space with the compact G6A6 for $189.99, or skip the plumbing with the M6HG countertop system for $299, which delivers filtered hot water for coffee and tea in seconds.

Whichever setup fits your routine, use code 9TO5GBF26 to take an extra 5% off those sale prices. Waterdrop’s offers also extend to whole-home filtration and replacement refrigerator filters, giving you plenty of ways to upgrade without stretching your holiday budget. Shop Waterdrop’s Black Friday deals on Amazon before the sale ends November 30th.

Since it’s Black Friday week, I figured I’d round up some of the best deals we’ve found so far.

A discounted Pixel: The Pixel 10 at $600 is a really good deal. Recent software updates have closed some of the feature gaps with the Pixel 11, and the hardware is still solid even a year after launch. If you want better cameras, the Pixel 11 Pro at $749 is an absolute steal.

The gaming-tablet deal: The Legion Tab Gen 5 at $599 makes much more sense than it did at $899: an 8.8-inch 165Hz display, a 12GB/256GB configuration, and an included folio. It’s a good fit for someone specifically seeking a compact Android gaming machine.

The last-generation watch: The 40mm Galaxy Watch 8 at $199 is the sort of older smartwatch worth considering when annual upgrades are modest. And because the Watch 9 is basically identical, I’d go for the Watch 8. If you are looking at Samsung’s latest and greatest, the Galaxy Watch 9 is $379 with a free $50 Amazon gift card, which is a decent deal, too.

A deal on the year’s best Android foldable: The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is showing up at $1,499.99 for some lucky shoppers and $1,549.99 for the rest. Either way, it’s Samsung’s best product of 2026, and my current favorite gadget.

Google’s latest mid-sized sedan AI model is taking the world by storm. Gemini 4 Neon is technically the middle sibling in Google’s lineup, with Carbon at the top and Hydrogen at the bottom, but everyone seems to agree it absolutely cooks.

If you’ve spent any time on X over the past few days (I hate myself for writing that), you’ll likely have seen an uptick in fervor over Google’s latest model, which seems to outperform Claude Opus 5.5, and even Gemini 4 Carbon itself, in many benchmarks and real-world tests. Even Google is providing some helpful guidance about when to use one or the other.

One thing I’ve read again and again about Neon is how adept it is at writing. I’m of two minds about this: the better an AI gets at doing my job, the less necessary I am at said job. On the other hand, I’m so tired of obvious AI slop that a model that actually writes well sounds like a relief. At least when I’m unemployed, I won’t be yelling at the screen all day about why the robot yet again used negative parallelism.

I didn’t get to play much GTA VI over the weekend, but I know a few (million) people who did. Publisher Take-Two says the game generated $3.79 billion in first-day retail sales. That’s an incredible number in any industry, but a truly mind-blowing one in the video game world. Rockstar also released its first patch for the game on Monday to fix a modest number of bugs with the launch code.

If you follow open-weight AI models, you’ll likely have heard of how influential Z.ai’s GLM-5.2 was when it launched in June. Now the company has released GLM-5.4, and it’s novel as much for its architecture as it is for its benchmarks.

Its Loop Transformer reuses a block of layers to do more computation without adding parameters. The company is pitching applications beyond software, including games, visual work, and CAD. The 750-million-parameter model reportedly performs at the level of a 2-3 trillion parameter non-looping equivalent, but we’re still waiting on a broader release for real-world testing.

In browser news, Ladybird’s first public alpha is now downloadable for macOS and Linux. It’s built with a brand-new engine that eschews many of the legacy decisions made by Google’s Chromium team. And the project has the backing of big names like Shopify, Cloudflare, and Proton. It’s still pretty rough, and features are paltry, but any new competition in the browser space is a win in my book.

#10524. The Moto G Stylus (2025) at $200 is great only if a pen is a must

Okay, so here’s the deal (literally): last year’s Moto G Stylus (2025) is on sale right now on Amazon for just $199, and if you desperately need a digital writing tool with your phone, it’s a good buy.

That is the short answer; the long answer is a little more complicated, as due to some issues with the 2026 model, you are missing out on exactly…nothing. Yes, at under $200, you are getting essentially the same phone because Motorola decided that, due to RAM and storage shortages and price hikes, the newer model would forgo any major upgrades. The 2026 model starts at $499, so if you had your eye on it, yeah, it’s a silly way to waste $300 in our opinion.

It has the same Snapdragon 6 Gen 3 processor, 8GB RAM, and 128GB storage. Other notable features include a 5,000mAh battery, 68W wired charging, 15W wireless charging, a 50MP camera setup, and, as of writing, it should be due for an Android 16 upgrade.

The star of the show of the Moto G Stylus (2025) is the stylus. It boils down to this: if you want a phone that isn’t a Galaxy Ultra, then this is the only other line that makes sense for document signers.

However, there are better deals out there. I think the Galaxy A56, at just $299, is an incredible offer right now and way more deserving of your hard-earned cash. Even so, if you want clean software or that stylus, the Moto G Stylus (2025) at $199 is at least an option especially for a family member or a backup to your existing phone.

This MMO / MOBA-focused mouse features 16 programmable buttons, 12 of which are on its customizable, movable side panel. It also sports Corsair's Marksman S 33K optical sensor, which has a maximum sensitivity of 33,000 DPI and a maximum speed of 750 IPS, and can handle up to 50G's of force.

The Scimitar Elite SE Wireless features 16 programmable buttons, 12 of which are on its movable side panel — you can move the side panel forward or backward, depending on your hand size and what's most comfortable for you. It sports Corsair's Marksman S 33K optical sensor, which has a maximum sensitivity of 33,000 DPI and a maximum speed of 750 IPS, and can handle up to 50G's of force. It weighs 4.02 ounces (114g), but let's face it — if you're looking for a mouse with 16 programmable buttons, weight is not your top priority. The Scimitar Elite SE Wireless also features a pretty solid battery life of 150 hours over its 2.4GHz wireless connection, which means you'll only have to charge it every couple of weeks (and that's if you're a serious gamer).

Read our full review of the Scimitar Elite SE Wireless here.

If you're looking for something modular and potentially customizable, HyperX's Pulsefire Saga Pro is a wireless gaming mouse with a couple of modular options in the box, plus the ability to print your own pieces. This lightweight gaming mouse also features five programmable buttons and up to a 4,000 Hz wireless polling rate, and it's currently on sale at Best Buy for $79.99 — $40 off its MSRP of $119.99.

This ultra-lightweight wireless mouse is modular and comes with 2 humps, 2 sets of primary buttons, and 2 sets of side buttons. It features up to a 4,000 Hz wireless polling rate and HyperX's 26K sensor.

The Pulsefire Saga Pro sports HyperX's 26K sensor, which has a maximum sensitivity of 26,000 DPI and a maximum speed of 650 IPS, and can handle up to 50G's of force. This is the same sensor we've seen in HyperX's mice for a couple of years now — it's a solid sensor in terms of speed and accuracy, but it might not be for you if you're looking for the latest and greatest.

However, if you're just looking for lightweight modularity, the Pulsefire Saga Pro is where it's at: this mouse comes with two mouse humps, two sets of primary mouse buttons, and two sets of side buttons, and it implements its modularity well — everything fits together nicely and feels sturdy, and we had no issues with creaking or parts moving around in our testing. All configurations will give you a weight of 2.54 ounces (72g).
