#10518. Hostinger VPS in 2026: Hands-On With the AI-Managed VPS

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, renting a VPS meant signing up to be a part-time sysadmin. You’d SSH into a blank Ubuntu prompt and lose a Saturday to Nginx configs and firewall rules before writing a single line of your actual project.

In 2026, three things have changed. What we build has changed: AI coding tools turn weekend ideas into working apps, and AI agents need a machine that stays on after your laptop closes. Who runs servers has changed: you can now ask an AI agent in your terminal to set up a reverse proxy, close a port or work out why something broke. And what servers cost has changed: the AI boom is soaking up the world’s memory supply, and hosting prices are rising with it.

So I bought the smallest Hostinger VPS plan, deployed a Node.js app in Docker, and handed the DevOps work to Hostinger Agent, the AI assistant built into the dashboard.

Below is what that felt like step by step and whether it’s worth locking in a price before they climb further.

Most VPS hosting for developers now falls into four buckets. They all run on the same kind of machine; what changes is how much RAM you give them.

Apps built with AI coding tools. Claude Code and Cursor get you to a working Node.js or Python app fast, then leave you wondering where to put it. A VPS gives you one fixed monthly bill instead of usage-based pricing. If you want push-to-deploy, Hostinger offers Ubuntu templates with Coolify or Dokploy preinstalled.

AI agents and automations that run 24/7. For most people, a “VPS for AI” means a CPU box that never sleeps, not a GPU. Think n8n workflows, a scheduled scraper, or your own agent in a Docker container. Hostinger even has an “Ubuntu 24.04 with Claude Code” template: SSH in, run claude /login, and the agent keeps working after you close your laptop.

APIs, backends and databases for side projects. A Postgres instance, a Redis cache and a small API behind a reverse proxy fit comfortably on a mid-sized plan. You pick the versions, the extensions and the backup schedule.

Client sites and stores. Freelancers outgrow shared hosting when one client needs a custom PHP extension or a WooCommerce store starts to crawl. A VPS with cPanel or Plesk, or a WordPress or WooCommerce template, keeps client work isolated and under your control.

If you’d rather not build anything from scratch, the Docker Manager catalog listed 1,017 one-click templates when I checked, including 93 under AI/ML and 162 under Developer tools.

For this Hostinger VPS review, the question I cared about was simple: how long from “Choose plan” to an app on the internet? On KVM 1, the entry plan, it was about 12 minutes. Here’s each step with rough timings.

I went with KVM 1: 1 vCPU, 4 GB RAM, 50 GB NVMe storage and 4 TB of bandwidth. On the Black Friday deal it costs $6.49/mo, paid upfront as $155.76 for 24 months, and renews at $11.99/mo.

You choose the server’s location and what to install during checkout, alongside your billing details. The location picker shows estimated latency for each data centre; mine showed about 80 ms.

Install options come in three tabs: Plain OS, Control panel and Applications. I picked plain Ubuntu 26.04 LTS to see how much work the AI would take off my hands. A daily backup add-on is offered, but weekly backups are already included.

The five-minute estimate was about right. The “you’re ready” screen then gives you two ways in: the VPS dashboard, or a pre-filled ssh root@ command. It also offers a free domain for a year to use as the server’s hostname.

The overview shows the OS, plan, CPU, RAM, disk and bandwidth at a glance, with Docker Manager, Backups & Monitoring, Security and DNS Manager in the side menu.

Every server also gets a hostname under hstgr.cloud (mine was srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud), which came in handy later for SSL. I’ve masked my hostname as srvXXXXXXX throughout and blurred IP addresses in the screenshots.

Plain Ubuntu doesn’t ship with Docker. Open Docker Manager in hPanel and it installs Docker for you with one click. From there you can deploy apps from a Compose file, a repository URL or the one-click catalog.

Click Web console and you’re logged in as root in a browser tab, with Hostinger Agent in a panel beside it. You don’t need an SSH client or keys. The login banner flagged 33 pending updates, 23 of them security fixes; more on that later. A quick docker --version confirmed Docker 29.9.3 was ready.

Here’s the whole app: a tiny HTTP server with a health check, and a Dockerfile on Node.js 24, a supported LTS line.

The build took 8.3 seconds, including pulling node:24-alpine, and the health check answered straight away. The --restart unless-stopped flag brings the container back after a crash or reboot, which I tested later.

That’s a working app, but on plain HTTP, on a high port, with no TLS. Getting it production-ready is the DevOps part I handed to the AI.

An AI-managed VPS isn’t the same as a traditional managed VPS. You still get a self-managed server with full root access, but Hostinger Agent does the work you’d normally do yourself. Agent (previously called Kodee) is an MCP-powered assistant that lives in the dashboard and beside the AI web terminal, and it’s included at no extra cost. You can also connect Claude or Cursor to your server through Hostinger’s MCP server.

My prompt: “Check that Docker is installed, then set up a reverse proxy with a free SSL certificate so my demo-app container (port 3000) is served at https://srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud.”

It started by looking around: the Docker and Compose versions, which ports were in use, whether a web server was already running, and the OS release. Then it installed Caddy from Caddy’s official repository, configured the proxy and made sure ufw allowed web traffic.

It read Caddy’s logs to confirm the Let’s Encrypt certificate, which arrived in about 6 seconds. Finally it tested its own work: HTTPS returned 200, and plain HTTP returned a 308 redirect. The whole task took about two minutes.

To give it a real fault, I ran docker stop demo-app and reloaded the site. Then I asked why it was returning a 502 and to fix it.

In a single command, it checked the container state (Exited (137)) and searched Caddy’s logs, where it found connect: connection refused on port 3000. It restarted the container, confirmed /health and HTTPS were back, and even pointed to my docker stop in the terminal history. It also explained that --restart unless-stopped covers crashes and reboots, but not a manual stop.

A tip it didn’t mention: exit code 137 means Docker had to force-kill the container, because Node running as PID 1 ignores SIGTERM. Add --init to docker run for clean, instant shutdowns.

This was the most impressive answer. ufw already allowed only those three ports, but Agent noticed port 3000 was still reachable from the internet, because Docker-published ports bypass ufw rules.

It recreated the container bound to 127.0.0.1:3000, so the app is only reachable through Caddy, and confirmed with an external port check. It left DNS, time sync and Caddy’s local admin port running, explaining that stopping them would break DNS and time sync.

Then I asked it to reboot the server. First it checked that Docker and Caddy start on boot and that the container’s restart policy was set, then it rebooted. The site came back over HTTPS without any help from me.

Nothing I asked for was destructive, so Agent just got on with it: the firewall change, the container rebuild and the reboot all ran without a confirmation prompt.

The Agent panel says so upfront, noting that terminal commands can run without one. For destructive actions such as reinstalling the OS or restoring a backup, Hostinger’s FAQ says Agent always asks for confirmation first.

One thing worth knowing: to close port 3000, Agent removed and recreated my container. That was harmless for a stateless demo, but a container that keeps data without a volume would lose it. Take a snapshot before big jobs, and keep the panel in view.

Here are the four plans at their Black Friday rates for a 24-month term.

For scale, here’s what my test used. With the Node app, Docker and Caddy all running, KVM 1 had 3.3 GB of its 4 GB RAM still available, and the app container itself used about 65 MB.

So 4 GB is plenty for one or two small apps. Move to 8 GB on KVM 2 once you add Coolify, a database, or n8n with its own Postgres, because those are the services that eat memory.

Finding a cheap VPS got harder in 2026, because memory got expensive. AI data centres are buying up DRAM, and hosts are passing the cost on. In April 2026, Hetzner raised cloud server prices by up to 37%, citing hardware costs, then raised prices again in June. On the same April date, OVHcloud’s entry VPS went from $4.90 to $7.60, a 55% jump. Nobody can promise where VPS prices go next, but the trend this year has been up.

This is how Hostinger’s Black Friday pricing works. You pay upfront for 12 or 24 months, and the 24-month term gives the lowest monthly rate. Your price is fixed for that whole term. After that, it renews at the standard rate.

On KVM 2, for example, you pay $210.96 today (the $8.79/mo rate) for 24 months, and it renews at $14.99/mo after that. Do the maths on the renewal before you commit, not just the headline rate.

Black Friday VPS deal: up to 70% off VPS hosting

Get up to 70% off Hostinger VPS plans this Black Friday, with prices from $6.49/mo on a 24-month term. Every plan includes Hostinger Agent, Docker Manager and free weekly backups. Your price is fixed for the term, then renews at the standard rate. The deal ends December 14, 2026.

#10516. New OpenAI and Anthropic models sound alarms on safety and control, report

Frontier AI models that have yet to be released by OpenAI and Anthropic are reportedly reaching the point where employees believe they cannot be safely released or controlled, employees reportedly say.

Axios cites “more than two dozen” employees inside of OpenAI and Anthropic who are sounding warnings around unreleased models from both companies.

While both OpenAI’s GPT and Anthropic’s Claude public models are increasingly powerful, these employees say that new models behind the scenes are starting to beat out the technical solutions for controlling them. This has led to debates between those who think they’ll be able to fix these problems, and others who believe companies should stop training new models altogether due to the risks.

If we continue trying to make models much smarter, such that they’re actually smarter than us … they will in fact be able to trick us successfully. And once you get to that point, many of the schemes that we’ve had at work up until now sort of go out the window.

The report goes out to point out that some researchers feel that executives at major AI companies have become “out of touch” with the severity of safety risks, though Axios was told that OpenAI is adopting new safety practices while Anthropic said that “Keeping a model internal doesn’t make it safe,” adding that “we may slow down or hold back even when it costs us.”

#10510. Is Kling 4.0 the Right AI Video Generator for Your Project?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A short clip can help a project in different ways. It can show an idea you have not filmed, put an existing design in motion, or give an announcement something to look at. The same approach will not work if the clip needs to demonstrate a real feature or show exactly what happened. Before choosing a generator, decide what the viewer needs to learn from the shot.

This article uses Kling 4.0 as a worked example, but the decision process applies to any AI video generator. It covers how these tools turn prompts and images into video, how Kling 4.0 and a few alternatives differ, how to choose a starting mode, how to add exact text and branding outside the generated clip with a short ffmpeg script, and when real footage is the better choice. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

● Generated video is best for imagined shots. It suits concepts, backgrounds and early explorations, not instructions or faithful records.

● Your starting material decides the mode. A written idea, a reference image or a pair of opening and closing frames each call for a different input.

● Keep exact wording out of the generated clip. Titles, dates and logos belong on separate layers in an editor.

● Tools differ on control, cost and licensing. Compare inputs, length, resolution, credits and terms before you commit.

● Always review. Watch the whole clip at its final size and check it against the real product or design.

Most current systems extend diffusion models across time. A model learns to turn noise into a coherent set of frames guided by a prompt, and sometimes by an image or a clip. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequences are easy to observe.

● The prompt describes change over time. One visible change and one camera instruction usually work better than a long list of actions.

● Reference material steers but does not lock. A picture can suggest shape and colour without guaranteeing an exact match.

● Longer clips give more room for drift. Faces, logos and fine text are the first things to change between frames.

● Cost grows with length and resolution. Most hosted tools charge credits per second, so short previews are cheaper than full shots.

If you only have an idea, start with text. For a short announcement, you might ask for a band of light to move across a wall of coloured tiles. No one needs to recognise a particular wall, so the model can decide how it looks. Give it one visible change and a clear view of the subject. A prompt such as Soft morning light travels slowly across a wall of blue ceramic tiles. Fixed camera, no lettering is enough to see whether the idea works.

If you already have an illustration or a photo, reference mode gives you another starting point. Suppose you want a short concept clip for a desk accessory that only exists as a drawing. The picture can show the general shape and colour while the prompt describes the view you want. A video reference can help when it is the motion that is hard to explain, such as a slow camera slide. Say what each asset is meant to guide, and do not add a clip just because you have one.

Use keyframes when a particular view matters at a particular moment. Start with an opening image. If a short reveal also needs to end with the object facing forward, add an ending image to show that view. That is different from a reference image that mainly shows what the object looks like. Neither method guarantees an exact match, so check the result rather than assuming the uploaded picture has locked the design.

No single tool fits every project. The table summarizes what each option’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

Reading the table: hosted studios such as the one on JXP are the quickest way to test an idea and need no setup, but you pay per second and work within their supported inputs. Kling’s own site is the place to check announcements and API options directly, since its page says little about durations or resolution. Runway positions its models for motion quality and prompt following, while Google’s Gemini API video documentation suits teams building generation into their own products. The Diffusers video guide covers open-source pipelines for developers who want local control and are ready to manage GPUs and licences.

Trying an idea before making it. An illustrator might want to see how an imagined landscape feels when the light changes. A quick clip gives the team something to discuss: is the change visible, and does it happen at the right pace? It does not need to be the final animation to answer those questions.

Showing a product concept. A rotating view or a slow camera move can help people discuss how a design might be presented. It is useful early, when the shape and lighting are still being explored. Once the clip represents a product someone can buy, the standard changes. If a port moves or a button appears that the real product does not have, it is the wrong clip for that job.

Making a short visual for a post. A few seconds of moving colour can give an event announcement or a project update a distinctive opening. Put the name, date and other exact wording in your editor so they are readable and easy to change. The background only needs to support the message. If it competes with the text, use less movement or keep the background still.

Generated video is unreliable at lettering, so add titles in an editor or with a script. The command below overlays a title on a generated background and then checks the file properties. It assumes you already have a clip named background.mp4 and ffmpeg installed.

-vf "drawtext=text='Launch Night | 12 March':fontcolor=white:fontsize=64:x=(w-text_w)/2:y=(h-text_h)/2:box=1:boxcolor=black@0.35:boxborderw=24" \

-c:v libx264 -pix_fmt yuv420p -an announcement.mp4

-show_entries stream=width,height,r_frame_rate,duration \

-of default=noprint_wrappers=1 announcement.mp4

Because the text lives in the command, you can reuse the same background for three events by changing one string. The font may need to be set with a fontfile option on some systems. This is the practical version of keeping the moving background in one file and the editable details in another.

If people will follow the clip as instructions, use real footage or a screen recording for the steps. A convincing shot of an app is not proof that a button is in the right place. A generated close-up of a product is not a reliable way to show how its parts fit. You can still use an imagined opening, but make the switch to the real demonstration clear.

Think about revisions, too. If your team expects to adjust the same title, logo or date every week, put those elements on separate layers in an editor. And if the only movement you need is a slow zoom on an approved picture, apply that zoom directly. There is no reason to risk changing the picture when a simple edit will do.

This matters when someone else will maintain the video. A reusable announcement template is easier to hand over if the moving background is one file and the event details are editable. If the team likes the movement but needs three versions with different dates, they can reuse the same clip. If every change calls for a new generation, the time you saved on the first version may disappear on the second.

The Kling 4.0 video generator on JXP currently offers 720p and 1080p settings and clip lengths from 3 to 30 seconds. According to its page, reference mode accepts up to 10 images and 5 videos, keyframe mode takes a first frame, middle frames and an optional last frame, and credits are charged per second with a higher rate at 1080p. For a first attempt, choose the frame shape you plan to use and make the clip only as long as it needs to be. Check the displayed credit cost before generating, because a short preview can tell you whether the direction works before you spend on a longer shot.

Decide what a good result looks like before you generate it. For the tile background, you need to see the light move without distracting from the words you will add. For a product concept, check whether the shape stays recognisable as the viewpoint changes. Watch the whole clip at the size the audience will see it. If the shot misses the point, revise one thing at a time: the motion, the framing or the source image.

● Read the licence. JXP’s page lists a commercial use license on each plan and says outputs are for creative and lawful use, with the user responsible for reviewing them. Other tools have different terms.

● Use inputs you have the right to use. Do not upload other people’s copyrighted work or photos of people without permission.

● Do not misrepresent products or events. A generated clip should not suggest features or footage that do not exist.

● Consider provenance. C2PA is an open standard for Content Credentials that records where content came from and how it was edited, and some tools support it.

● Disclose where required. Follow platform and advertising rules on labelling AI-generated or altered content.

● Fine text, logos, hands and fast motion can drift or flicker between frames.

● Reference images and keyframes steer the result but do not guarantee an exact match.

● Vendor specifications, such as resolution and length, can differ between a model announcement and a particular platform.

● Credit costs and plan terms change, so check them on the day you generate.

● Long or exact sequences are often cheaper and safer to film than to generate.

Short imagined shots, concept clips and moving backgrounds, where some details can be left open and the clip is reviewed before use.

Use text when you only have an idea, reference mode when you have a picture or clip to guide the look, and keyframes when specific views matter at specific moments.

Not reliably. Use real footage or a screen recording for instructions and demonstrations.

No. Hosted studios, vendor sites, developer APIs and open-source pipelines all exist. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your project.

Kling 4.0 is a good candidate when the missing piece is an imagined shot and you can describe what it needs to do. Pick one such shot, try it, and put it next to the material you already have, and compare at least one alternative before you commit. If the project needs exact instructions or a faithful record, use the real thing. A useful first result is one that does its job in the edit, not simply one that looks impressive on its own.

Size: 27 inches seems to be the typical entry-level size whereas 24 inches is small and 32 inches or higher should be considered large.

Refresh Rate: If you're a gamer, you'll prefer a high-refresh rate display that's at least 120 Hz and supports AMD FreeSync and/or Nvidia G-Sync technology. Unless you're an esports professional, you probably don't need more than 165 Hz.

Resolution: 4K is the top resolution most people can afford, but it can be expensive if you want both high resolution and high refresh rates. You can usually get a good price on 2K, high refresh monitors, and even better savings on 1080p screens.

#10501. China’s AI hardware push is moving beyond GPUs to the PCBs underneath them — Beijing races to corner the market on copper foil and micro-drill bits as board complexity explodes

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Much of the attention on China's AI hardware industry has been focused on the country’s drive to develop domestic GPUs, including Huawei's Ascend processors, as U.S. export restrictions make it harder to buy advanced chips in the region. But making an AI server involves far more than securing its processors. The printed circuit boards (PCBs) connecting chips, memory, and networking equipment are becoming harder to manufacture, and Chinese companies are racing to supply the materials and equipment they require.

More than 20 Chinese PCB manufacturers announced major expansion plans in the first half of 2026. Three recent reports from HSBC and J.P. Morgan suggest the shortages could extend to products as unglamorous as drill bits and copper foil. Having a plentiful and regular supply of those components in the manufacturing process is important because the tiny drill bit used to make the circuit boards inside AI servers could be worn out after drilling fewer than 200 holes, according to HSBC, compared with around 2,000 holes for older materials.

It’s all part of a manufacturing process which stresses every part of the supply and manufacturing chain, beyond obvious components.

PCBs are built by layering copper and insulating material together, then drilling holes through them to connect their circuits. But the complexity of those boards is increasing as AI servers are designed to handle more data, including more layers than those seen in ordinary computers. HSBC estimates that a notebook PCB has four to eight layers, compared with more than 20 for Nvidia's H100 generation of GPUs.

But that’s just the beginning of the process. Analysts at the bank forecast that a backplane in Nvidia’s Rubin Ultra systems could contain 78 layers. Nor is the Rubin Ultra an outlier in that process: the bank also forecasts a 36% annual rise in the number of holes required per board between 2025 and 2028. The holes themselves are becoming more challenging to drill. According to a report by Taiwanese brokerage KGI Securities, the ratio between the depth of the hole and its diameter has increased from around 20:1 to 30:1 or 40:1. That requires pushing an extremely thin drill bit deeper into increasingly tough materials without bending or breaking it, or damaging the delicate circuitry, which can easily happen in such a sophisticated manufacturing process.

As well as the number of layers which have to be drilled through increasing in number, the materials involved are also adding complexity to the equation. New grades of copper-clad laminate — the glass-fibre-and-resin material onto which copper is bonded — are becoming trickier for the tiny tungsten-carbide tools previously used in the process to cut. “AI PCBs are denser with thicker layers, requiring more holes drilled per PCB,” writes Helen Fang, head of Asia Pacific industrials research at HSBC. The increasingly common use of harder laminates is resulting in the bits being consumed more quickly.

That’s good news for those making drill bits: HSBC forecasts global demand growing 55% a year in the coming three years. And as with many parts of the AI revolution, the voracious appetite of the sector means that supplies can’t keep up. Even with manufacturers increasing production, the bank forecasts a 24% supply shortfall in PCB drill bits in 2027.

That could push the world towards Chinese suppliers, right as the west is trying to wean itself off a dependency on the country for rare earths and other elements necessary to make GPUs. Guangdong Dtech, based in China, is the world’s largest PCB drill bit maker by volume. Around three in every 10 drill bits made in 2025 come from the company, with its monthly capacity potentially climbing from 160 million bits in June to more than 235 million by year-end. Japanese and Taiwanese manufacturers are still dominant at the premium end of the market, according to consultancy Frost & Sullivan, though Chinese manufacturers are trying to close that gap.

In its 2026 interim results, Dtech reported that it had begun mass-producing drill bits with aspect ratios exceeding 40:1, while developing techniques for producing bits exceeding 60:1. Its newer diamond-coated tools for particularly tough M9+ board materials have also entered small-scale use.

It’s not just in drill bits that China has a chokehold. Another Chinese manufacturer, Defu Technology, has historically been a key supplier of copper foil for lithium-ion batteries, but is increasingly changing its manufacturing processes to try and capitalise on the AI boom. “AI server PCB/CCL upgrades require smoother HVLP copper foil to reduce high-speed signal loss,” writes Avery Chan, a JP Morgan analyst.

HVLP stands for hyper-very-low-profile copper foil, and its manufacturers must make the surface smooth enough to minimise signal loss while making sure it can still stick to the insulating material it is attached to.

The business has historically been dominated by suppliers outside mainland China, with Japan’s Mitsui Kinzoku and Furukawa Electric, and Taiwan’s Co-Tech, holding more than 60% of global HVLP supply in 2025. Defu is trying to break in to disrupt that, with the firm shipping around 700 tonnes of HVLP foil in September, including more than 180 tonnes of HVLP4. JP Morgan’s Chan forecasts a shortage of qualified HVLP4 supply of around 700 tonnes a month in 2026, rising to 1,200 tonnes in 2027. Defu plans to add 50,000 tonnes of annual high-end PCB foil capacity over 2027 and 2028. But all the foil it creates will have to get past stringent testing.

Even before finished chips reach a server, they must be tested using specialist probe cards and sockets, which themselves rely on sophisticated PCBs of a type not found in a server but just used to check whether the chips work. “PCB is becoming the bottleneck and the value-add in probe cards,” wrote JP Morgan analyst Jerry Tsai.

Because of that, China is focusing attention there, too. In September, its Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and National Development and Reform Commission published a five-year plan calling for advances in high-frequency, high-speed and densely layered PCBs, alongside copper-clad laminates, low-profile copper foil and specialist manufacturing equipment.

But China won’t see the benefits of building new factories immediately. Producing the most sophisticated PCBs requires experience, specialist equipment and the ability to manufacture complex designs consistently at scale. Tsai warned that “the learning curve is measured in years, rather than quarters.”

Chris Stokel-Walker is a Tom's Hardware contributor who focuses on the tech sector and its impact on our daily lives— online and offline. He is the author of How AI Ate the World, published in 2024, as well as TikTok Boom, YouTubers, and The History of the Internet in Byte-Sized Chunks.

#10498. How Consumer AI Companions Handle Context Windows, Persistent Memory, and Voice Latency

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI companions are the most demanding consumer use of large language models right now. A coding assistant can forget your last message and still be useful. A companion that forgets your name, your running story, or the plan you made last Tuesday has failed at its one job. On top of that, users now expect voice calls that feel like a phone conversation, not a walkie-talkie exchange with a five-second pause.

Three engineering problems decide whether a companion app works: how much of the conversation fits in the model's context window, what the app remembers once the window fills up, and how fast the voice pipeline answers. Here is how consumer companions handle each one, with real tier and pricing examples, and what developers can take from their designs for their own products.

Most LLM products are judged on single answers. Companions are judged on continuity. That creates pressure in three places at once:

Every consumer companion is a different set of trade-offs across those three constraints, and the trade-offs are increasingly how the apps are priced.

The context window is the total number of tokens the model can see when it writes the next reply. In a companion app, that budget is spent before the user says anything:

When the chat log outgrows what is left, the oldest messages fall out. The user experiences this as the companion "forgetting" mid-conversation. This is why context size is now a headline spec in the category, and a paid feature. SpicyChat AI, for example, gives free accounts a 4K context window, then scales memory to 8K on its $19.95/mo tier and 32K on its $29.95/mo tier. Janitor AI runs about 9K of context on its own JanitorLLM. Independent uncensored AI chat benchmarks record these context sizes alongside refusal behavior and pricing, because context length predicts how long a roleplay stays coherent better than any marketing claim.

Some apps sell memory in messages rather than tokens. Fantasy GF's pricing page lists a 60-message base "smart memory" and 5x that, 300 messages, on its Ultra plan. Others sell it as a memory multiplier on higher subscription tiers. The pattern is consistent across the category: memory depth is the upgrade lever, not the model itself.

No consumer app keeps an entire relationship in the prompt. Persistent memory is a separate system that writes to storage and reads back into the context window on each turn. In practice it has three layers:

The differences users notice are usually retention policies, not algorithms. Crushon AI's own subscription docs say free-plan chat history is deleted after 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans keep it for the membership period. Paid tiers across the category commonly promise that memories never expire. There is also a security angle worth stating plainly: companion chat logs are among the most sensitive data a consumer product can hold. A 2024 breach at Muah.ai, first reported by 404 Media, exposed roughly 1.9 million users' email addresses together with their private prompts. Any app that builds persistent memory is also building a liability, and developers copying the pattern should treat the memory store as the most protected part of the system.

A voice call in a companion app is three models chained together: speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech. Run them strictly in sequence and a single reply can take several seconds, which reads as dead air. The apps that feel fast attack each stage the same way:

With every stage streaming, time-to-first-audio can land close to a second on a good connection, which is roughly the threshold where a conversation stops feeling laggy. Voice is also expensive to run - three model calls instead of one, plus audio bandwidth - which is why it is metered harder than text. Fantasy GF charges 8 tokens per minute for voice calls (4 on Ultra), which turns the 100 monthly tokens on its entry plan into about 12 minutes of call time. SpicyChat, for its part, only adds text-to-speech at its top tier. Latency engineering and unit economics are the same problem here: faster pipelines cost less per call, and cheaper calls are what make voice viable below the top plan.

Tier details below come from each app's own pricing or documentation pages, checked November 16, 2026, and cross-checked against il.ly's AI companion testing guide, which hands-tests 15 companion apps across memory, media, and voice.

Two takeaways from the table. First, nobody sells a bigger base model - they sell a bigger window into the same one. Second, the free tiers are deliberately memory-poor: limited context and short retention are the upgrade prompt.

Even if you never build a companion, these apps are a useful reference architecture for any stateful LLM product:

Consumer AI companions have turned three hard infrastructure problems - bounded context, durable memory, and real-time voice - into their pricing pages. Context size, memory depth, and voice access are the tiers. If you are building anything stateful on an LLM, this category is worth watching closely: it is solving continuity and latency problems at consumer scale, in public, one subscription tier at a time.

It is the total amount of text, measured in tokens, that the model can consider when writing its next reply. The character definition, stored memories, recent chat, and the new message all share that budget. When the conversation exceeds it, the oldest content drops out and the companion appears to forget.

Yes, through a memory system separate from the context window. Apps summarize older chats, extract stable facts about you into a profile, and retrieve relevant past moments into the prompt. Retention varies: some free tiers delete chat history after as little as 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans typically keep memory for the membership period.

A voice turn runs three models - speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech - instead of one, plus audio transfer. Providers pass that cost on through per-minute token charges or by locking voice behind higher tiers.

For casual chat, 4K to 9K tokens works. For long-running roleplay or a companion expected to hold plot threads for weeks, 32K and up, or a well-built memory layer on top of a smaller window, makes the visible difference.

#10495. GTA VI’s pretentious puzzler makes indie developers feel ‘part of larger culture’

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Tenebrae Lux is a gag that has inspired some larger conversations about the gap between indie games and big-budget blockbusters.

Tenebrae Lux is a gag that has inspired some larger conversations about the gap between indie games and big-budget blockbusters.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

One of the most intriguing games of the year is called Tenebrae Lux. It’s a moody, black-and-white title with obtuse puzzles and a main character that constantly narrates to himself with dramatic language. Its credits attribute pretty much everything to a developer named Johannes Zuch. It’s generated a lot of discussion for seemingly mocking games like Jonathan Blow’s The Witness or The Chinese Room’s Dear Esther. It’s also a game that only exists inside of Grand Theft Auto VI.

Developer Rockstar Games has stuffed a lot of satirical media inside of GTA VI, including TV shows you can watch, books you can read, and radio shows you can listen to. It’s all in service of making the world feel like a funhouse-mirror version of our current reality — Ted Lasso star Jason Sudeikis plays a right-wing radio host, for example. There are more games you can play inside GTA VI in addition to Tenebrae Lux, including a zombie-themed roguelike. But Tenebrae Lux, which you have to see as part of GTA VI’s main story, is the one that has struck a chord, including with indie developers like Sam Barlow and Robert Yang.

“I have to admit it’s kind of funny for the biggest blockbuster game of all time to go after arthouse indie,” Yang tells The Verge, noting that it’s “like a fighter jet getting obsessed with an ant, petty in a funny way.” (Given that GTA VI earned $3.79 billion in its first day, the gulf between Rockstar and indie game studios that are struggling to get by is vast.)

But Yang suggested that Rockstar developers are probably well versed in the types of games they’re lampooning in GTA VI. “I don’t think gamers understand how much indie experimental stuff any decent AAA dev plays,” Yang said, pointing out that Rockstar’s environmental artists and level designers “all probably love Dear Esther,” the influential first-person game that helped popularize the “walking sim” concept when it launched in 2012.

“I was mostly taken with what it meant that the millions of people playing the game were having to sit down and play through an extended gag about indie games,” Barlow tells The Verge. “That made me feel like we were part of larger culture.” He added that he hopes some of these players might “seek out what these arty indie games might be.”

Rockstar is self-aware enough to use its video games to poke fun at the medium itself. As part of a long conversation between protagonist Jason Duval and his friend Cal Hampton before they start playing Tenebrae Lux, Cal hypes the game by saying that none of it is “just sitting around waiting for people to finish talking.” Initially, you only play Tenebrae Lux for a few minutes, which is enough time to get stumped on the initial puzzles, before the game cuts back to Jason being bored. “Got anything else, a little less, uh… y’know?” he asks. They play the zombie game, titled Street Trash 3 Dezombifier, right after. (You can go back and play the entirety of Tenebrae Lux later if you want.)

Barlow said that the cut “felt very true to the character and story. I gather there is more to the game, but it felt natural to me to hit a wall with the pretentious puzzle design and then move on!”

Yang also pointed out that from a design perspective, GTA VI has more in common with Tenebrae Lux than Street Trash 3 Dezombifier. GTA VI is “basically a walking sim where you walk around Florida and listen to people talk,” Yang said. “What’s the difference? Well, just like a billion dollars I guess.”

#10494. YouTube appears to be quietly testing a new mute button in its video UI

Users are seeing the option to mute a video appear in the YouTube app on Android, but it isn’t showing up for everyone.

Screenshots of a new video control button have started appearing on Reddit over the last week. The posts haven’t exactly begun hot debates, but it’s an interesting UI change. A mute button now sits next to the fullscreen option in the left section of the video player. Tapping it does nothing other than cut audio, until it’s tapped again to unmute.

One user details the app version on their Pixel 11 Pro XL – version 21.46.522. They also note that other devices they’ve checked on the YouTube app versions don’t appear to show any sort of mute button in the UI. Without many comments on either post, it’s hard to nail down just how wide this rollout might be.

In reality, this seems like a very small UI design test for the YouTube app. YouTube will often select a small number of users to push experimental features to while they polish them further before release. Unlike features that Premium users get to test, these are locked.

It’s not necessarily a big change, but it’s sort of a confusing one. One wonders why a mute button is necessary. It also adds another button to accidentally hit when trying to tap the fullscreen option.

#10487. Yes, the Pixel 10 is still a great buy [Video]

If you’re looking for a decent replacement for an older phone or a backup, and the Pixel 10 looks eerily similar to the Pixel 11, is it still worth it? Well, of course it is, but let’s get into why in this deep dive.

One of the best things about the Pixel 10 is that it looks almost identical to the Pixel 11; in some cases, it’ll work with the accessories for the newest phone – but check that before you try. So that means you’re not getting a majorly out-of-date design.

That doesn’t matter to most people, but it could be important to you. Pixelsnap magnetic accessories still work, and while the Pixel 11 trimmed down the camera bar to an all-glass visor, the overall footprint is virtually identical.

Using the same formula might be stale to some people, but it works, so why change it for the sake of it?

I’d argue that the best news is that this finally shows Google is confident, given its success with the Pixel 9 and 10 design. The company may make changes in the future, but it’s a fairly solid design that feels great after they settled on a physical form factor that works after years of fiddling, and it’s no secret that the goal is to make the switch from iOS to Android as “easy and painless” as possible. A familiar design goes a long way to calming the nerves of a potential switcher. The Pixel 10 is a perfect example of this philosophy. The Pixel 11 essentially proves that too.

Over a year on from release, there don’t seem to be any major or widespread reports of issues on the Pixel 10. I’d say that is a good omen, as people will often know within that timeframe if there are quality control issues and problematic hardware faults.

That said, the only problem I think you have to deal with is the base 128GB storage configuration. It’s not insignificant, but still not going to be tons of room for everything. If you take lots of photos and download loads of things locally to your device, then maybe go for the 256GB model.

One of the weirdest things about the Pixel 10 was that after the Pixel 11 launched along with all other supported handsets, it had a newer version of Android than the latest phone.

Yes, you read that correctly: the Pixel 10 had QPR1 before the Pixel 11.

The entire experience is just more of the same goodness that started with the addition of Material 3 Expressive last year. Android 17 keeps improving; the quality-of-life changes make for a really compelling experience that, while taking some cues from iOS, hasn’t gone full Liquid Glass. Google hasn’t quite kept the day-one update promise like the old days of Pixel, but you still have the cutting edge of the software. Even the Galaxy S26 series isn’t running a release this new and probably won’t for a while.

Rambler has even been added recently, so there is a function bleeding down from the Pixel 11, and there are loads of other quality-of-life changes and features that came with Android 17 QPR1 thrown in. Any bugs that might have been there, however limited, have been addressed in the year the phone has been around.

With the Pixel 10, you may miss out on some of the extra Gemini Intelligence perks or a couple of visual gimmicks like the Pro’s HiLight glow, but none that is yet groundbreaking enough to be drastically upset about.

Sure, the competition might offer a faster processor or newer silicon, but can they offer the fluidity of a system that is fully integrated into all parts of your digital life? That’s what the Pixel 10 delivers.

As I noted, the Pixel 10 has already seen multiple updates, smoothing out early thermal quirks and optimizing battery efficiency. These monthly security patches and Pixel Drops are a testament to the fact that your phone is a constantly evolving product.

With more updates due very soon, the prospect of an even more optimized experience, and many of the best bits of Google all rolled into one handset, it’s a damn good buy, especially as other OEMs are still struggling to get their own Android 17 updates out in a timely fashion. We’re several months on from the full launch, and barely anyone outside of Samsung has offered a wide rollout. Of course, all Pixel 10 devices get day-one updates, with six years of support still left on the clock.

There is one sore point on the base Pixel 10 that I think might make it hard to recommend over the Pixel 10 Pro. The main camera is the same as the Pixel 9a and the 10a on the base 10. It makes it harder to suggest if you care about camera quality.

It’s still super reliable and does take great photos, but it might not be the best in class like previous generations may have been. In fact, I’d go so far as to say that while it’s great you get a telephoto zoom lens, the lower-quality main sensor diminishes it somewhat.

Most people will be better off with the Pro because it has a lot more to work with, but the cameras are still very good on the Pixel 10 and are backed by Google’s excellent updates.

Even video quality stacks up, though I will say Google’s reliance on Video Boost as a workaround for lower-quality footage is a bit of a cop-out. However, it’s a good workaround, and the resulting footage is still excellent, even if I’m hoping for better on-device processing in future Pixel generations.

Ultimately, the camera is a prime example of the Pixel value proposition. You don’t need the absolute latest, greatest, multi-lens Pro Max Ultra setup to take amazing photos. You need a Pixel. The camera on the 10 series is still right up there with the best from Samsung, OnePlus, and others. It’s doing just as much with technically much less.

The biggest reason that many people who are looking for a new phone might want to consider at least the Pixel 10 lineup is that, with discounts, you’re getting a great deal — especially since Google bumped the entry price on the Pixel 11. Sure, bigger, better, faster, and shinier phones exist. They don’t give you the same experience, though. You’re paying for something that others can’t replicate, even at higher price tags.

With more updates due very soon, the prospect of an even more optimized experience, and many of the best bits of Google all rolled into one handset, it’s a damn good buy.

Is the Pixel 10 the best option for a phone right now? Suppose you want arguably the best software and are willing to forgo a few of the bells and whistles. In that case, it is an excellent alternative for anyone looking to save a little money without sacrificing too much. It’s a better buy than the Pixel 10a for most people, in my opinion, and you’ll be hard-pressed to find a more compelling mix of software intelligence and hardware value.

#10486. Serving a Local LLM to Several Users: Ollama, LM Studio, llama.cpp and vLLM Benchmarked

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

When one person chats with a local model, it barely matters which server you run. On one RTX 3090 with the same model file, Ollama, LM Studio and llama.cpp wrote text within about 10 percent of each other, because all three run llama.cpp underneath. That changes when your app sends several requests at once. With four requests, the best setup produced more than three times as much text as the worst, and the cause was a default setting, not the engine. With 32 requests, vLLM produced 2.5 times as much text as the best llama.cpp setup I tested.

This article puts those numbers side by side and shows which setting to change for your app.

I ran every test on one machine. The full write-ups, with every command and the raw results, are on homebrewserver.club.

Ollama, LM Studio and llama.cpp loaded the exact same GGUF files, checked by SHA-256. vLLM cannot run GGUF files well, so it loaded a 4-bit AWQ file of the same model (9.1 GB). Answer quality was not compared.

The tests ran between 8 and 16 November 2026, and speeds on this machine varied by a few percent from one day to the next. Treat a gap under about 5% as a tie. All of this is one consumer GPU and one model family, so read the numbers as a guide to how the servers behave, not as a ranking for your hardware.

This is the short prompt with a 512-token answer.

Ollama and LM Studio both start llama.cpp's llama-server program for each model they load. Ollama has done this since version 0.30, and a comment in its source code says so. On Linux, LM Studio's CUDA runtime is a folder with llama.cpp libraries and a llama-server binary.

Ollama logs the command it starts when you set OLLAMA_DEBUG=1. When llama.cpp was started by hand with that exact command, in a run next to Ollama, its speed matched Ollama's within 1%. So Ollama adds no speed cost of its own. LM Studio was 5 to 10% slower in this test. The test did not separate whether that came from its llama.cpp build or its other start options.

For one user, the gap between Ollama and LM Studio was about 0.4 s on a 512-token answer from the 9B model. You would notice it in long answers, and hardly in a chat window.

A web app rarely sends one request at a time. A chat page, a background summary job and an agent can all call the same model at the same moment. The next test sent four requests at once, each asking for 256 tokens, and measured the total output per second.

A slot is one request that the server works on at the same time as others. llama-server sets the number of slots with -np (short for --parallel), and Ollama and LM Studio pass their own setting through to it.

With one slot, the server answers the four requests one after another, so the total stays at the speed of a single request. That is Ollama's default. With four slots, the server puts the four requests through the model together, and the total rose to more than twice that of one slot. That is the default in LM Studio and llama.cpp. vLLM was the fastest at four requests, but it was tested only with the 9B model. Its lead grows with more requests, as the section after next shows.

For your app, this means that the fourth user of a default Ollama server waits for the first three answers to finish before their own answer starts.

The obvious fix is OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL=4. It worked for the 9B model, though in one of three rounds Ollama still answered at the speed of a single request. With the 27B model, it made Ollama slower than its default.

The cause is how each server sizes the context, the memory that holds each request's prompt and answer. Ollama picks the context from the GPU's memory: 32,768 tokens on a 24 GB card, according to its docs. With four slots, it gives each slot its own 32,768 tokens, so it started llama-server with a 131,072-token context. The Ollama FAQ says the memory needed grows with the number of parallel requests multiplied by the context length.

That context did not fit next to the 27B model in 24 GB. ollama ps showed 16% of the model on the CPU, and the total speed fell to 33.1 tokens per second.

LM Studio and llama.cpp handle it differently. Their four slots share one pool of context:

The price of LM Studio's default is a short context. Four requests sharing 8,192 tokens leave little room for long documents or a long chat history. If your app sends long prompts, load the model with a larger context.

In Ollama, setting a smaller context with OLLAMA_CONTEXT_LENGTH next to OLLAMA_NUM_PARALLEL should avoid the spill to the CPU. The test did not try this, so measure it on your own card.

The last test went further, from 1 to 32 requests at once, with the 9B model. It compared vLLM, a server built to serve one model to many users, with llama.cpp at its defaults and with more slots. Ollama and LM Studio were tested only up to four requests, so they are not in this table.

With its defaults, llama-server chose four slots by itself, so it worked on four requests and queued the rest. Its total stayed near 330 tokens per second from 4 requests to 32. Setting -np 16 or -np 32 roughly doubled the total at 16 requests.

vLLM's lead came from each request running faster inside a large batch. At 32 requests, each vLLM request generated about 61 tokens per second, against about 27 on llama.cpp with 32 slots. vLLM's README names the two techniques behind this: PagedAttention, which stores each request's working memory in small blocks, and continuous batching, which adds new requests to the batch the GPU is already working on.

Total output tells you how much work the server does. The wait for the first word tells you how the app feels to the user behind the last request:

For a web app, that is 22.5 seconds of spinner before any text appears. Long prompts make every wait longer, on both servers. With eight requests of about 4,120 tokens each, the last request waited 7.9 s on vLLM and 8.8 s on llama.cpp with 32 slots.

If you set the slots yourself, the llama-server README lists three options that work together:

If you run an older llama.cpp, update first. Four requests at once ran 14 to 22% faster on v1.0.0 than on the build from five days earlier, while one request stayed the same. The most likely reason is a new CUDA kernel in v1.0 for steps of 2 to 24 tokens on RTX 30-series and newer cards.

vLLM's throughput comes with two costs that matter on a shared machine.

It reserves most of the GPU. By default, vLLM takes 92% of the card's memory before it loads anything, and fills what the model does not need with cache. With a desktop and a browser holding about 2 GiB, vLLM refused to start:

Setting --gpu-memory-utilization 0.6 alone was not enough with this model, because the default limit of 256 sequences needed more cache than fit. Adding --max-num-seqs 32 fixed it. With both settings, vLLM held 12.7 GiB and kept the same speeds. llama.cpp works the other way: it sizes its cache from -c and -np and never asked for more than 15.1 GiB in this test.

It starts slowly. llama-server answered requests 2 to 5 s after its container started. vLLM compiles the model before it serves anything. That took 72 s with its compile cache already on disk, and 173 s on the first start without it. Changing a vLLM argument triggered a fresh compile.

It also needs a different model file. vLLM loads Hugging Face safetensors, so a 4-bit model is a separate AWQ or GPTQ upload. The AWQ file here was 9.1 GB against 5.7 GB for the GGUF, because its recipe leaves some layers unquantized. Check the file size, not only "4-bit", when you size a model for your card.

All four servers have an OpenAI-compatible API under /v1, so the same client code works with each one. You change the base URL and the model name. These are the ports used in the tests:

In Node.js with the openai package, that looks like the sketch below. The measurements came from a Python test client, and this snippet was not part of them:

Qwen3.5 thinks before it answers unless you turn it off. The tests sent "chat_template_kwargs": {"enable_thinking": false} in the request body to vLLM and llama.cpp.

None of the servers asked for a password in these tests. llama-server also warned at start-up that its CORS rule allowed all origins. Bind the port to 127.0.0.1, as in the table, and set --api-key on llama.cpp or vLLM if anything else on your network can reach it.

Before you choose, count how many requests your app really sends at the same moment. Log the timestamps for a day, or count the parallel calls your agent makes per user action. If that number is one, any of these servers will do. If it is four or more, the slot setting will change your users' wait more than the choice of engine.

Not for one request with the same settings. Ollama runs llama.cpp's own server, and the two were within 1% when started with the same command. llama.cpp was faster with four requests at once, because it starts four slots by default and Ollama starts one.

Ollama gives each parallel request its own full context. With four slots on a 24 GB card, that was 131,072 tokens, which pushed 16% of a 27B model onto the CPU. A smaller context may avoid that, but this test did not measure it.

When many requests arrive together. At 32 requests, vLLM produced 2.5 times the output of llama.cpp with 32 slots and kept every first-word wait under a second. For one request at a time, llama.cpp was 5% faster and started in seconds instead of more than a minute.

Usually not. All four serve OpenAI-compatible endpoints under /v1, so you change the base URL and the model name.

#10482. Lenovo Legion Go Beam leaks with Android instead of SteamOS

The variety of gaming handhelds from Lenovo is about to get larger. Leaked details suggest the Lenovo Legion Go Beam will run Android instead of SteamOS on a high-refresh display.

Lenovo is sticking with the Legion Go branding to offer something a little different to users. Instead of a SteamOS or Windows device, the Legion Go Beam is said to run Android (via Windows Latest). Specifically, Android 16.

That doesn’t limit it to Android titles alone. Android happens to make the perfect platform for cloud game streaming. Services like GeForce Now, Xbox Game Pass, and more offer a comparable experience to what a PC does for a fixed subscription, and that’s without the hardware internals something like the Legion Go 2 would need to run games natively.

The leak also notes the Lenovo Legion Go Beam will have a 7-inch 120Hz display, though there’s no information on what resolution or panel type the handheld might have. It’s likely 1080p or higher, though a lower resolution obviously will net users better performance. Battery is up in the air, too, other than a standard “long-lasting battery life” claim attributed to Lenovo.

This handheld takes a lot from the Legion Go 2 in design, as it should. It’s a comfortable device, and this shouldn’t be any different. It lacks a trackpad in favor of a traditional controller layout. It also has standard ports like one USB-C and 3.5mm headphone jack.

Pricing is a mystery. Devices like the Logitech G Cloud were such a hit because they delivered the needed performance at a good price. But those handhelds had a lower refresh rate. Still, hoping for a price tag around $350 or less isn’t such a bad thing.

#10481. GTA VI PC recompilation project shut down by Take-Two

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Modders are racing to get Grand Theft Auto VI running on PC.

Modders are racing to get Grand Theft Auto VI running on PC.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Game modding communities are racing to get Grand Theft Auto VI running on PC, but one big effort has been taken down by Take-Two today. Leonida, a fan project by 15-year-old “NotRexed,” has vanished after a DMCA takedown notice from GTA VI publisher Take-Two.

Leonida was designed to translate a leaked GTA VI Xbox build to run natively on Windows. Cyberleek, the leaker who posted multiple GTA VI gameplay videos over the summer, published a full Xbox build of the game earlier this week. The leak has kicked recompilation efforts up a notch, as modders race to reverse-engineer the game’s code and rebuild it into native PC code.

Some have managed to get the game to boot on PC, but recompiling the full game won’t be easy — even with help from AI tools like Claude. Take-Two is trying to slow this progress, and its IP enforcement team targeted the main GitHub repository for Leonida and more than 2,900 forks of it, claiming that it “infringes upon Take-Two’s copyrighted works and facilitates copyright infringement of Rockstar Games title, Grand Theft Auto VI.”

PortWatch VI is tracking the efforts to recompile, emulate, or port the leaked Xbox build over to PC. At the time of publishing, the VIctory project and forks of it are leading the way. People are also trying to use XCL, a compatibility layer that runs Xbox Series X games on a Windows PC, to get GTA VI running.

At this point, it seems like Take-Two might not be able to avoid the inevitable here. The clock started ticking down to the release of the PC version last week, and if recent reports are true, we might see it in a few months. If GTA VI is close to being officially ready for PC, we could hear about a release before modders manage to get the game running on PC unofficially.

Hardware-wise, you get all of the fixings, including four M.2 sockets (a whopping 3x PCIe 5.0), four SATA ports for storage, a flagship-class last-gen Realtek ALC1220 audio codec (no amps/DACs), 2.5 GbE and integrated Wi-Fi 7 (160 MHz), three full-length PCIe slots for expansion, and plenty of USB ports on the rear I/O, including two USB4 (40 Gbps) Type-C ports. And don't forget about the EZ DIY features.

Gigabyte's X870E Aorus Elite Wifi 7 offers fast Wi-Fi 7 and networking, ample USB ports including dual USB4 (40 Gbps) ports, four M.2 sockets and four SATA ports for storage, and a slew of EZ DIY features on top of a black aesthetic. It's a great base for any AM5 processor.

Next is an awesome deal from Woot.com for a budget-class AM5 board, MSI's B850 Tomahawk Max Wi-Fi, which we reviewed back in 2025 and gave four stars. For the money, the Tomahawk Max offers some mid-range class features, including 5 GbE and integrated Wi-Fi 7 (320 MHz), ample USB ports on the rear I/O, including three Type-C (though the only 20 Gbps-plus is on the front). It's also one of the only boards at this price point with two PCIe 5.0 M.2 sockets (total of four).

Other features include three full-length PCIe slots for expansion, Supplemental PCIe power, and MSI's EZ DIY features like EZ M.2 Shield Frozr II and EZ M.2 Clip II to make M.2 installation seamless. This also extends to the GPU, where a simple button locks/unlocks the graphics card. For the current sale price, there isn't a better B850 board around.

Save big in this budget B850 motherboard from MSI. The Tomahawk MAx Wifi is an update from teh original offering Wi-Fi 7 and 5 GbE, ample USB ports, four M.2 sockets (dual PCIe 5.0) and more.

#10476. 5 Things Most Developers Get Wrong When Turning Images into HTML

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Turning a design image into a working webpage can look simple.

You have a picture (a mockup, a screenshot, a Figma export), and your job is to turn it into working HTML in a browser.

But here is the catch. A design image is static, while a webpage is dynamic and responsive across different screen sizes and devices. A webpage needs to load quickly, adapt perfectly to different screen sizes, and allow touch interactions on mobile devices.

Most mistakes in this process happen when the conversion is treated as copying a picture instead of rebuilding it.

This section walks you through the mistakes developers often make, and more importantly, how to fix each of them.

Whether you are slicing a client mockup or converting a screenshot into code, avoiding these mistakes can save you from hours of rework. Let’s get into the details.

A screenshot reflects how a page appears at a particular display. But it doesn’t show how the layout will behave when the content changes or the screen gets smaller.

Developers sometimes try to copy the exact positions and dimensions of every element. To match the screenshot, they may use fixed widths, large margins, and absolute positioning.

This can work at one screen size, but the layout may break on smaller screens or when a heading wraps onto an extra line.

The better approach is identifying the layout rules behind the design. CSS Grid can arrange the cards in columns. Flexbox can help align elements within each card. And media queries can adjust the layout for smaller screens.

Pixel-level comparison can be useful in some cases, but it should not come at the cost of a flexible layout.

When developers recreate a design, it’s easier for them to focus on shapes and spacing rather than the purpose of each element. So, they may use generic elements for headings, buttons, navigation areas, and content sections instead of choosing elements that reflect each element's purpose.

This may produce the right appearance, but it often makes the code harder to maintain and understand. And when developers fail to use the right HTML elements, it can also create accessibility problems later.

So don’t put everything inside several unnamed containers. Instead, use a clear structure with appropriate headings, meaningful alt text for informative images, and real buttons for actions.

Semantic elements (e.g., header, nav, main, section, and footer) can also help organize a large page when they match the content’s purpose.

A design image may have headings, descriptions, prices, labels, or button text.

Reusing the entire image to reproduce the original design feels quick. But it can create problems when that text has to work as part of a responsive web page.

For example, there’s a promotional banner that contains a heading, a short description, and a CTA button. If you use the entire banner as one image, you can’t easily change the heading, translate the text, or make the button work as a real element.

So, don’t treat the image like that. Instead, use the original image where appropriate and recreate the text and button in HTML. CSS can control their position, size, color, and spacing.

Make sure you distinguish between visual elements that should remain images and text that should be recreated as HTML.

Not every part of turning images into HTML needs to be done manually.

Developers sometimes spend too much time on recreating basic elements when there are free online conversion tools available. These tools can help them get an initial version much faster.

An online conversion tool may be helpful when you need to extract text or generate a rough starting layout from a design image.

For example, you can upload a design image into an online image to html converter to get a starting point (rather than starting from a blank file). Review the tool’s output. Identify the parts you can reuse (as they are) and tweak the parts that need adjustments.

Keep in mind that the first result generated by the tool isn’t the final code. You may need to reorganize the HTML, refine the CSS, and adjust the layout to make it responsive.

Use automation where it saves time and your own judgement where it matters.

A screenshot shows just one view of a page. It doesn’t show how the layout may change on a phone or how people using a keyboard/screen reader can interact with it.

So, developers who focus only on the desktop design may end up with:

content that becomes too small on mobile devices

There’s also a chance of accessibility problems when images lack suitable alt text/interactive elements.

Let’s say a page has three cards in a row. On the desktop, this arrangement may work well. But on a phone, you may need to stack the cards vertically so their content remains readable.

That’s why accessibility should be considered during the same process. Use meaningful headings and provide appropriate alt text for images. Also, maintain sufficient text contrast and make sure interactive elements can be operated with a keyboard.

Lastly, don’t forget to test the page on both desktop and mobile screen sizes before finalizing it.

HTML and CSS handle the structure and appearance of a webpage, but JavaScript makes it possible to add interactive features.

For example, a design image may contain a navigation menu with a button. When recreating this design, developers should make sure the button performs an actual action rather than simply looking like a clickable element.

You can use JavaScript's event listeners to handle these interactions.

Now, add JavaScript to make the menu functional:

In this example, clicking the button opens or closes the navigation menu. The aria-expanded attribute also communicates the menu's current state to assistive technologies.

This is a simple example of why converting a design image into HTML involves more than matching its appearance. Developers also need to recreate the behavior of interactive elements using appropriate HTML, CSS, and JavaScript.

The process of turning an image into HTML feels easy when you treat it the right way.

Start by identifying the main sections, text, images, and interactive elements. Then create meaningful HTML and build the layout with CSS. Make the necessary adjustments to ensure it works across different screen sizes.

Use a conversion tool when it can really save time. But don’t forget to review the generated output of the tool thoroughly before using it.

A screenshot is a visual reference, not a complete set of development instructions. And the best result for you isn’t simply a page that looks like the image.

It is a page that also works well, adapts well, and stays easy to maintain.

#10472. Why GTA VI’s version of Florida feels so real

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Virtual city expert Emma Fraser explains what makes Rockstar’s latest open world work.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

There are plenty of reasons why Grand Theft Auto VI is so popular: the 13 years of anticipation, the relatable lead characters and romantic storyline, the sheer level of depth. But another major factor is the sense of place. The GTA series is unmatched when it comes to re-creating the feeling of a bustling, real-world city, and GTA VI’s fictional rendition of Florida is the studio’s most lifelike setting yet. It has functioning buses and trains, plentiful buildings you can go into and explore, residents you can chat with, and huge areas where you can interact with wildlife far away from the bright lights of the Miami-like Vice City. For Emma Fraser, an assistant professor at UC Berkeley who studies urban ruins and virtual cities, the sense of realism has created a unique sensation while playing.

“There’s something about this particular game that is really discombobulating and distracting in a sense,” she says. “Because you have these moments where you think, ‘I’m in a place, I’m experiencing a location.’ You comprehend intellectually that it’s not real or that it’s at best a representation, but you still have this feeling in your brain and you’ll still dream about being in that place.”

Fraser teaches courses on topics including urban geography and virtual worlds and has written papers exploring topics like “mapping in the digital age” and “the virtual spectacle of the end of the city.” She says she started researching video game cities in part because, despite their influence and reach, they’re an underexplored space in academia. “I’m a geographer, and geographers are obsessed with cities,” she explains. “So much of geography is thinking about: How do we imagine cities? How do we navigate cities? How do we build cities? How do we map cities? And video games have all of that, but they’re not taken that seriously as an academic interest.”

Like much of the world, she’s been looking forward to GTA VI for years, particularly because of Rockstar’s on-the-ground approach to research. Before development on the game began, developers from the studio traveled to Florida to get an accurate picture. As GTA VI producer Rob Nelson told me, the starting point for the game was the setting, rather than the story or characters. “We just went around to feel it out,” he said. “Go to the Everglades, go to the Keys, go to the city, as many places as we can, and start thinking about what aspects of the place we want to incorporate.”

While there are plenty of open-world games, many of the most successful — from Elden Ring to The Witcher — are sent in fantastical spaces. GTA stands apart in how its various settings look and feel true to the real world. And Fraser believes that this is one of the key reasons the series has such universal appeal. “You know immediately how to function in a city,” she explains. “You know there’s going to be law enforcement, there are going to be city blocks, there are going to be pedestrians, cars, and buses. It’s this really interesting environment that gives you a lot of possibilities to get a player more immersed in your world, because they already know a lot about a city as a general concept.”

“You can’t hold a whole city in your mind at one time.”

Fraser describes GTA VI’s world as having “implied expansiveness.” It’s not a one-to-one re-creation of the real location. There are plenty of things missing; you don’t see schools or daycares, and parking spaces are relatively sparse. But the game gets enough details right to be recognizable. Fraser explains that as long as certain parts of the city look correct — the lighting, the coastline, the decoration of street corners, the major arterial roads — that’s enough to convince people that they’re in something resembling Miami.

“You can’t hold a whole city in your mind at one time,” she says. “Instead it’s these highlights. What [Rockstar] do very successfully in the game is those snapshots. They’ve got far more content that is recognizable as Florida or Miami than they’ve been able to fit into previous games. So it’s a much more overwhelming sense of being in that place.” The “implied expansiveness” also comes in the form of dialogue, radio programs, and TV shows that suggest a much bigger world by discussing places and moments that exist outside of the game you can actually explore.

This level of verisimilitude — combined with the fact that players can explore the city from multiple perspectives, everything from walking to flying a plane — has led to an experience that Fraser likens to virtual tourism, which can result in all kinds of different playstyles. “Some tourists are going to blow a lot of money on taxis and get to wherever they’re going as fast as they can,” she explains, “and some people just like sitting on a bus or a train and getting to know a place.” Similarly, there are certain players who will just experience the story and play through the main missions, and others who will get lost in side activities like bird watching.

“Some people just like sitting on a bus or a train and getting to know a place”

What’s also notable about GTA VI is how its relationship-focused premise is motivating some players to experience the world in a much less violent way. And that includes how they move through the city. Some people are driving more carefully to avoid killing civilians, while others are exploring spaces while both characters hang out together. “In the first few games, you’re this empty vessel. You’re a criminal, you’re doing criminal things,” says Fraser. “Whereas with GTA VI your motivation and the way you think about your character is really important.”

Now that the game is out and millions of players are venturing through Vice City, Fraser is hoping to use it to research the ways that real and virtual worlds intersect when it comes to how we navigate cities. GTA VI offers a lot of choice in this regard; you can still steal a Lambo and speed through the streets, but you can also hop on a train, put on some headphones, and listen to a podcast while getting where you need to be. “Because this world is so fully furnished, and you have so many options, you might start to import your own real-world practices into the game,” she says.

But at this point, what has struck her most is the attention to detail that separates the universe of GTA VI from its contemporaries. “You can’t just generate a generic city and stick icons in, which is what a lot of games do,” she explains. “You aren’t going to get a sense of a real place if you do it that way.”
