#10471. Kagi, the paid Google Search alternative, now has a Gmail rival too

Kagi is best known as a paid alternative to Google Search, but the company is now introducing its own email service that offers an alternative to Gmail.

Kagi Mail has been in the works for a long time, the company says in a post today, but it’s only just now launching in public beta. Users get an “@kagimail.com” address or can use their own domain, with both email and a calendar included.

There’s a 60-day trial for Kagi Mail, with the service costing $99.99/year (or included in Kagi’s $25/month Ultimate plan). The reason you’d pay that, Kagi explains, is because this is a product designed to “do its job and then get out of your way,” with no ads or “surveillance” as a part of the package.

Does the world need another email service? We asked ourselves that question more than once. There are great ones out there, and we’re fans of what Fastmail and Tuta are doing. We still built our own, for the same reason we build everything at Kagi: technology should do its job and then get out of your way.

For email, that job is to be fast, private, and secure. On top of that, it should help you stay on track with what’s important: bring forward what you need to act on, respond to, or acknowledge, and quietly handle the rest.

There’s a mobile app coming for Kagi Mail in early 2027, with a contact book and Kagi Assistant integration also slated for early next year. There is some natural language AI for organization, blocking of tracking pixels and remote images by default, and support for email clients like Apple Mail, and Kagi says everything can be exported to move to another provider should you choose.

#10467. Google Maps might change its colors again, but it’s optional this time [Gallery]

Google Maps has a pretty consistent style, but a new option appears to be in the works with a darker “high-visibility” setting.

The last major change to Google Maps styling came in 2023 (yes, time flies) when the platform rolled out a new color palette to all users. This was divisive among users, but ultimately worked out.

Now, Google appears to be working on another new shake-up.

The folks over at Android Authority spotted a new “high-visibility” toggle in the latest Google Maps update for Android, with the new style having darker colors for the map in both light and dark modes, as well as a purple line for your route compared to the blue we’re used to seeing.

The change was also spotted on Android Auto, with much darker colors (including a near-black dark mode) in both color settings for your car’s display. This is likely tied to this new “High-visibility” setting, but it’s hard to say for certain – teardowns and enabling new features early can only tell us so much.

This appears to all be controlled by a new “Map style” setting in Google Maps, with “Standard” and “High-visibility” settings available. Functionally, everything remains the same, it’s just a new set of colors.

So, where the previous color changes were forced on all users, this one is at least optional, and the “High-visibility” naming seems like it’s targeted at those with difficulties in sight more so than just a general change for all users.

#10466. GTA VI developers vote against unionization

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Workers voted 75 percent against the IWGB recognition agreement.

Workers voted 75 percent against the IWGB recognition agreement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Workers at Grand Theft Auto VI developer Rockstar Games won’t unionize, after the vote for a formal recognition agreement failed, with only 25 percent of votes in favor of unionization. The Independent Workers Union of Great Britain (IWGB) said that Rockstar workers in the union will continue organizing even without formal recognition.

Voting ran from November 12th through 26th, covering the launch of GTA VI. If successful, the vote would have established a formal framework for collective negotiation with the studio over pay, overtime, bonuses, leave, and pensions, along with a right to consultation over future changes including AI implementation and layoffs. Without it, Rockstar won’t have an obligation to meet union representatives, though workers and union members can still lobby for change informally.

“For anyone at Rockstar who believes in a fairer workplace, a fairer sector and a fairer society - this isn’t a sign to retreat, it’s a sign to double down on what we stand for,” IWGB said in a statement sent to The Verge. “Hundreds of our coworkers voted in favour of our union at Rockstar, yet sadly we recognise we have more work to do to build understanding and win the trust of more of our colleagues. We’re ready to put in the effort to make that happen.”

When Rockstar fired 31 IWGB members last year, the union accused it of “the most blatant and ruthless act of union busting in the history of the games industry.” That case has gone to a UK employment tribunal, with a judgment still pending.

“We are extraordinarily proud of our team, the culture we’ve built together and the launch of Grand Theft Auto VI,” Rockstar head of publishing Jennifer Kolbe told Bloomberg News. “This result reflects our team’s strong support for continuing the direct relationships, collaboration and open dialogue that have helped make Rockstar Games so innovative and strong. As we look ahead, we’re excited to build on all we’ve accomplished together.”

#10464. Retail Merchandising Principles for Web Designers and Developers: Display Design, Ranking Rules and Measurement

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Every e-commerce grid, product page and checkout screen is a shelf. Merchandisers have spent decades working out what to put at eye level, how many choices to show and how to tell whether a display works, and web teams are now rebuilding the same logic in code: sort orders, pinned products, badges, filters and A/B tests. Knowing where those ideas came from helps you build them with fewer mistakes.

This article maps physical retail merchandising onto digital product design. It covers visual hierarchy and choice load in listing UIs, a small rule-based ranking function you can test, accessible price and badge design with a contrast checker, and a significance test for layout experiments. It also covers why good plans fail at the handoff between design, engineering and operations. The numbers in the examples are illustrative, so use your own data. The code was run with Node.js 22.

Merchandising shapes how products are displayed, promoted and made accessible across sales channels. A well-planned strategy considers visual hierarchy, packaging, promotional materials, inventory timing and consistency between locations. For example, a skincare product may struggle to convert if testers are missing, shelf information is unclear or displays arrive after a campaign begins. If you build for the web, nearly every one of these problems has a direct counterpart:

Customers scan before they read. Nielsen Norman Group defines visual hierarchy as the arrangement of elements so the eye moves through them in order of importance, and names color and contrast, scale and grouping as the main tools. On a shelf, a larger facing or a contrasting background makes the hero product visible first. In a product grid, the equivalents are a larger card for a featured item, a clear boundary around a bundle, and a restrained use of badges so that only one or two items carry emphasis.

A common mistake is giving every card a badge, a color and a call to action. When everything is emphasized, nothing is, and the page reads like a crowded table in a store. Treat emphasis as a limited resource and have the design system say how many emphasized cards one screen may show.

A premium item placed beside entry-level alternatives may lose perceived value, and a crowded display can make fresh products look less appealing. Hick’s Law states that the time to make a decision increases with the number and complexity of choices, and its practical advice is to reduce options when speed matters and to highlight recommended ones. In a listing UI that suggests a curated first screen with a clear sort label, a few high-confidence recommendations and filters that handle the long tail, rather than an unbroken wall of products. Hick’s Law is a guide, not a rule, so treat it as a hypothesis to test on your own audience.

Merchandisers often ask for things like “pin the new range to the top, boost this collection, never show more than two items from one brand in a row, and push sold-out items down”. Implemented as scattered CSS order values and one-off queries, these requests become hard to audit. A small, pure function keeps the rules explicit and testable.

Because the function has no side effects, a short test can check every rule. This one confirms that pinned items lead, a pinned item that is out of stock is skipped, the brand cap pushes a third item from one brand behind other brands, sold-out items go last and a boost can lift a weaker product:

The design decision here is that stock demotion and the brand cap are defaults, while pins and boosts are inputs a merchandiser can change without a deploy, for example from a config file or admin screen. A real system would also log which rule moved each item, so that someone can answer why a product appears where it does.

Price cards and shelf labels are text, and text needs contrast. The WCAG contrast requirement asks for at least 4.5:1 for normal text and 3:1 for large text. Product cards are a place where this slips, because grey secondary prices and pastel sale badges look refined in a mockup. The following script computes the WCAG contrast ratio, so you can run it over your design tokens in CI.

It prints 4.54 for mid grey #767676 on white, which just passes for normal text, 7.00 for #595959, which would also meet the stricter enhanced level, and 3.96 for the pink #d94f70, which fails for small badge text but would pass for large text. A sale badge that fails the check should be fixed in the token, not on one page.

A useful display respects the realities of the sales environment: floor space, customer movement, staff workload, replenishment access and how much information shoppers can absorb at a glance. The digital version is the gap between the design file and what ships. Test the setup with the people who will use it, not only with the team that created it, because their feedback reveals practical problems before a wide rollout.

A polished plan can fail in practice when materials arrive late, displays are assembled incorrectly or teams miss replenishment. On the web, the matching failures are a promotion that goes live before its product data is synced, a badge that stays on after a sale ends, and a hero banner that links to a discontinued item. Execution needs coordination across marketing, suppliers, logistics and engineering, with clear instructions, realistic schedules, display standards and checks. Some companies outsource this kind of field merchandising. One provider’s services page, for example, lists planogram and retail resets, end-cap displays, signage placement, on-shelf availability, in-store audits and visit reporting. Whether or not you hire help, that list is a useful checklist of what execution involves, and most items have a digital twin you can automate: availability checks, expiry dates on promotions and audit reports. Treat any performance figures on vendor pages as the vendor’s own claims and ask for evidence before relying on them.

Timing matters most for seasonal promotions and limited windows. A campaign that goes live after peak demand has passed cannot deliver the same value, so give promotional content a start and end date in the data model and have a scheduled job remove it, rather than relying on someone to remember.

Sales alone cannot tell you whether visibility, placement, stockouts, confusing information or poor setup caused a result. For a web listing, the answer is a controlled experiment. The script below compares conversion for a control grid and a variant with a two-proportion z-test, a standard way to check whether a difference is larger than chance would explain.

With these illustrative numbers, conversion rises from 3.00 percent to 3.90 percent, a relative lift of 30.0 percent, and the z value of 2.21 exceeds the 1.96 threshold for roughly 95 percent confidence. That does not prove the curated grid is better in general. It says this difference is unlikely to be noise for this test, and it assumes you fixed the sample size in advance and did not stop the moment the result looked good. Use a proper experimentation platform or a statistician for anything with real revenue at stake.

A strong merchandising operation does not require a large budget. A simple guide for product priorities, layout, pricing, replenishment and approved materials keeps execution consistent, and it is the physical version of a design system: a small set of approved components and rules that make the correct result the easy path. For a product card, that means a documented component with a fixed set of badge types, a defined maximum of emphasized items per screen and tokens that already pass the contrast check.

Consistency also protects trust. Damaged fixtures, inconsistent packaging, missing prices and outdated signs make good products look poorly managed, and the digital equivalents are broken images, stale prices and mismatched labels between the listing and the product page. Aim for consistency, not identical pages: a mobile grid and a kiosk screen can differ, but they should share product priorities, visual identity and core messages.

Temporary and in-store selling spaces demand quick communication. Visitors move fast, attention is limited, and interfaces must be clear from a distance and forgiving of mistakes. If you build kiosk, tablet or point-of-sale software, these constraints shape the interface:

When results disappoint, audit the real experience before commissioning a redesign. Think like a first-time customer: is the offer clear, are premium items distinct and can the team maintain the page easily? Government small-business guidance offers concrete analogues. The Queensland Government’s retail display guidance recommends showing essential information and prices so customers can decide quickly, placing popular or profitable items at eye level, and refreshing window displays every two to four weeks. The Westminster City Council guide similarly links visual merchandising, layout and planned promotions to attracting customers and encouraging them to spend more time and money in a business.

Prioritize visibility, clarity, stock accuracy and consistent execution before investing in a full redesign. Small operational improvements can make a meaningful difference, especially when they remove obstacles that repeatedly interrupt the buying process.

The public guidance cited here was written for general small-business use, and UX principles such as visual hierarchy and Hick’s Law come from interface research, so they are useful analogies rather than guarantees. The code is a teaching sketch: it ignores personalization, pagination, caching, search relevance and many other concerns of a production ranking system. Results vary with category, audience and device, so test changes in your own setting and treat any benchmark as a starting point.

What is merchandising in e-commerce? It is how products are displayed, ordered, promoted and made accessible across a site or app, including layout, labels, badges, sort rules and inventory handling.

How does visual hierarchy apply to a product grid? The same tools apply: contrast, scale and grouping decide what the eye sees first and which products read as related.

What should I measure first? Start with conversion, add-to-cart rate and stock availability for the listing, then add average order value and revenue per visitor once the basics are tracked.

How do I know a layout test is reliable? Fix the sample size in advance, randomize users, run it long enough to cover weekly patterns and check significance, for example with the two-proportion test above.

Do small teams need a big design system? No. A short component guide with a few badge types, spacing rules and accessible color tokens is enough to keep pages consistent.

Merchandising is applied interface design with a long history. Clear hierarchy, a curated first screen, readable prices, explicit ranking rules and controlled experiments carry over directly from the shelf to the screen. For designers and developers, the practical lesson is that a good layout only counts if it ships correctly, stays current and is measured. Audit the basics, put the rules in code, test one change at a time and record what you learn.

#10463. Hands-on: Sony’s Walkman-revival USB-C headphones are back in stock and worth a look

Sony’s iconic Walkman headphones are back in the USB-C MDR-3R and, after quickly selling out on launch day, they’ve now been restocked. And, after using them, I can see why they sold out so quickly.

The MDR-3R are Sony’s revival of the wired over-ear headphones that many used with a classic Walkman player. The retro style still looks great in 2026, but using an original pair is a bit of a headache in today’s age of headphone jack-less smartphones. So it was nice to see Sony give this a revival with USB-C.

Update 11/24: After we first published our hands-on with MDR-3R last week, Sony’s USB-C headphones sold out pretty quickly again. As of today, though, they’re back in stock and still shipping in early December – don’t miss out!

While the style won’t be for everyone, I think these work brilliantly. The sound quality is excellent and having a pair of headphones that doesn’t fully enclose my ears is pretty refreshing. There’s no noise cancellation, but with open-ear earbuds becoming more popular, I actually really like these as a similar solution (though high volumes will definitely be heard by those around you). I’m not fully cut off from the world around me, but I’m also able to get good sound quality. Lows are the weakness here, with unimpressive bass drops, but the sound quality is crisp and clear and pretty neutral, and the open-ish style makes it feel like the room around me is filled with music.

The physical hardware is great. While a plastic and metal build is objectively not as comfy as a premium pair of ANC headphones, the extremely light weight helps MDR-3R become an afterthought. You simply forget you’re wearing them after a few minutes.

The earpads are swappable, with orange and blue options included in the box.

As for the USB-C cable, it’s fully plug-and-play. No app needed and it worked across every device I tested (including Pixel, which doesn’t work with all USB-C audio output). I do wish the cable was perhaps a few inches longer, but it’s long enough to wear the headphones with my phone in my front pocket. There’s a remote along the cable for play/pause and volume adjustments, though I think it’s a little too high on the cable.

For $59.99, these are a fun pair of USB-C headphones to have on hand. They sound good, work great, and don’t cost an arm and a leg. I can see why they sold out so quickly on launch day – in just 10 hours, Sony told me.

Sony MDR-3R are back in stock now and shipping in early December. If you want a pair, I say don’t wait. They’re well worth at least trying if you can get them for retail price – please don’t give in to the scalpers selling these at double, they’re not worth that.

#10460. Grand Theft Auto VI is a different kind of GTA — so it needed a different kind of ending

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

GTA VI producer Rob Nelson explains Rockstar’s approach to letting players explore Vice City after the main story.

GTA VI producer Rob Nelson explains Rockstar’s approach to letting players explore Vice City after the main story.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

In most Grand Theft Auto games, and most open-world games for that matter, the world is left open for you to explore once the credits roll. It could be Los Santos or a twisted take on Manhattan, but the idea is that the main story is just one part of the experience. After that’s done, there are usually still places you haven’t explored or side missions you left lingering. But Grand Theft Auto VI doesn’t work quite the same way. Because of its storyline, which involves a couple trying to get out of the criminal life for good, that kind of ending didn’t really fit.

“In this one it didn’t make sense,” Rob Nelson, producer on GTA VI and co-studio head at developer Rockstar North, told me ahead of the game’s launch. “Their story needed to end and have some kind of conclusion.”

This story contains light spoilers for the ending of Grand Theft Auto VI, along with a key plot point from chapter 1.

To be clear, you can still play GTA VI after the main storyline wraps up. But in order for that to happen, you have to zip back in time to a moment before a pivotal decision — I won’t spoil it for you — that alters the lives of main characters Jason and Lucia, along with their young son Tomás, who they’re attempting to get custody of. Nelson says the team tried “all sorts of things” but soon realized that “in no way did it make sense” to have the game end like a traditional GTA experience.

“Their story needed to end and have some kind of conclusion.”

“You go back and have this open time again where you have the best single-player safehouse you’ve ever had in a GTA, with a garage full of supercars, a helipad, a gym, and all of the stuff you could ever want,” he says of the solution the studio landed on. “And you can spend as long as you want there. But you don’t have Tommy. So the characters don’t have the one thing that they wanted at the start. There’s the tension where you have to spend some extra time grinding to make some more money without knowing what it’s going to get you, and it plays out differently if you get that money.”

One advantage of this structure is that it lets you try out different endings. GTA VI’s story can wrap up in multiple ways based on a variety of factors: how much money you have saved up, what kind of criminal you were throughout the game, and some key decisions you’re forced to make in the final moments. The new postgame structure creates an opportunity to see some of the alternate ways the story can go. “There’s a large number of potential outcomes based on how you played it,” Nelson says.

Last week, Google announced that Gemini tasks (previously called Spark) is now available to free users in the US, along with an integration in Google Messages. In testing, I’ve found this instance of Gemini to be proactive and feel more like an assistant than the Gemini app does.

This updated experience makes use of the “Channels” feature introduced last week that allows you to Text or Email with Gemini to accomplish tasks.

With the latest Google Messages beta (version 20261120_01_RC00), we’re seeing the Gemini FAB (or top-right corner icon with the homepage redesign) open gemini.google.com/channels on a fresh install. If you’ve already set up the new integration, the shortcut takes you to the new chat, which uses RCS Business Messaging behind the scenes.

#10454. 5 AI Tools for Creating Audio-Led Music Videos: A Practical Workflow

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

You can open on the singer’s first line, cut to another image as the instruments answer, and return to the singer for the key line of the chorus. If that return depends on a convincing close-up, test it before building the rest of the video. Choose a phrase with a quick consonant followed by a held vowel. Those two sounds give you clear points to check against the recorded vocal.

Use the recorded vocal as the timing reference. AI tools can help build opening shots, instrumental cutaways, and singing performances. Compare MakeSong, HeyGen, and Hedra on the singing shots themselves, and try Freebeat for an audio-led visual draft.

For creators developing multimedia experiences, the workflow also connects with broader web technologies. SitePoint's guide to building better video experiences on the web explores some of the practical considerations involved in working with video content.

AISong provides an image-led way to build the visual world around a recorded track. It fits a performance video that uses atmosphere and supporting shots in addition to views of the singer.

AISong’s AI Music Video Generator combines the song with a set of image references for a multi-shot sequence. Use that feature for the opening, an instrumental cutaway, or an establishing scene that can support the performance footage. Check whether the setting and the singer’s close-up look like they belong in the same video.

When preparing these assets for a website or multimedia project, consistency between images and video clips is important. This is also relevant to the process of building an AI media pipeline from concept art to web video.

Portrait art, rehearsal stills, or images of the setting can give these supporting shots an established look. It is particularly useful when some parts of the song call for visuals that give the audience a break from the performer.

MakeSong can build a video around the recorded track and reference images. Its lip-sync option requires a real human face. Start with a clear frontal image and check the result against the original vocal.

MakeSong’s AI Music Video Generator accepts audio, images, and a written prompt. The product page also offers lip sync for eligible face references. Give the close-up a particular vocal phrase and reserve a wider shot for the next musical response. Check mouth timing and the performer’s appearance at the cut.

This approach works when a single visual brief needs to connect the performance with the supporting scenes. The artist should have an approved reference and a clear idea of when the singer should appear.

HeyGen is a performer-led music video option. It is particularly relevant when the same on-screen singer should remain recognizable through more than one setting or camera distance.

Its music video workflow accepts a song and supports singing performance. Use a close and a wider view to compare mouth timing and continuity on the same vocal phrase. Watch for changes in the face or outfit when the framing shifts.

Choose this route when viewers need to follow the same singer across several scenes. This makes more sense than a single avatar clip when the performer is the visual thread for the whole video.

Hedra can animate a portrait from an audio clip. Try a single line with a quick consonant followed by a held vowel to see where the mouth falls behind the recording.

A clear portrait and one clean line give you a controlled test. Inspect the start and end of the phrase, then check how the clip cuts to the next shot. When only one line is convincing, use that line and let the original edit carry the rest.

A short close-up or a fictional singer’s entrance in the chorus is a good use for this approach. It is a practical fit when you already have an edit and only need one additional vocal moment.

Freebeat offers an audio-to-video workflow with a performance-oriented mode. It gives artists another way to test a performance cut driven by an uploaded track.

Use its singing video route for an excerpt with an audible pause and a clear vocal re-entry. Compare whether the performance feels natural during the pause and whether the visual action resumes where the vocal does. Keep the same approved track for the comparison.

Try this early to compare a full performance treatment with a version built from shorter clips before settling on an edit.

Decide how much of the release the singer needs to carry. An image-led sequence can support the track around key lines, a performer-led workflow can keep the singer present through the full edit, and an avatar clip can cover a single important phrase.

For developers and designers working with audio and video on the web, the final production stage also deserves attention. Video formats, playback behavior, and audio synchronization can affect how the finished content performs across devices. SitePoint's tutorial on the Web Audio API provides useful background on handling audio in web applications.

Judge the hardest line first and use the original vocal as the reference at every cut. Once the performance works, build the supporting shots around it and prepare the final video for its intended platform.

#10453. This gaming mouse should be a Logitech-killer, but it has one big flaw

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Keychron’s G6 HE uses magnetic switches like Logitech’s Superstrike mice, but the clicks are all off.﻿

Keychron’s G6 HE uses magnetic switches like Logitech’s Superstrike mice, but the clicks are all off.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

When Keychron announced its $90 G6 HE gaming mouse, I immediately thought, “Cool! A cheaper alternative to the pricey $180 Logitech G Pro X2 Superstrike.” Logitech’s innovation with the Superstrike is using magnetic Hall effect switches for a user-adjustable mouse click depth, with haptic vibrations mimicking the tactile click feel. The Keychron G6 HE, as its name indicates, also uses Hall effect switches for that same kind of customization. But something felt immediately off as I began testing it.

The mouse’s shape and design are very reminiscent of Logitech G Pro models, with more colorful flair. The magnetic switches are there and easily customizable (through a browser-based app, no less). But the clicks? The clicks are all wrong.

The left and right mouse buttons on the G6 HE use Keychron’s MagOptic switches, which are both magnetic Hall effect and optical. They click just like any standard mouse, regardless of where you set the actuation point. There are no fancy haptics on the Keychron. If you set the buttons to a shallow hair-trigger depth, you’ll register a press before you actually feel or hear any click. Set the depth for a very heavy press, and the mouse clicks before it actually registers. That… kind of defeats the purpose here.

There’s not much point in tuning the actuation depth of the G6 HE’s buttons if the click feel doesn’t match it. I suppose it’s still nice to have a hair-trigger-like setting for taking faster single shots in an FPS, but not feeling anything when you shoot is weird. And it gets frustrating when using the G6 HE as a regular mouse for productivity — like you’re getting phantom clicks as you mouse around your computer. At least I can feel my accidental clicks when using a Logitech Superstrike with the action set too high, and I know I’m to blame for leaving the mouse on my try-hard settings.

I reached out to Keychron for the reasoning behind the G6 HE’s magnetic-but-not-haptic setup, and cofounder Fab Chan said, “There are two reasons for this design. First, many users still prefer the feel of a traditional click over haptics. Second, a haptic motor adds weight, and for this model we prioritized a lighter design. That said, we’re planning a model with haptic feedback. It will be slightly heavier than the G6 HE, and we’ll share more details soon.”

The G6 HE is a lightweight mouse. It’s only 49g with its battery, or 40g in wired mode with the battery removed. The Logitech X2 Superstrike is 60g, and the newer X3 Superstrike is 59g — each still pretty light. If you ignore all the Hall effect stuff and just use the G6 HE as a regular mouse, it has some things going for it over the Superstrikes. In addition to its lighter build and much lower price, it feels a tiny bit comfier in my hand, thanks to its side walls that curve slightly inward at the bottom.

One of its best features is hot-swappable batteries. From my time testing Angry Miao’s AM Infinity Mouse, I know that once you can pop out a magnetic battery pack and quickly swap it, you never want to go back to plugging in your mouse to charge. You can also get the G6 HE in black with a standard built-in rechargeable battery for $15 less, but I don’t think that’s enough savings to forgo the convenience of hot-swap batteries.

The G6 HE’s charging base doubles as the wireless receiver, and of course it’s lit up with customizable RGB lighting. It has four programmable buttons for on-the-fly changes to things like DPI, lighting, mouse profiles, or just about anything else you want to program. The browser-based Keychron Launcher software is robust and makes tinkering with the G6 HE or its base very easy. And for some reason, the receiver / charging base is designed like a vacuum tube, which is fun, but it means you’re unscrewing its clear housing to access the battery — a little tedious.

I can’t shake my disappointment with the G6 HE’s lackadaisical magnetic switch implementation. Keychron seems proud of the fancy MagOptic Switch it put into the G6 HE, even going so far as to call it “next gen,” but this mouse seems a jack of all trades and master of none. The G6 HE is cheaper than most other mice with hot-swap batteries — which can still make it worthwhile if you don’t care about all the Hall effect stuff — but that is its lone saving grace.

We’ll have to wait and see if Keychron’s follow-up model with haptics can properly compete with the Superstrike mice and their hallmark feature. I’m excited to see where Hall effect tech goes in mice, and how more companies can evolve it, but the G6 HE is a half-measure.

Photography by Antonio G. Di Benedetto / The Verge

#10447. Google Maps update on Android Auto adds navigation routes for motorcycles

Google Maps is rolling out an update on Android Auto that provides better navigation routes specifically for motorcycles.

Users in India have recently noticed that Google Maps for Android Auto is showing a new message asking them to restart the app to “switch to two-wheeler directions.” Once enabled, a motorcycle icon appears on the ETA card.

On a support page, Google details this mode, explaining that the mode tailors directions to skip routes where two-wheelers are not allowed, while also included narrow roads that may be faster. Google says that the feature is only available in “limited locations.”

These directions show you routes away from roads where two-wheelers aren’t allowed. You may be directed towards shortcuts along narrower side roads.

The page also explains that it is only available for mobile devices, with no mention of Android Auto and CarPlay. Yet, a number of users are seeing the adjustment on their devices within the past two weeks, so it seems Google is expanding it out to Android Auto. That makes sense, as Android Auto has been working to better accommodate motorcycles for a while, and some motorcycles even now have Android Auto support built-in.

#10444. YouTube TV sale drops the price to $49.99/month for a limited time

#10438. Sizing GPU Memory for a RAG Service: KV Cache, Context and Concurrency

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) app is usually drawn as a language model plus a vector database. When it goes live, the part that fails first is rarely the retrieval. It is GPU memory on the generation model, because a model that loads fine for one request can run out of memory when several users send long questions at once.

This article shows how to estimate that limit before you deploy, with a short script you can run, and then covers what else in the pipeline can slow responses down.

Serving a model takes more than its weights. The GPU also holds:

the key-value (KV) cache, which grows with the length of every active request

memory used by the inference runtime itself

SitePoint's guide to running a local LLM on 10GB of VRAM makes the same point for a single desktop GPU: the usable budget is the total VRAM minus overhead, and the KV cache grows with the context window, so the real model budget is smaller than the number on the box.

For a RAG service, the KV cache matters even more. Retrieved passages are pasted into the prompt, so every request carries a long context, and every concurrent request holds its own cache.

The cache size per token depends on the model's architecture: two tensors (keys and values) for every layer, for every KV head. This script turns those numbers into a rough limit on concurrent requests:

def kv_bytes_per_token(layers, kv_heads, head_dim, dtype_bytes=2):

# Keys and values, for every layer, for one token.

return 2 *layers *kv_heads *head_dim *dtype_bytes

def max_concurrent(vram_gb, weights_gb, ctx_tokens, per_token, reserve_gb=2.0):

budget = (vram_gb - weights_gb - reserve_gb) * GIB

return int(budget // (ctx_tokens * per_token))

per_token = kv_bytes_per_token(32, 8, 128)

print(per_token // 1024, "KiB per token")

n = max_concurrent(24, 16, ctx, per_token)

print(ctx, "tokens ->", n, "concurrent requests")

For a 24 GB GPU holding about 16 GB of fp16 weights with 2 GB kept in reserve, it prints:

The numbers are estimates. Real runtimes add their own overhead, and the architecture values come from the model's configuration file, so read them from the model you actually use. The pattern is what matters: doubling the context length halves the number of requests that fit. A RAG prompt that stuffs in ten retrieved chunks costs real capacity.

Serving runtimes do not simply crash when the cache is full. The vLLM documentation on optimization and tuning explains that when KV cache space is insufficient for all batched requests, the engine preempts some of them and recomputes them later, which can hurt end-to-end latency. Its suggested remedies include giving the cache more memory, reducing the number of concurrent sequences or batched tokens, or sharding the model across GPUs.

So the symptom of undersizing is often not an error. It is slow, uneven responses under load. Test with realistic prompt lengths and several simultaneous users, not a single request.

Embedding and ingestion have a different profile from generation. Embedding is often lighter than serving a large language model, and some teams run it on CPU. Document parsing, chunking and tokenization also run on CPU, so a fast GPU cannot make up for a slow CPU upstream. SitePoint's tutorial on a local RAG system for private documents walks through chunking, embeddings, vector storage and reranking, and is a good map of which stages run where.

RAM: vector indexes, document loaders and caches all live in system memory, so large collections need more of it.

Storage: model files, indexes, logs and backups add up. Fast storage shortens model loading and re-indexing.

Network: if the vector database, application server and model server sit in different places, latency adds to every request.

Two layouts are common. A single instance runs everything and is simplest for prototypes and internal tools. Separated services put ingestion and retrieval on smaller machines and keep the GPU for generation, which scales better as traffic grows.

Memory estimates only hold if the GPU memory is really yours. On a shared or time-sliced GPU, other workloads can take memory and compute away from your service, which makes capacity hard to plan. Hosting providers describe this in different terms, such as passthrough, vGPU, MIG and time-slicing. For a view of how one GPU hosting provider explains those allocation methods, GPU Mart's GPU virtualization guide compares them. It is a hosting company's page, so confirm any provider's claims about isolation and performance with your own load test.

Whatever provider you choose, ask these questions:

Is the physical GPU assigned to you exclusively, or partitioned or time-sliced?

Do you have root access to install your own drivers, CUDA libraries and containers?

Which minimum commitments, regions and support terms apply?

A small stack can run as a handful of containers: an API service for request handling and prompt assembly, an ingestion worker, a vector database and a model server. Keeping them separate lets you monitor each one's memory and CPU use and move the heavy one to bigger hardware without rewriting the rest.

For a service that stays up all month, a fixed monthly term is easier to forecast than hourly capacity. Hourly capacity suits testing quantization settings or processing a one-off document batch.

Is GPU memory always the bottleneck for RAG?

Not always. Generation usually hits memory first, but slow parsing, a small CPU or a distant vector database can dominate response time.

Does quantization solve the memory problem?

It shrinks the weights, but the KV cache still grows with context length and concurrency. Test with your real prompt sizes.

It depends on volume. Small workloads can run on CPU, while a large ingestion job may benefit from a GPU for a limited time.

Size a RAG service for the worst realistic moment, not the single-request demo. Estimate the KV cache from the model's architecture, remember that long retrieved contexts reduce how many requests fit, watch for preemption under load, keep CPU, RAM, storage and network in the plan, and find out whether the GPU is exclusive or shared. The script above is small, but it turns a vague worry about memory into numbers you can check.

#10428. From Script to Screen: A Developer's Workflow for Long-Form AI Video

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Generating a single AI video clip is easy now. You send a prompt and get a few seconds of footage back. The trouble starts when the goal is three, five, or ten minutes of video where the same character looks the same in every scene, the style does not drift, and the captions line up with the picture.

Most guides stop at the clip. This one covers the part after it, treating a video as a small build pipeline: a storyboard that is data, prompts generated from that data, clips normalised and stitched with ffmpeg, and captions derived from the same source. If you want to wire a generation model into your own application first, SitePoint's guide to integrating generative video APIs into web applications covers the request and webhook side. If the finished video is headed to a YouTube channel, how to get a YouTube API key and use the Data API is a good starting point for the publishing side. Everything between those two is below.

Long-form AI video fails at the seams between clips, not inside them. Plan for continuity before you generate anything.

A storyboard stored as JSON can be validated, versioned, diffed, and turned into prompts by code, which a pile of loose prompts cannot.

Repeat the full character description in every prompt. Models do not remember the previous clip.

Clips from different generations rarely share resolution, frame rate, or codec settings. Normalise them before joining.

Captions are cheapest when generated from the script and measured clip durations, not transcribed afterwards.

Current video models generate short clips, commonly a handful of seconds each. A longer video is therefore many clips, and every clip is an independent generation. The model has no memory of what the character wore in the previous one.

That produces three kinds of failure. The character drifts, so hair, clothing, or proportions change between scenes. The style drifts, so lighting and colour grade shift from cut to cut. And the technical specs drift, so one clip is 24 frames per second at one resolution and the next is something else. Each of these is fixable, but only if the project is organised so the fixes can be applied consistently.

Start with a structured storyboard instead of a document full of prompts. One file holds the style, a "character bible", and an ordered list of scenes.

"style": "soft watercolor picture book illustration, warm evening light, 16:9",

"mira": "a nine year old girl with short curly black hair, a yellow raincoat and red rubber boots",

"lantern": "a small round paper lantern with a gentle orange glow and a tiny smiling face"

"setting": "a narrow cobblestone street in light rain",

"action": "walks slowly past closed shops, looking up at the sky",

"camera": "slow push in from a medium shot",

"narration": "It had rained all afternoon, and the street was quiet."

"action": "finds the lantern glowing on a wooden crate and kneels beside it",

"narration": "Then she noticed something glowing under the awning."

Because it is data, a script can check it before you spend any generation credits. Two checks pay for themselves quickly: every character named in a scene must exist in the bible, and no scene may be longer than the clip limit of the model you use.

const known = new Set(Object.keys(board.characters));

errors.push${scene.id}: unknown character "${name}");

${scene.id}: ${scene.duration}s is over the ${MAX_CLIP_SECONDS}s clip limit, split it into shots

Treat MAX_CLIP_SECONDS as a setting. Limits differ between models and change over time, so check the one you are using rather than trusting the number in this example.

Never write the per-scene prompt by hand. Generate it, so the style line and the character descriptions are identical every time.

The character description appears word for word in every scene that includes that character. This is the single most effective consistency tactic that needs no special tooling. Changing a detail, say the colour of the boots, becomes one edit in the bible instead of a search through twenty prompts, and the diff shows exactly what changed between versions.

Put the style line first and keep it identical across all scenes.

Describe one action per clip. Two sequential actions in one prompt often produce a blend of both.

Keep camera directions simple. A single move per clip is more reliable than a combination.

Avoid naming real people, brands, or existing characters in prompts. Beyond the rights issue, it makes results harder to reproduce.

Clips from separate generations often disagree on resolution, frame rate, pixel format, or codec parameters. Joining them directly can produce stutters, green frames, or an outright error. Convert every clip to one agreed format first.

-vf "scale=1280:720:force_original_aspect_ratio=decrease,pad=1280:720:(ow-iw)/2:(oh-ih)/2,fps=24,format=yuv420p" \

-an -c:v libx264 -crf 18 "norm/$(basename "$f")"

The filter scales each clip to fit a 1280 by 720 frame, adds padding if the aspect ratio differs, sets 24 frames per second, and forces a widely compatible pixel format. The -an flag drops any audio the model generated, since narration will be added as one track later. Name clips with zero padded numbers such as s01.mp4 and s02.mp4, so a shell glob sorts them in story order.

Once every file shares the same parameters, the concat demuxer can join them without re-encoding:

for f in norm/*.mp4; do echo "file '$PWD/$f'"; done > list.txt

ffmpeg -y -f concat -safe 0 -i list.txt -c copy stitched.mp4

Hard cuts are the safe default. Crossfades are possible with ffmpeg's xfade filter, but they require careful offset arithmetic and a re-encode, so add them only when a cut genuinely looks wrong.

Captions are easiest when they come from the script, not from a speech recognition pass over the finished video. The storyboard already holds the narration text and each scene's length, so an SRT file is a few lines of code. Use measured durations rather than planned ones, because a generated clip is often a little shorter or longer than requested.

const { execFileSync } = require('node:child_process');

const ms = Math.round(totalSeconds * 1000);

const h = String(Math.floor(ms / 3600000)).padStart(2, '0');

const m = String(Math.floor((ms % 3600000) / 60000)).padStart(2, '0');

const s = String(Math.floor((ms % 60000) / 1000)).padStart(2, '0');

const rest = String(ms % 1000).padStart(3, '0');

const end = cursor + scene.duration; // set from measureSeconds() after generation

${blocks.length + 1}\n${toTimestamp(start)} --> ${toTimestamp(end)}\n${scene.narration}\n

Update each scene's duration from measureSeconds on the normalised clip before calling buildSrt. If a narration line is long, split it across two caption blocks so it stays readable. Then combine picture, voiceover, and captions in one final step:

ffmpeg -y -i stitched.mp4 -i narration.mp3 -i captions.srt \

-c:v copy -c:a aac -c:s mov_text -shortest final.mp4

Soft captions like these can be switched off by the viewer and read by assistive technology, which is better than burning text into the picture. Keep the .srt file as well, since most platforms accept it as a separate upload.

Everything above is a good fit when you need repeatability: many videos from templates, versioned storyboards, automated publishing, or tight control over the model. The price is that you own the glue code, the ffmpeg settings, and the failures.

For one off projects, or when the people making the video are not developers, a managed platform is often the more sensible choice. Tools such as magiclight.ai bundle story to video generation, character handling, voice, and an editor in one interface, and advertise videos of up to 50 minutes. Check the specifics yourself, because claims like that are marketing, and results depend on your content. One point matters for developers in particular: if you need to automate generation from your own code, confirm that the platform you pick offers an API or export route before you build a workflow around it. A tool with a good editor and no programmatic access suits a different job than the pipeline in this article.

A short manual pass catches what automation does not. Watch the whole video once at normal speed, looking only at continuity. Read the captions against the audio. Check that no real person's likeness, brand mark, or protected character slipped into the footage. Finally, check the disclosure rules of the platform you publish on, since many now ask creators to label realistic synthetic media, and the rules change.

The model is only one component of a long-form AI video. The reliability comes from the structure around it: a validated storyboard, prompts generated from it, clips forced into one format, and captions derived from measured data. Each of those is ordinary software engineering, and none of it depends on which generation model you choose, so the pipeline survives when the model changes.

Start with a two minute video and four or five scenes. Fix the seams there before scaling up, because every continuity problem you find at that size is one you will not have to hunt for in a ten minute project.

#10426. Arm strongarms site that hosts ad-free documentation and it may disappear

Easy-to-use, popular, not collecting data for us, or serving our ads? Down with you!

Arm is swinging the hammer at an enthusiast’s site hosting a bloat-free version of its instruction reference documentation, demanding it be taken offline after several years of availability.

The demand from Arm lawyers to Jon Palmisciano concern Jon’s Arm Reference, a site that provides the same arm Instruction Set Architecture (ISA) info available on Arm’s site, but with what Palmisciano describes as “without a million popups and all of the bloat” that Arm provides on its own.

According to Palmisciano, his copy of the Arm ISA was culled from machine-readable XML files and stripped of the bloat. He built his version in 2022 with new CSS on top of it, and the addition of a quick search feature that, according to his comments on Hacker News, isn’t available in the official version.

“The aim has always been to make it the most comfortable and bullshit-free browsing experience for people who need these docs daily,” Palmisciano wrote in HN. Based on the responses the site seems to have plenty of fans.

That doesn’t matter to Arm, though: As far as the lawyers are concerned, Palmisciano’s site is a copyright violation due to its use of Arm trademarks and inclusion of copyrighted reference material for the Armv9-A and Armv9-A architecture.

Arm doesn’t care if Palmisciano’s project is well intentioned or for the benefit of the many software developers that write code for its chipsets, either: They want it offline by December 4 – 10 days from the publication of this story – or else.

As for what is going to happen, it seems like Jon’s Arm Reference will likely cease to exist in a little over a week – at least in its current form.

“Ideally I'd love to just get permission from Arm to keep the site up, but I'm pretty pessimistic about that,” Palmisciano told us. That said, Palmisciano has “already seen a few archives and mirrors already.” The HN thread discussing the takedown order includes links to several versions, and Palmisciano has another trick up his sleeve that he’s considering playing: Open-sourcing the software he used to build his ISA page.

“I'd also consider open-sourcing my tooling so that people can (for their own private use) turn the original XML downloads from Arm into an identical site they can host locally,” Palmisciano told us. He said that he'll likely tweet about opening the tooling if he does do so. He's also considered directing the URL for Jon's Arm reference to point to the open-source tool, but Arm wants the subdomain it's hosted on removed from his site as well, so that may not happen.

Arm has been in touch to defend itself as well, saying that Arm appreciates the efforts of people like Palmisciano, but that it still has to protect its brand, and documentation.

"We have a responsibility to ensure Arm is the primary source of technical resources and information and to protect the Arm brand," an Arm spokesperson told us. "Striking the right balance between third parties sharing helpful peer-to-peer resources while ensuring copyrighted Arm-related technical content is accurate has been critical to building the thriving ecosystem we have today."

In other words, if you’re an Arm dev worried about losing a valuable resource and having to turn to a website that bombards you with junk just to look up a few simple opcodes and operands you’d better find a backup host before next month.

That said, Arm is willing to work something out with Palmisciano, the company told us.

"We have recently flagged our concerns with regard to the use of Arm copyright content and the Arm brand on this website and shared the Arm trademark guidelines," Arm explained. "Our hope is to open a constructive dialogue, and we've asked that the website owner reach out as we seek to resolve this."As Palmisciano posted on HN, Arm is asking for removal of its trademark and copyrighted documentation. That, Palmisciano said, "seems hard to work around while still keeping the site useful."  He also followed up around 8.30 pm GMT on Tuesday to let us know that he has not yet heard directly from Arm. ®

#10421. 'We are not ShinyHunters,' says criminal hacking crew that looks and acts just like ShinyHunters

Inception, a new data theft and extortion gang that security researchers have theorized is a rebrand of the ShinyHunters group, “reject[s] this attribution” and claims it has no affiliation with any prior cybercrime enterprise.

In a lengthy statement posted Tuesday and shared on X, the extortionists insist that they are a new group, with new operators, and that their alleged social engineering attacks aren’t copying anyone - but rather just abusing the weakest link in an organization’s security: “a human being holding a phone.”

The rant follows a Monday report by Okta Threat Intelligence - along with social media speculation since Inception’s inception last week - that the newly emerged group is very likely ShinyHunters with a different name.

Okta’s researchers based their analysis on the two groups’ shared infrastructure, plus Inception’s nearly identical tactics: voice phishing, during which the scammers trick targets into entering credentials into phishing pages and approving multi-factor authentication (MFA) requests.

Inception apparently took offense to this. “We would like to address this once, clearly, and only once,” the crims' statement says. “We are not ShinyHunters. We were never ShinyHunters. We are Inception.”

The gang argues that its overlapping domain registrars, hosting providers, and DNS services don’t prove anything. These are commodity services, “available to anyone who can pay for them,” Inception wrote.

Plus, their similar methodologies don't necessarily make Inception a reboot. “That is not theft of tradecraft,” they wrote. “It is acknowledgment of a good idea – the same way every group after LockBit used a leak site, and nobody called them LockBit.”

“Inception is a new group, with new operators, our own objectives, and our own name,” the statement continues. “What we share with the past is plumbing.”

To be fair, the new crime crew has every reason to distance itself from ShinyHunters.

That gang’s September FBI hack - plus several earlier digital heists during which the criminals stole information belonging to millions of people, including cancer patients, university and K-12 students - put a massive target on its collective backs.

And while at least three ShinyHunters suspects were arrested in September and October, there's no confirmation that authorities have dismantled the entire operation.

Plus, as a ShinyHunters spokesperson told The Register in an earlier interview, they and others in the crew started off as GnosticPlayers before rebranding as ShinyHunters in 2020 - so a reboot isn’t beyond the realm of possibility.

Inception, for its part, says it “will not comment further on the ghost of ShinyHunters,” and it has its own work to do. And by work, it means copying ShinyHunters' playbook, stealing corporate data, and extorting victim companies for massive sums of money.®
