#10419. Your AI Pipeline Works. But Can You Trust It?

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI is becoming a part of almost everything we do in technology today. Whether it's detecting fraudulent transactions, assessing credit risk, forecasting demand, or personalizing customer experiences, organizations are increasingly relying on AI to make faster and smarter decisions.

And naturally, when we talk about building AI systems, most of the conversation revolves around model accuracy, performance, and deployment. We spend considerable time making sure our models are trained well, our pipelines run efficiently, and our systems can handle production workloads.

But here's something we don't always pay enough attention to: Can we actually trust the data feeding these models?

After all, even the most sophisticated AI model can produce misleading results if the underlying data is incomplete, inconsistent, or incorrectly interpreted. What's interesting is that these problems don't always show up as technical failures. Sometimes, everything appears to be working perfectly fine.

Imagine a financial institution that has recently deployed an AI-powered loan risk assessment system. The team has spent months preparing the data, training the model, testing its performance, and finally moving it into production.

The deployment goes smoothly. Data pipelines are running on schedule, predictions are being generated, and all the monitoring dashboards are green.

A few weeks later, however, the business team notices something unusual. The model has started classifying more customers as high risk than expected.

The data science team checks the model configuration. Nothing has changed. The engineering team reviews the pipelines. All jobs have completed successfully. Even the infrastructure looks healthy.

It turns out that an upstream source system changed the definition of a customer income field from annual gross income to annual net income. The field name remained the same, the data type was still numeric, and none of the existing technical validations detected the change.

The pipeline continued running as expected, but the model was now receiving information with a different business meaning.

This brings us to an important point: Just because an AI pipeline runs successfully doesn't necessarily mean we can trust its output.

Building reliable AI systems requires us to look beyond the model itself. We need to understand where the data originates, how it changes as it moves through different systems, whether it meets quality expectations, and who is responsible when something goes wrong.

In this article, we'll explore three important elements of trustworthy AI pipelines (data quality, data lineage, and accountability) and look at how engineering teams can incorporate them into their existing workflows without making the process unnecessarily complicated.

Most modern data pipelines come with some form of operational monitoring. Engineering teams track job completion, execution time, system availability, and processing failures.

These metrics are important because they help us understand whether our systems are functioning as expected.

Operational monitoring primarily tells us whether a pipeline executed successfully. It doesn't necessarily tell us whether the data being processed is accurate, consistent, or suitable for the model consuming it.

Suppose we're building a loan risk model that uses a customer's debt-to-income ratio as one of its input features.

Debt-to-income ratio = Monthly debt payments / Monthly income

Now imagine that an upstream system starts supplying annual income instead of monthly income.

The calculation still executes successfully. The values are numeric, the transformation doesn't throw an error, and the model continues generating predictions.

However, the resulting debt-to-income ratio is incorrect because the numerator and denominator represent different time periods.

This is where traditional monitoring falls short.

To build trustworthy AI pipelines, we need to look at different dimensions of system health.

A pipeline can be operationally healthy while processing unreliable data. Similarly, a model can continue producing predictions even when the meaning of an important input field has changed.

For engineering teams, the objective should be to identify these problems as early as possible, ideally before they reach downstream models.

We've all heard the phrase "garbage in, garbage out."

And despite all the progress we've made in artificial intelligence, this principle hasn't changed.

AI models may be capable of processing enormous volumes of data and identifying complex patterns, but they cannot automatically guarantee that the information they're receiving is correct.

This is where data quality becomes important.

Data quality refers to how well information meets the requirements of its intended use. In AI pipelines, it helps us determine whether incoming data is sufficiently reliable for model training, evaluation, or inference.

Let's look at four dimensions that are particularly relevant.

Imagine we're developing a loan risk model that relies on customer income, outstanding debt, repayment history, and credit utilization.

What happens if 20% of the incoming records are missing income information?

Should we reject those records? Should we replace missing values using an approved statistical method? Or should we flag them for additional review?

There isn't one answer that works for every system.

The right approach depends on the model, the business requirements, and the potential consequences of the decision.

What matters is that missing information is handled deliberately.

For example, replacing missing income with zero may allow the pipeline to continue running, but it could significantly distort the model's predictions.

Validity checks whether values meet expected formats, ranges, and business rules.

Consider a customer record containing the following information:

The credit utilization value immediately stands out.

Although credit utilization can exceed 100% in certain circumstances, a value of 450% may warrant investigation.

This highlights an important distinction: Not every unusual value is incorrect, and not every technically valid value is meaningful.

A database may accept any numeric value, but the business may need additional rules to determine whether that value is appropriate for the model.

Engineering teams should distinguish between invalid records, unexpected observations, and legitimate exceptions.

This is one of the most common challenges in enterprise data environments.

Imagine two systems providing customer income information.

Both datasets contain a column called income.

If we combine these datasets without understanding their definitions, we risk introducing inconsistencies into downstream calculations.

The data may look perfectly fine from a technical perspective. The problem is that the same field represents different business concepts.

This is why data definitions, units, and transformation rules are just as important as schema validation.

Suppose a model predicts a customer's current financial risk using income information that was last updated 18 months ago.

The data might be accurate, complete, and consistent.

But is it current enough to support the decision?

Different AI applications have different freshness requirements.

A real-time fraud detection model may require near-real-time transaction information, while a long-term forecasting model may work with less frequently updated datasets.

The important thing is to define these expectations based on how the data will be used.

The good news is that improving data quality doesn't always require sophisticated enterprise tools.

A practical starting point is to define validation rules for the most important input fields.

For example, consider the following conceptual configuration:

This is an illustrative data quality contract rather than executable configuration for a particular framework.

It defines basic expectations and specifies what should happen when the data fails validation.

Tools such as Great Expectations can help automate these checks. For smaller systems, custom SQL or Python validations may be sufficient.

However, technical validation alone isn't enough.

Remember our original example where the income field changed from gross to net income?

Both values could easily pass a rule requiring income to be greater than zero.

Detecting that kind of issue requires clear business definitions, communication with upstream systems, and appropriate change management.

In other words, data quality isn't just about checking values. It's also about understanding what those values represent.

Now let's assume our loan risk model generates an unexpectedly high risk score for a customer.

The model output is available, but we want to understand what contributed to the prediction.

The income information came from a customer application system. Outstanding debt came from another database. Both datasets went through several transformations before reaching the feature engineering pipeline.

The final features were then passed to the model.

At this point, a seemingly simple question becomes surprisingly difficult:

Where exactly did the information used for this prediction come from?

This is where data lineage becomes valuable.

Data lineage describes how data originates, moves, and changes across different systems.

For AI pipelines, it helps us understand the journey from raw source data to the features used by a model.

Source Systems → Data Transformation → Feature Engineering → Model Inference → Predictions

Each stage introduces dependencies that can affect downstream results.

Data lineage can be captured at different levels of detail.

Dataset-level lineage shows relationships between datasets.

Loan Applications → Cleaned Applications → Model Features

This helps us understand how datasets move through the overall architecture.

However, suppose the model's debt-to-income ratio suddenly changes.

Dataset-level lineage might tell us which datasets were involved, but it may not provide enough detail to identify the affected field.

That's where column-level lineage becomes useful.

debt_to_income_ratio = monthly_debt_payment / monthly_income

Column-level lineage can show that the resulting feature depends on two upstream attributes.

If one of those attributes changes, engineers can trace the dependency and investigate the impact.

This is especially valuable in environments where multiple reports, applications, and AI models depend on shared data sources.

Large organizations may use dedicated metadata management and lineage platforms.

However, smaller teams can start by capturing essential metadata during pipeline execution.

OpenLineage provides an open standard for collecting and exchanging lineage metadata across supported systems.

It's important to understand that lineage doesn't automatically guarantee data accuracy.

A pipeline can be perfectly documented and still contain incorrect transformation logic.

But when something goes wrong, lineage gives engineering teams a clearer starting point for investigation.

Instead of searching across multiple systems and trying to reconstruct dependencies manually, they can identify where data originated and how it changed along the way.

An AI model starts producing unexpected results.

The data science team checks the model and finds no issues.

The data engineering team confirms that the pipelines are running successfully.

The source-system team verifies that data was delivered according to its interface.

Everyone has completed their individual checks, but nobody has identified who should coordinate the investigation.

This is where accountability becomes important.

AI pipelines often involve multiple teams responsible for different stages of the data lifecycle. Without clearly defined ownership, even relatively simple issues can take longer to resolve.

A practical accountability framework should distinguish between three responsibilities.

Data owners are responsible for defining the business meaning and expected quality of critical information.

For example, someone needs to determine whether an income field represents gross income, net income, or another financial measure.

These definitions should be documented and communicated to downstream consumers.

Data engineering and platform teams typically manage data ingestion, transformations, validation mechanisms, and pipeline operations.

Their responsibilities may include implementing quality checks, monitoring failures, and assessing the technical impact of changes.

However, technical teams shouldn't be expected to make business-definition decisions without appropriate input.

Data science and ML engineering teams are responsible for evaluating model behavior, managing model versions, and assessing how changes in input data may affect predictions.

If a critical feature changes, the model team may need to determine whether additional testing, recalibration, or retraining is required.

Defining ownership is useful, but connecting it to an operational process is even more important.

Consider a critical data quality failure during feature generation.

The exact workflow will vary depending on the organization and the risk associated with the application.

The objective is to ensure that technical alerts lead to clear actions.

After all, an alert that nobody owns is just another notification waiting to be ignored.

So far, we've discussed why data quality, lineage, and accountability matter.

But how can we bring these concepts into everyday engineering practices?

Here are five controls that teams can introduce without redesigning their entire architecture.

Data contracts define expectations between data producers and consumers.

They can document schemas, field definitions, units, freshness requirements, and compatibility expectations.

For example, rather than specifying only that annual_income is numeric, a contract should clarify whether it represents gross or net income, the currency, and the applicable time period.

This reduces the risk of downstream systems misinterpreting technically valid information.

Quality gates validate critical data before it progresses through the pipeline.

These checks can be implemented during ingestion, transformation, feature generation, or inference.

For example, a feature engineering process calculating debt-to-income ratios should verify that debt and income values use compatible time periods.

Failure handling should reflect the severity of the issue. Some records may be quarantined, while more serious failures may require stopping the pipeline.

Pipeline execution should produce sufficient metadata to identify source datasets, transformations, and downstream outputs.

Where appropriate, teams should also capture model and feature versions associated with predictions.

This information supports impact analysis, debugging, and investigation when unexpected results occur.

Datasets change, transformation logic is updated, features are modified, and models are retrained.

Versioning helps engineering teams understand which components were active at a particular point in time.

Relevant evidence may include pipeline configurations, dataset snapshots, model versions, validation outcomes, and deployment records.

Every critical data element or pipeline component should have an identified owner or responsible team.

More importantly, ownership should connect directly to incident management and change approval processes.

If a source definition changes, teams should know who evaluates the impact.

If a quality gate fails, the responsible team should receive the alert.

If model performance deteriorates, the investigation should have a clear escalation path.

These controls are most effective when they become part of normal development and deployment practices rather than separate administrative activities.

Implementing comprehensive governance across an entire data environment can be challenging, particularly for organizations with legacy systems and complex dependencies.

A more effective approach is to begin with one critical AI pipeline.

For example, a team operating a loan risk model could start by identifying the inputs that have the greatest influence on its predictions.

These might include income, outstanding debt, repayment history, and credit utilization.

For each critical input, the team can document five things:

Once these foundations are established, the team can implement automated checks and gradually extend the approach to additional datasets.

This incremental strategy also makes it easier to demonstrate the value of governance controls.

For instance, teams can track how quickly data-related incidents are detected, how long investigations take, and whether recurring issues are reduced.

The goal isn't to introduce governance for its own sake.

It's to improve the reliability, maintainability, and transparency of the systems developers are responsible for operating.

Explainable AI has become an important topic in machine learning.

Techniques such as feature importance analysis and SHAP can help teams understand which inputs influence model predictions.

However, explainability and data trustworthiness address different problems.

Suppose a model explanation identifies debt-to-income ratio as a major contributor to a customer's high-risk classification.

That explanation may accurately describe the model's behavior.

But if the debt-to-income ratio was calculated using an incorrectly defined income field, the explanation alone may not reveal the underlying data issue.

This highlights the importance of evaluating AI systems across multiple dimensions.

Model explainability helps us understand how predictions are generated.

Data quality helps establish whether inputs satisfy defined expectations.

Data lineage provides visibility into where information originated and how it was transformed.

Accountability ensures that identified problems can be investigated and addressed.

These capabilities are complementary rather than interchangeable.

None independently guarantees that an AI system is accurate, fair, secure, or compliant. Together, however, they provide a stronger foundation for evaluating and improving system reliability.

As AI systems become more integrated into business processes, engineering teams face increasing expectations around reliability, transparency, and accountability.

Model accuracy and operational performance remain essential, but they represent only part of the overall picture.

A pipeline can execute successfully while processing inconsistent information. A model can generate predictions using features whose definitions have changed. And an explanation can describe a model's behavior without identifying problems in the underlying data.

Building trustworthy AI therefore requires attention to the entire data lifecycle.

Data quality controls help detect unreliable inputs. Lineage provides visibility into how information moves through systems. Clear ownership ensures that issues can be investigated and resolved.

Importantly, these practices don't need to begin with large-scale governance programs or expensive technology investments.

Engineering teams can start with a single critical pipeline, identify its most important data elements, establish quality expectations, and introduce basic traceability and accountability mechanisms.

Over time, these practices can become part of standard development and deployment workflows.

The real measure of a trustworthy AI pipeline isn't simply whether it produces a prediction. It's whether the organization can understand, validate, and take responsibility for the information behind that prediction.

After all, a green dashboard is reassuring. But it shouldn't be the only evidence that an AI system is working as intended.

#10414. AI godfather Yann LeCun torches Anthropic CEO over AI gatekeeping

‘It’s like the Ottoman Empire banning the printing press for Arabic so they would keep in control of the dogma,’ he says

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei is taking heat from AI luminary Yann LeCun, one of the founding fathers of modern machine learning, because of the Claudefather's fear-mongering around open-weights models, which also happen to compete with his products.

In an X post this week, LeCun likened the Amodei's advocacy to a “new form of medieval obscurantism.”

“Dario’s spiel since GPT-2 has always been about hyping the dangers so that open models would be made illegal, and he would stay in control,” LeCun wrote in response to a now three-year-old video posted to the platform in which Amodei warned US Congress members about the dangers of open-weights models, the use of which could not be monitored or policed.

“He left OpenAI because he wasn’t going to have control. It’s like the Ottoman Empire banning the printing press for Arabic so they would keep in control of the dogma.”

LeCun is one of the most influential names in AI and ML. His team is credited with the development of convolutional neural networks (CNNs) in the late ’80s during his time at Bell Labs. He later oversaw the development of Meta’s Llama herd of models as chief AI scientist before shifting gears away from LLMs to world model architectures like JEPA. He has also been a strong proponent of open source AI development and open models.

Of the major model houses, Anthropic has taken the most hostile position against the adoption of open-weights models.

Despite advocating against the unregulated diffusion of open models for years, Anthropic’s fearmonger-in-chief has maintained that the company isn’t opposed to them. “Anthropic has never advocated for a ban on open-weights models,” Amodei wrote in a blog post earlier this year.

The executive has instead fomented fears over their potential misuse.

“Open-weights models — it does not matter whether they come from China or anywhere else — do potentially present a higher risk than closed models, because it is very difficult to apply guardrails to them or monitor their usage, and, once weights are released, they cannot be withdrawn,” the blog post read.

Amodei has repeatedly hammered these talking points over the past several years, including in the Senate hearing that solicited LeCun’s post.

Amodei has also called for measures, such as stricter export controls on the export of high-end accelerators to China; a crackdown on industrial-scale distillation operations, for which his company has previously accused Alibaba and other Chinese model devs; and mandating safety testing for sufficiently advanced open-weights models.

In other words, Anthropic doesn’t want all freely downloadable models banned, just the ones that Amodei thinks aren’t playing fair or are powerful enough to compete with its own.

LeCun isn’t the only one who has pushed back against anti-open-weights rhetoric in recent memory.

Earlier this summer, a cadre of tech titans, including Meta, Microsoft, IBM, Dell, and Nvidia, wrote an open letter [PDF] in support of open-weights models.

Open models “strengthen safety and cybersecurity, accelerate innovation and diffusion, and enable sovereignty,” Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said at the time. “The world needs both frontier closed models and frontier open models.”

As we previously reported, Nvidia’s future depends on its ability to sell to a diverse set of customers ranging from the big model houses like Anthropic and OpenAI to public clouds, neoclouds, and enterprises. And while many of these companies are perfectly happy to get their AI through Claude or ChatGPT, others, whether it be for regulatory or cost reasons, are not.

Earlier this month, we reported how British neobank Revolut cut their vibe coding tab by 10x by moving its roughly 2,000 employees from Claude to Z.AI’s GLM 5.1 running on a cluster of 56 Nvidia B200 GPUs.

The problem for Anthropic, and OpenAI for that matter, is the more enterprises that embrace open-weights models, the less likely they are to spring for Claude or ChatGPT.

The Register reached out to Anthropic for comment, but had not heard back at the time of publication. ®

#10412. Android Auto update improves support for wide displays, but not everyone will see it [Gallery]

Google has rolled out a new update for Android Auto that improves support for wider display formats, but there’s a good chance you won’t be able to see it.

Android Auto has to scale to a lot of different display shapes and sizes, and its functionality varies depending on what you’re using as the platform adapts to what your display can handle. This can sometimes mean you are locked out of a specific feature or need to access it another way. A couple of infamous examples of this include the “Report” option in Google Maps being hidden on smaller displays, as well as the weather card only being on larger, taller displays.

That said, Google is now making an effort to improve how apps on Android Auto adapters to wider formats.

In the latest app update (v18.2), Android Auto is tweaking apps to switch from a top-bar layout to a sidebar for navigation. So, in Spotify, this means the “Home,” “Recents,” “Browse,” and “Library” shortcuts move to a sidebar rather than being up top.

It’s a good use of space, as it allows more content to be visible on wide but short displays. Android Authority spotted the change across Spotify, YouTube Music, and Phone, implying this is probably a platform-level change rather than being tied to individual app updates.

It’s a great change, but one you might not be able to see.

As mentioned, Android Auto adjusts to take advantage of whatever display space it has available, and that often includes putting Google Maps off to the side of other apps. While you can easily set Maps to take up the full display, other apps are less likely to be able to do that, with Maps semi-permanently off to the opposite side. In a car with a wide display we tested for this new format, Maps prevented any other apps from going wide enough to show the new layout, so your results may vary. In many cases, this new layout may simply not be visible. In a way, though, that’s a good thing – it shows that Google is addressing even the most niche aspects of Android Auto, and that’s a win for everyone.

#10410. WhatsApp officially adds scheduled messages, and the rollout is scheduled too

It’s about time. WhatsApp announced today that it is finally adding support for scheduled messages in its app.

A staple of many other messaging apps, WhatsApp is adding scheduled messages to its app. This gives users the ability to write a message but schedule sending it to another user or group chat at a later time.

Scheduled messages on WhatsApp can be sent up to 14 days after you draft them in both 1:1 chats and group chats. You can schedule through a long-press on the send button, with a menu popping up to let you pick the future time when the message will be sent. You can also see scheduled messages before they’re sent to cancel sending or edit the message.

WhatsApp notes that these messages are sent even if your phone’s battery is dead or if the device is off, and everything is still end-to-end encrypted. It works with text, photos, and videos, but apparently not with voice notes.

That said, the rollout is also scheduled out. WhatsApp says the feature will start showing up for users “over the coming weeks” globally, so you might be waiting a bit longer for this long overdue release.

#10408. Dell and HPE both say they’ve become ‘different companies’

Still slinging hardware – now with added AI and attendant risks or opportunities

Dell and HPE this week both told investors they’ve become “different companies.”

The two hardware giants made that assertion on their respective earnings calls this week. HPE went first, on Monday, when CEO Antonio Neri declared “We are now a fundamentally different company” after acquiring Juniper Networks and creating products “built to capitalize on the next phase of AI.”

On Tuesday, Dell’s chief operating officer and vice chairman Jeff Clarke declared “We are now a different company” because the Infrastructure Solutions Group (ISG) has become the source of “the vast majority of our profit and growth.”

HPE’s claim to having changed is perhaps more credible on the face of it, given that its acquisition of Juniper means it has become more of a networking player.

Dell hasn’t made significant additions to its hardware portfolio, but there’s a big difference in its sources of revenue, which three years ago was just about evenly split between its infrastructure and client devices. In its Tuesday announcement, Dell revealed ISG revenue grew 198 percent year over year to $42 billion, while CSG racked up three percent growth to $12.9 billion – meaning Dell is now predominantly an infrastructure company.

Clarke said he’s seen some companies deferring their PC purchases to buy AI hardware instead, and said Dell is re-assigning parts it ordered to build PCs and using them for servers instead.

Dell exited its Q3 with a backlog of $32 billion worth of AI servers and is clearly aware that investors might worry that’s not a useful metric because some buyers’ dreams of operating neoclouds have struck financial or regulatory barriers. Clarke assured investors that Dell is “highly selective about the partners we work with”

“Every AI order is vetted for access to capital, end customer quality, trade and export compliance, and access to land and power.”

HPE also has wrinkles in its AI business, because buyers won’t stand for fat margins and that’s dented the company’s profits a little. Neri was able to point to AI-related revenue also rising sharply, up 68.6 percent year over year to $11.3 billion, while overall revenue climbed 50 percent to reach $14.5 billion. Networking grew at a more relaxed 12 percent.

The CEO said HPE expects better growth ahead, because new server purchases create demand for networks and storage. He also pointed to HPE having the opportunity to sell its networking kit to other OEMs who build AMD’s Helios rack-scale systems. HPE predicted its next quarter will see revenue reach $14.0 billion to $14.6 billion, up 51 to 57 percent year over year.

Dell is also optimistic that AI buyers will spend on kit beyond servers and GPUs and updated its full-year revenue outlook to $202 billion, $10 billion above its previous forecasts and up 78 percent year over year. ®

#10406. How Developers Can Detect Fake Payment Gateways and Build Secure Checkout Integrations

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Online payments are an essential part of modern web applications. E-commerce platforms, subscription services, and online marketplaces all depend on payment gateways to process transactions and manage customer orders.

However, integrating a payment gateway involves more than adding a payment button or displaying a checkout form. Developers must ensure that payment requests are sent to legitimate providers, transaction information is validated correctly, and payment confirmations cannot be manipulated.

Fake payment gateways introduce another challenge. They can imitate legitimate payment interfaces, use copied checkout templates, and redirect customers to fraudulent websites. Even when a checkout page looks professional, its underlying implementation may not be trustworthy.

For developers, understanding these risks is important when designing payment workflows. This article explores common payment integration vulnerabilities and practical techniques for building secure checkout systems using JavaScript and Node.js.

A payment gateway connects an application with a payment processing service. It handles transaction requests and communicates payment results to the merchant's application.

A typical checkout process involves several components:

A frontend checkout interface where customers review their orders.

A backend application that creates payment requests.

A payment provider that processes transactions.

A webhook endpoint that receives payment notifications.

A database that stores order and transaction information.

Attackers can target different parts of this process.

For example, a fraudulent website may copy the appearance of a legitimate checkout page while sending submitted information to an unauthorized server. Another approach involves manipulating payment requests or creating fake confirmation pages that make an unsuccessful transaction appear complete.

These techniques are also relevant to marketplace fraud. An eBay scam can involve fraudulent payment requests and misleading transaction links, illustrating why developers need to consider payment security beyond the appearance of a checkout page.

The important distinction is that a legitimate-looking interface does not establish that the payment request is reaching an authorized provider.

One of the first steps in reviewing a payment integration is checking where the application sends its payment requests.

Developers should inspect network requests using browser developer tools. The Network tab can reveal which domains receive requests when a customer opens the checkout page or submits a payment form.

Look for unexpected requests, unfamiliar domains, and scripts loaded from sources that are not part of the approved integration.

For example, a checkout application might load a payment script like this:

The URL above is illustrative, not an actual payment provider.

Developers should compare the script URL and integration method with the provider's official documentation rather than trusting a URL simply because it appears familiar.

Reviewing external JavaScript dependencies.

Checking whether payment forms submit data to approved endpoints.

Confirming that HTTPS is used throughout the payment process.

Reviewing changes to payment-related scripts during development.

A Content Security Policy (CSP) can also restrict which domains are allowed to load scripts and receive certain types of requests.

These example domains must be replaced with the actual domains documented by the selected payment provider.

A carefully configured policy can reduce the risk of unauthorized scripts and unexpected form submissions.

A common mistake in payment development is trusting values submitted directly from the browser.

Consider an application where the frontend sends an order ID and payment amount to the backend.

An attacker could modify the amount before submitting the request. If the backend accepts that value without checking the original order, the application may create a payment session for an incorrect amount.

The safer approach is to retrieve the order from the database and calculate the amount on the server.

Here, payment Gateway create Checkout Session() represents a provider-specific server-side SDK method.

The important part is that the backend retrieves the amount from its own database rather than accepting a price supplied by the customer.

Developers should also validate product IDs, quantities, discounts, currency, and order ownership before creating a payment session.

This prevents frontend manipulation from directly determining the final transaction amount.

Developers should avoid building payment processing around unverified scripts, copied templates, or unknown third-party services.

Instead, use official APIs and SDKs provided by established payment processors.

A hosted checkout integration is one common option. The merchant's backend creates a payment session through the provider's API, and the customer is redirected to the provider's hosted checkout page.

This approach can reduce the amount of sensitive card information handled by the merchant's application.

However, developers still need to configure the integration correctly.

Store secret API keys in server-side environment variables or a secrets manager.

Never expose private credentials in frontend JavaScript.

Use the provider's official SDK where available.

Validate checkout session creation on the backend.

Restrict success and cancellation URLs to approved destinations.

Use HTTPS for application and payment communication.

Developers should also understand the difference between public and secret API credentials. Public keys may be designed for browser use, while secret keys must remain on the server.

The OWASP Third-Party Payment Gateway Integration Cheat Sheet provides additional guidance on securing payment requests, validating transaction details, and handling payment callbacks.

Payment providers often use webhooks to notify applications when transactions are completed, failed, or refunded.

However, webhook endpoints are publicly accessible URLs in many integrations. Without proper verification, an attacker may send a fabricated HTTP request that looks like a successful payment notification.

Developers should verify webhook signatures using the payment provider's official SDK.

Consider this simplified Node.js example:

The verify Webhook() method represents the signature verification functionality of a payment provider's SDK. It is not a universal Node.js method.

The example also assumes that the provider's event contains the relevant order and transaction details. Developers should follow the provider's actual event schema and retrieve the authoritative payment status when required.

The application should never mark an order as paid simply because a customer has reached a success URL.

Instead, payment confirmation should depend on an authenticated provider notification or a server-side verification request.

Another important issue in payment development is duplicate processing.

Imagine a customer clicks the payment button twice because the first request takes too long. The application may create two payment sessions or attempt to charge the customer more than once.

Network interruptions and automatic retries can create similar situations.

Idempotency helps prevent these problems by ensuring that repeated requests with the same idempotency key do not create duplicate operations.

A simplified example using Node.js might look like this:

The example uses a provider-specific idempotency option. Actual SDK syntax and key requirements vary between providers.

In a production application, developers should also store payment attempts in the database and enforce appropriate uniqueness constraints.

This helps maintain consistent transaction records even when requests are repeated or interrupted.

A payment integration should be tested beyond the normal successful checkout process.

Most established payment providers offer sandbox environments that allow developers to simulate transactions without charging real customers.

These environments are useful for testing different transaction scenarios.

Developers should also test whether the application handles provider downtime correctly.

For example, if the payment provider becomes temporarily unavailable, the application should not mark an order as completed or assume that the transaction succeeded.

Automated integration tests can help verify these scenarios before deployment. Monitoring payment failures, unexpected status changes, and repeated webhook errors can also help developers identify problems after release.

Security does not end when a payment integration goes live.

Developers should implement monitoring to identify unusual transaction patterns and unexpected changes in payment behavior.

Recording payment attempts and transaction IDs.

Monitoring webhook signature verification failures.

Detecting repeated requests for the same order.

Alerting on unexpected order status changes.

However, logging must be implemented carefully.

Applications should never store card verification codes or expose secret API keys in application logs. Sensitive information should be excluded from debugging output, and access to transaction records should be restricted.

Developers should also follow the payment provider's security requirements and applicable PCI DSS obligations when handling payment data.

Detecting fake payment gateways and building secure checkout integrations requires more than checking whether a payment page looks legitimate.

Developers need to examine the complete transaction flow, validate payment information on the server, use official payment APIs, verify webhook signatures, and prevent duplicate transactions.

JavaScript and Node.js provide useful tools for implementing these security controls, but the reliability of a payment system ultimately depends on how its frontend, backend, database, and payment provider communicate.

A well-designed checkout integration should verify every important transaction before fulfilling an order. By following these development practices, teams can build payment systems that are easier to maintain, more reliable, and better protected against common payment manipulation techniques.

#10405. Save serious money with RedPocket Mobile’s $5 Black Friday plans

You’re not going to believe this, but there’s one part of your life that’s not actually getting more expensive. If you switch to RedPocket Mobile during its Black Friday sale, you can ditch your costly carrier and get a plan that starts at $5 per month. It’s not too good to be true: RedPocket has been around for 20 years, and unlike most MVNOs, it’s independently owned and operated. That gives it the ability to offer you real network choice when you sign up for a new plan (we’ll explain more later), and keeps your costs low. Really low.

But you’re reading this on 9to5Google, so how does it impact you as an Android user? That’s a great question.

RedPocket is offering $150 rebates on Android devices like the Pixel 11, Galaxy S26, Galaxy Z Flip 7, Moto G Play, and more. (You can also get $100 rebates for iPhones if you’re into that sort of thing.)

These offers run from November 9 through the 30th at redpocket.com, so don’t wait too long to save big.

It’s OK if you haven’t heard of RedPocket Mobile, because it has been quietly working for 20 years to improve the way people use their phones. Founded in 2006, the company works with all three major US carriers — AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon — and because it doesn’t own any of its towers, it can pass along significant savings to its customers. Here’s what the company says on its website:

RedPocket was built for price-conscious consumers who want real value and freedom from rigid, high-cost contracts. As a no-contract carrier, we’ve stayed focused on making reliable, transparent, and flexible phone service for everyone. Our plans are designed to fit real lifestyles, whether you’re a heavy data user, a frequent traveler, or just looking to cut your monthly bill.

Most MVNOs are owned by the Big Three, which means you don’t really have a choice of network when you sign up. But RedPocket works with all three major carriers and lets you choose which one works best for your location and usage patterns.

RedPocket offers a tool called CoverageGenius that helps you decide which network is right for you. While you can’t switch between networks once your line is set up, it’s a great way to figure out where to start.

I teased that $5/month cost at the beginning of the article, but here’s the real breakdown: you can get a full year of service for $60 if you’re a light data user. RedPocket’s Annual Essentials and Annual Plus plans get you unlimited calling and texting in the US, along with calling and data roaming in dozens of countries around the world. The main difference is how much data you get: 3GB for Essentials and 20GB for Plus.

Annual Premium ups the ante to unlimited data, plus calling and data roaming in even more countries.

Across all three plans, you’re saving between 33% and 50% off the regular price when you sign up between November 9th and 30th.

One thing to note about all of these plans: the annual prices are paid upfront, not billed monthly. Promotional pricing applies to the first year, and after that, annual plans renew at $110 for Essentials, $220 for Plus, and $330 for Premium.

RedPocket understands that it may be hard to commit to a full year of service even if it’s super cheap. That’s why it offers monthly plans, too, that can serve as a trial for any of the networks. It costs just $10 for the first month to try any of the three plans, with Monthly Plus rising to $20/month after that and Premium $30/month thereafter (Essentials stays at $10/month).

If you need a new phone to go along with your new plan, RedPocket is a great place to get it.

#10401. Nutanix CEO announces gradual departure, intent to complete set of storage links

Rajiv Ramaswami will stay in the chair until new hire lands, then stick around as advisor

Nutanix CEO Rajiv Ramaswami has decided to step down from the job once the company’s board can find his successor.

Ramaswami announced his decision on Tuesday and in conversation with The Register said he doesn’t have another job lined up, isn’t sure what he will do next, fancies sitting on boards or advising startups, but will take a break after Nutanix finds its next leader.

The CEO moved from VMware to Nutanix in 2020 and characterized his time at the company as writing its “second chapter.” The first, he explained, saw Nutanix focus on hyperconverged infrastructure (HCI) and a limited group of workloads, especially desktop virtualization, while struggling to make a profit.

In chapter two, Nutanix redefined HCI as “hybrid cloud infrastructure” by enabling its stack to run on-prem or in public clouds, and by adding support for containers alongside VMs. Ramaswami took credit for stabilizing Nutanix’s finances while leading the expansion of its platform and for keeping net promoter scores high while the company grew.

The CEO added that Nutanix’s decision to end its devotion to virtualized storage and start to support external arrays was also part of chapter two. Ramaswami said he hopes to expand the number of storage vendors Nutanix supports beyond Dell, Everpure, and NetApp, until it works with all major array suppliers.

Chapter 3, he said, will be making Nutanix an AI platform, “by catering to a whole new set of ecosystems.”

Ramaswami told The Register he had been thinking about moving on for some time and raised it with the board in recent weeks. In the letter he wrote to Nutanix staff, the CEO said the company's board has begun the search for a successor and said he will remain at his post until a replacement is found and then “continue to serve as a senior advisor to the company for a period of time.”

News of the CEO’s planned departure came on the same day Nutanix announced its Q1 results, which saw 15 percent revenue growth to $772.4 million. The company added 410 customers in the quarter, the slowest growth for several years. Ramaswami said high hardware costs remain “a headwind” for Nutanix, but said the company continues to win customers seeking an alternative to VMware. He also pointed to the largest new customer win for the quarter coming from a financial services outfit that uses Dell external storage, a significant score because two years ago Nutanix could not have competed for that business.

Nutanix forecast slightly faster growth for the rest of its financial year, suggesting it is not ready to ride AI’s rocket to riches. Investors knocked about ten percent off the company’s share price in after-hours trading, sending it to around $50, roughly double what it was when Ramaswami took over as CEO, but below $79-plus peaks in late 2025. ®

#10397. Hello Weather is the best Apple Weather app on Android

While Pixel Weather serves my needs quite well, I’ve been looking for a way to check Apple Weather (previously Dark Sky) forecast data on Android, and I’ve finally found it with Hello Weather by Electric Dream Machine.

Hello Weather started out on iOS before coming to Android a few years ago. On Apple devices, the app got a big redesign last year, and it just came to Android last month with a promise that updates will roll out to both platforms at the same time going forward.

The app has a four-tab layout with the bottom bar aligning with the latest Material 3 Expressive height, avoiding a common pitfall. One small customization is being able to turn on/off tab names.

Locations shows a list of all your saved cities, while Forecast is the main view. The current condition with a large icon — five different sets are available — appears first. Be sure to enable the stats row so you can see cards like Wind, Cloud Cover, and UV Index without having to tap for the bottom sheet. When open, you get the following stats presented as a grid: Today (temperature range), Precipitation, Wind, Humidity, UV Index, Cloud Cover, Visibility, Pressure, Air Quality, Pollen, Daylight, and Moon Phase.

The Hourly Forecast section is a scrollable carousel, with the Bonus Info toggle in Settings adding another stat. “Automatic” will intelligently select “relevant info for you.” You can tap to browse the hourly breakdown in a sheet view.

Finally, there’s the Daily Forecast section that gives you an eight-day overview via a temperature-range layout, which is my favorite way to visualize this information. Again, this can be tapped to get a bottom sheet explorer.

I've lost count on how many weather apps on Android I've tried out over the years but since the Pixel Weather app became a thing I've tried far fewer and the few I have tried while many have been prettier to look at, I've always ended up coming back to the stock PW app. It never hurts to have options though and this one looks like a good one. Thanks for the mention, I hadn't heard of it before.

The Radar tab is up next, with Google Maps powering this view. From the three-dot overflow menu, you can customize the Animation Speed, Map Layers, Style, and Options.

Settings is where you can make all the aforementioned customizations, including changing which Forecast Data source you’re using. The usual players are here, like The Weather Channel and AccuWeather. I also like the stylized in-app changelog.

In Notifications, you can have a persistent silent alert appear. There’s also the ability to receive Morning and Evening Reports, along with Precipitation and Severe Alerts. This is joined by a trio of homescreen widgets.

#10394. How to Test Android Apps on Windows: A Practical Guide to Emulators and APK Testing

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most Android bugs are not exotic. They are a button hidden behind a keyboard, a screen that breaks on a smaller display, a permission prompt nobody tested, or an install that fails on an older version of the system. You can catch many of these problems without owning a single test phone, as long as you set up your Windows PC properly and test with a plan.

This guide covers how to prepare Windows, how to choose and configure an emulator, how to install and verify APK files with Android Debug Bridge (ADB), what a good test session checks, and where virtual devices stop being trustworthy. It includes a small Python helper that explains install failures and reads launch times from ADB output, with tests. Tool versions and menu names change, so confirm details against each vendor’s current documentation.

● Emulators find the large, repeatable problems fast. Use them for functional checks, layouts and install testing, and confirm hardware behavior on real devices.

● Prepare Windows first. Enable hardware virtualization and the Windows Hypervisor Platform, and check the result with the emulator’s own acceleration check.

● Match the virtual device to real users. Test the oldest and newest Android versions you support, a small screen and a modest memory profile.

● Learn ADB. It makes installs, data resets and evidence capture scriptable and repeatable.

● Do not trust emulator performance numbers. Your PC is usually faster than your users’ phones.

Testing on a desktop gives you speed and control that physical phones rarely match. A fresh install takes seconds. You can wipe app data, change the screen size, switch the Android version and try again without hunting for a charger. When a tester finds a bug, they can record the screen, copy the log and attach both to a report from the same machine.

It also opens testing to people who are not developers. Product managers, support staff, designers and content reviewers can open a build, follow a script and report what they see with a full-size screen, a keyboard and a mouse. That alone shortens the feedback loop on most teams.

The tradeoff is realism, which this guide returns to later. For now, the point is that an emulator is the fastest way to find the large, obvious, repeatable problems, which is exactly what you want to clear out before involving real devices.

There are two broad families of tools, and they suit different work.

The first is the official Android Emulator that comes with Android Studio. It simulates hardware in detail, supports a wide range of device profiles and connects directly to debugging and profiling tools. It is the right choice when you are tracing a crash, profiling memory or testing against a specific system image. It is also heavier to install and configure.

The second family is general-purpose emulators built for speed and convenience. A good android emulator such as LDPlayer installs quickly, runs on ordinary hardware, can open several instances side by side, and makes it easy to drop in an APK, map keyboard controls and record the screen. Other examples include BlueStacks and Genymotion, the latter aimed at developers and test automation. Many of these tools are designed first for gaming or for general app use, so check whether they expose the Android versions, system images and debugging features your testing needs.

Many teams use both: the official tools for debugging, and a lighter emulator for broad functional testing. When you compare options, ask a few practical questions:

Preparing Windows Before You Install Anything

Ten minutes of preparation prevents most early frustration.

Turn on hardware virtualization. Emulators run dramatically faster when virtualization is enabled in your BIOS or UEFI. The setting is usually called Intel Virtualization Technology (VT-x) or SVM Mode on AMD systems. If your emulator feels slow for no clear reason, check this first.

Enable the Windows Hypervisor Platform. The Android documentation recommends the Windows Hypervisor Platform (WHPX) for Windows acceleration, and notes that you can turn it on under “Turn Windows features on or off”, then restart. Its older Android Emulator hypervisor driver is being phased out, so check the current emulator acceleration guide for your setup. Also note that a hardware-accelerated emulator cannot run inside another virtual machine.

Verify it. The emulator includes a check that reports whether acceleration is usable:

Update your graphics drivers. Android interfaces rely on graphics acceleration. Outdated drivers cause black screens, flickering and random crashes that testers wrongly blame on the app.

Check memory and storage. As a rule of thumb, 8 GB of RAM is workable for a single virtual device and 16 GB is more comfortable for several. Leave several gigabytes of free disk space, because every virtual device stores its own system image and data.

Close heavy background programs. Browsers with dozens of tabs and other virtual machines compete for the same resources and make performance results misleading.

The default configuration is rarely the one your audience uses. Spend a few minutes matching it to reality.

Pick a screen size and density similar to a popular mid-range phone rather than a very large display, because layout problems tend to appear on smaller screens first. Assign a sensible number of CPU cores and memory, enough to be smooth but not so much that you hide performance problems your users on modest phones will feel.

Choose the Android version carefully. If your app supports several versions, test at least the oldest and the newest. Behavior changes significantly between releases, especially around permissions, notifications, background activity and gesture navigation. For example, Android 13 (API level 33) introduced a runtime notification permission, so a flow that works on an older version can silently differ on a newer one.

With the Android Emulator, you can list and start saved devices from the command line, which helps when you keep several profiles:

Finally, confirm the network works by loading a web page in the virtual browser. A surprising number of reported app bugs turn out to be a virtual device with no connection.

An APK is the package file Android uses to install an app. Installing one on a virtual device is easy, but doing it carefully makes your results more reliable.

Use trusted files only. Take builds from your own team, your continuous integration system or the developer’s official release page. An APK from a random download site can be modified or carry malware, and testing it proves nothing about the real product.

Install it. Most emulators accept a dragged APK file or have an install option. If you use the command line, Android Debug Bridge (ADB) installs a package with a single command and is the better route when you want to automate repeated installs. These are the commands you will use most:

adb devices                          # confirm the emulator is connected

adb install -r app-debug.apk         # install, replacing an existing copy

adb shell pm clear com.example.app   # wipe app data for a fresh start

adb shell am start -W -n com.example.app/.MainActivity   # launch and print timing

adb logcat -d > session.log          # save the log for a bug report

adb exec-out screencap -p > shot.png # take a screenshot

Know the common install failures. If the install fails, the message usually explains why. A package built for a newer Android version than your device runs is rejected, a build compiled only for a different processor architecture may not install or may crash on launch, and an update signed with a different key than the installed version is refused, in which case you uninstall the old version first. The helper below turns raw ADB output into a one-line explanation and also reads the launch time that am start -W prints:

"""Small helpers for reading adb output during APK testing."""

"INSTALL_FAILED_OLDER_SDK": "The app needs a newer Android version than this device runs.",

"INSTALL_FAILED_NO_MATCHING_ABIS": "The app has no native code for this device's CPU architecture.",

"INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE": "Signed with a different key than the installed copy. Uninstall first.",

"INSTALL_FAILED_VERSION_DOWNGRADE": "The installed copy has a higher version code. Uninstall first.",

"INSTALL_FAILED_INSUFFICIENT_STORAGE": "The virtual device is out of storage. Wipe data or enlarge the disk.",

def explain_install_failure(output: str) -> str:

"""Turn raw adb install output into a one-line explanation."""

m = re.search(r"(INSTALL_[A-Z_]+)", output)

return "Unknown failure. Read the full adb message."

return f"{code}: " + INSTALL_HINTS.get(code, "No hint recorded for this code.")

"""Read TotalTime from adb shell am start -W output, or None if the launch failed."""

m = re.search(r"TotalTime:\s*(\d+)", output)

The tests feed it realistic ADB output, including a successful install, three common failures, an unknown code, a successful launch and a failed one:

from adbtools import explain_install_failure, launch_time_ms

assert explain_install_failure("Performing Streamed Install\nSuccess") == "Installed."

older = "adb: failed to install app.apk: Failure [INSTALL_FAILED_OLDER_SDK]"

assert "newer Android" in explain_install_failure(older)

abi = "Failure [INSTALL_FAILED_NO_MATCHING_ABIS: Failed to extract native libraries, res=-113]"

assert "architecture" in explain_install_failure(abi)

sig = "Failure [INSTALL_FAILED_UPDATE_INCOMPATIBLE: Package com.example signatures do not match]"

assert "Uninstall first" in explain_install_failure(sig)

assert explain_install_failure("Failure [INSTALL_FAILED_SOMETHING_NEW]").endswith("No hint recorded for this code.")

assert explain_install_failure("error: device offline").startswith("Unknown failure")

good = "Starting: Intent { cmp=com.example/.MainActivity }\nStatus: ok\nActivity: com.example/.MainActivity\nTotalTime: 523\nWaitTime: 530\nComplete"

assert launch_time_ms("Error type 3\nError: Activity class does not exist.") is None

Understand split packages. Many modern apps ship as bundles made of several files instead of one APK. If you are given a bundle format, you may need a tool that installs all the parts together, or a universal APK generated for testing.

Test the right build. Debug builds and release builds do not always behave the same. Release builds often apply code shrinking and optimization that can break features that worked in debug. Before sign-off, run your main flows on a release build, not only the one developers use daily.

Wandering through an app and tapping things will find some problems. A structured pass finds far more. Work through these areas on each build.

Capture evidence as you go. A short screen recording with the exact steps, plus the saved log, is worth far more to a developer than a paragraph of description.

A very common mistake is testing everything on one virtual device and assuming the result applies everywhere. Android is a fragmented platform, and your audience is spread across many versions, screen sizes and memory limits.

Create a small set of profiles and rotate through them. A low-memory profile reveals how the app behaves on budget hardware. A small screen exposes cramped layouts. An older Android version shows compatibility gaps. A different language and region setting reveals text-length problems, date and number formatting issues and right-to-left layout errors.

Because tools such as the Android Emulator and several third-party emulators let you keep multiple saved devices, you can keep these profiles ready and compare behavior across them. This is where testing on Windows becomes efficient, turning a long list of devices you would have to buy into a handful of saved configurations.

Being honest about the limits protects you from false confidence.

● Hardware features. Camera quality, fingerprint sensors, Bluetooth accessories, NFC, precise GPS behavior and motion sensors are hard to reproduce faithfully. You can simulate basic values, but real hardware behaves differently.

● Real performance. Speed on an emulator depends on your PC. A strong computer can make an app feel faster than it will on a typical phone, so never use emulator results as proof of smooth performance for users.

● Manufacturer customizations. Phone makers modify Android with their own battery management, notification handling and interface layers. An app can work on a clean system and fail on one brand’s modified version, particularly around background tasks and push notifications.

● Real networks. A home network is stable. Mobile networks have weak signal, handoffs between towers and restrictions that an emulator will not recreate.

● Apps that block virtual devices. Some apps, particularly in banking, security and competitive gaming, detect virtual environments and refuse to run. That is a deliberate choice by the developer, and testers should respect it instead of attempting to bypass it.

The sensible approach is to let emulators handle the broad middle of your testing, then confirm anything involving hardware, payments or notifications on a few real devices before release. The script above also only interprets a handful of ADB messages, so it is a convenience for triage, not a replacement for reading the full output.

If you want something simple to adopt, use this sequence on every build.

Start with a clean virtual device matching a common phone.

Install the new build and run a quick smoke test, a fast pass over the main features to confirm nothing is fundamentally broken.

Test the specific areas that changed in this version in more depth.

Repeat the most important flows on a second profile with a different Android version and screen size.

Log every problem with exact steps, the build number, the device profile and a screenshot or recording.

Before release, verify camera, location, notifications and payments on at least one physical phone.

Write this down as a short checklist and share it. Consistent steps make results comparable between testers and between builds, which is how you notice regressions instead of rediscovering the same bugs.

Do I need Android Studio to test an APK on Windows? Not necessarily. Many emulators accept an APK directly, and ADB can be installed on its own as part of the Android platform tools. Android Studio becomes useful when you need to debug or profile.

Why is my emulator slow? The usual causes are virtualization or the Windows Hypervisor Platform being off, outdated graphics drivers, too little memory and heavy background programs. Run the acceleration check first.

Can an emulator replace a real test phone? No. It covers the broad middle of testing, but hardware, true performance, manufacturer behavior and real networks need a few physical devices.

Is it safe to install APKs from the internet? Only from trusted sources, such as your own team, your build system or an official release page. Files from random sites can be modified or carry malware.

Testing Android apps on Windows is practical, affordable and flexible when you treat the emulator as a tool with a clear purpose. It gives you fast installs, repeatable conditions and easy access to many device profiles, which makes it well suited to functional checks, layout reviews and APK testing. It does not replace real hardware for sensors, true performance and manufacturer-specific behavior, so keep a few physical devices for the final confirmation.

Prepare your PC, choose a tool that fits your workflow, configure devices that resemble real users, follow a consistent checklist and vary your environments. Do that consistently and you will catch most problems long before they reach your users.

#10390. ‘Old’ Galaxy phones are a better deal; here’s what you should buy

Old is a label, and saying last year’s Galaxy flagships from Samsung are dated or worthless is disingenuous. You can get a killer deal on a phone a generation or two old.

Beyond that, it still has a flagship SoC in the Snapdragon 8 Elite – the same 3nm foundation as the Snapdragon 8 Elite Gen 5. It also takes on much of the same design, so if you like the rounded corners of the Galaxy S26 Ultra, you’ll be missing out on almost nothing by choosing the Galaxy S25 Ultra for a better deal.

Right now, a 1TB model Galaxy S25 Ultra can be had for $780 on Amazon. It’s a “renewed” model, but that’s an excellent choice over a used device, since Samsung isn’t making them anymore. That’s down from the original $1,659 price tag when it launched. It’s also a good bit cheaper than the best deal you can get on an S26 Ultra – $1,049 for 256GB model from Samsung.

You can get a slightly better deal on something like the Galaxy S24 Ultra, though it might not have the same upsides as the S25 Ultra. There are a couple of reasons for that.

First, it’s already in its second year of a seven-year update policy. In effect, you’re only getting five years of updates. Second, it doesn’t have the newest design language, if that’s something important to you.

On the flip side, you might prefer the boxier look Samsung used to run by on its devices.

The Galaxy S24 Ultra deal on Amazon that sits it at $591 right now also applies to the 512GB model, unlike the deal for a 1TB variant above. Still, it’s a solid deal for a phone that really isn’t that old, and it’s close to what the mid-range Galaxy A57 sells for, new.

It’ll still handle everyday tasks with next to no issues, and that makes it a great gift – possibly overkill for a first phone, to be frank.

The Galaxy S25 Plus is closer to a “first phone” than an Ultra model would be. It has excellent specs, but it only misses out on some of the raw power Samsung’s flagships have and an S Pen.

During Black Friday deal week on Amazon, it looks like an unlocked Galaxy S25 Plus goes for $683. It’s higher than the previous year’s Ultra, but it isn’t as expensive as the renewed Galaxy S25 Ultra. It has the same design language as Samsung’s new phones, and it still has six of the seven years left in its update lifetime.

It certainly beats paying over $900 for the new one, even with a deal.

This is also a good opportunity to get a foldable, if you’ve been eyeing the Galaxy Z Fold lineup. The Galaxy Z Fold 8 series is fantastic, and it blows other devices out of the water. But it’s pricey.

Instead of paying $1,499 for a Galaxy Z Fold 8 right now, even though that’s a great deal, you can get a 512GB Galaxy Z Fold 7 for $867 on Amazon. It’ll be sold under Amazon’s “Renewed” program, so it’ll be covered under some type of warranty.

Doing that is also a great way to get set up for a trade-in deal when the next set of Galaxy Z Fold 9 devices roll out.

Fun fact: Samsung is selling a refurbished Galaxy Z Fold 7 for the exact same price as the Galaxy Z Fold 8 while the new one is on sale. Obviously, the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is the better choice on Samsung’s website.

If you’re absolutely set on getting a Galaxy phone that came out in the last year, Samsung’s deals are better than anywhere else. When Samsung runs deals like it has right now, that’s the best time to buy a device. In any other case, it can be a hard bargain.

The Galaxy S26 Ultra is always a good choice, but it’s going to be $999 without a trade-in. That said, trade-in values are boosted right now. You can get $600 for something like your Galaxy S24 Ultra, but you can’t stack discounts. Trading a Galaxy S24 Ultra in brings the Galaxy S26 Ultra down to $799. Still a good deal.

Samsung’s devices offer the latest Android updates and features, even if they’re a couple of years old. Some of the Galaxy phones we knew as the “best” last year are affordable options right now.

#10389. This could mess with Thanksgiving, says VMware as it drops third critical patch bundle in a week

Four more critical vulns, including another unauthorized access flaw in vCenter

VMware has issued its third critical security advisory in a week, this time to warn of four critical flaws and address a total of 23 issues.

VMSA-2026-0011 landed on November 24, just five days after two other advisories. VMware’s FAQ for the new advisory includes the following remarks:

Because of coordinated disclosure and release pipeline timing this VMSA comes on the heels of VMSA-2026-0009, and atop a major holiday in the United States.The security vulnerability landscape is more dynamic than ever; please know that while we acknowledge this timing is not optimal, your ability to operate and maintain a secure environment amidst modern, AI-assisted threats is paramount to us.

Make of that what you will as you ponder how and when to patch the four critical flaws, including the 9.8-rated CVE-2026-123903, which allows a malicious actor to bypass authentication and gain unauthorized access to vCenter, the management tool VMware users rely on to manage hosts and VMs.

This is the third 9.8-rated authentication issue VMware has fixed in vCenter since July.

Two of the other critical flaws in this new advisory allow an attacker to run code on an ESX host, earning the bugs a 9.8 rating. CVE-2026-123910 makes that possible thanks to a heap out-of-bounds write vulnerability in the I/O filter service, while CVE-2026-123911 is a use-after-free vulnerability.

The other critical flaw, CVE-2026-123912, can result in a guest escape from VMware’s desktop Workstation and Fusion desktop hypervisors. It’s also present in VMware’s ESX hypervisor, but attackers thankfully can’t escape that product’s sandbox. This one’s easy to mitigate because the problem is in a USB controller that users can turn off.

Most of the other flaws require installation of updated software.

There’s a little good news in that some of the software updates VMware issued last week include fixes for the newly disclosed flaws. But there are still plenty of fresh fixes to apply, across ten products: ESX, vCenter, Workstation, Fusion, VMware tools, Cloud Foundation, vSphere Foundation, the Telco Cloud Platform, Telco Cloud Infrastructure, and the VMware Avi Load Balancer.

VMware’s advisory thanks external researchers for finding and reporting most of the flaws. The Broadcom business unit is known to be a member of Project Glasswing, the Anthropic-run effort to use the Mythos model to find flaws that has seen many vendors dramatically increase the number of patches they produce.

In past years, VMware has often published considerably more than this year’s 11 security advisories. With five weeks left in the year, perhaps vAdmins need to worry Christmas might also get complicated. ®
