#10388. Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

Long-context training — including pretraining and post-training, has become a foundation of frontier AI models. Longer native sequence lengths give large language models (LLMs) the working memory to read a whole code repository or ingest hours of video, and reason through multi-step agentic tasks. In three years, context windows have grown from 128K–200K tokens to 1M–2M, and models trained toward 10M tokens are arriving. Training at those lengths, not just serving them, is where the system limits show up.

In particular, efficient training at multi-million-token sequence lengths runs into the following bottlenecks:

Today, we are excited to announce long-context training support in MaxText, Google Cloud's open-source JAX training framework. MaxText enables this by supporting four context-parallel strategies on Cloud TPU: All-gather, A2A (DeepSpeed Ulysses), P2P (Ring Attention), and the A2A + P2P hybrid (Unified Sequence Parallelism, or USP), combined with load balancing and sequence packing. All four use the Tokamax Splash Attention kernel. Combined with the 3D inter-chip interconnect (ICI) ( supported on TPUs such as TPU 5p or TPU 7 (Ironwood) ), this lets one sequence span an entire slice while communication overlaps compute and memory per chip stays flat. In our benchmarks, Llama 3.1 8B (25 head dim, 16 query heads) demonstrates exceptional long-context efficiency, achieving 70% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) on TPU 5p for sequence lengths up to 4 million tokens. Across both TPU 5p and TPU 7, context can be seamlessly extended to 14 million tokens—with TPU 7 consistently maintaining 50% MFU across all sequence scales below 6 million tokens.

This functionality is available today in MaxText v0.2.4(doc).

Context parallelism splits a long sequence across chips, so no chip holds the activations of the whole sequence. Context parallelism can be combined with data and tensor parallelism, and the smallest context-parallel size that fits your sequence in HBM is usually the right one. This also holds for sparser attention variants which cut attention compute but not the activations of every other layer.

Attention is the one global operation where every token needs the keys and values of every preceding token, so each context parallel strategy has to bring those keys and values back together. Each makes a different tradeoff between memory (how much KV a chip holds), communication (how much moves per layer, and whether it overlaps compute), and model shape (how many attention heads to split).

All-gather: Each chip keeps its chunk of the sequence and, at every layer, gathers the keys and values from every other chip. It is the simplest option. However, because causal masking causes later tokens to attend to larger prefixes, All-gather requires balancing compute load across devices.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

All-to-All (Ulysses): Gathering keys and values makes every chip hold the whole sequence, so attention memory never shrinks no matter how many chips you add. All-to-All changes what each chip owns instead. Attention is independent across heads but not across tokens, so the head axis is free to shard and the token axis is not. An All-to-All before attention rotates the sharding from one onto the other: each chip gives up the heads it is not responsible for and receives the tokens it was missing for the heads it keeps, ending up with the full sequence for a few heads instead of a few tokens for all heads. This is also why Ulysses does not need token load balancing. Attention then runs as ordinary single-chip attention with standard kernels, and a second All-to-All rotates the output back. KV per chip shrinks by the context-parallel factor, but you cannot split across more chips than the model has heads — and tensor parallelism shards that same dimension, so TP × CP is capped by the head count (under GQA, by the KV heads: eight for Llama 3 8B). The all-to-all itself, though, does not overlap with compute — attention cannot start on a partial sequence, so every chip waits for data from every other chip before any of them begins.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

P2P (ring attention): Ulysses' ceiling is the head count; ring attention removes it by never leaving the token axis. Keys and values never materialize in full. KV chunks travel around a ring of chips one hop at a time, neighbor to neighbor. Each chip attends its queries to the chunk it holds while the next one is in flight, and merges the partial results as it goes. A chip holds its own chunk and one visiting chunk, never the full sequence, and the communication overlaps compute. However, dividing attention computation into smaller chunks can reduce local kernel efficiency, and unlike All-to-All Ulysses, ring attention requires explicit load balancing.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

A2A + P2P (USP): A2A + P2P (USP): Neither strategy scales on its own — All-to-All stops at the head count, and a long ring accumulates hops while thinning the overlap that makes it work. Unified sequence parallelism composes them on two axes of the chip mesh, so the context-parallel degree becomes the product of the two: All-to-All along one axis, sized at or below the KV-head count, and a ring along the other to extend past it. Because each All-to-All group has already pooled its tokens, a ring step now carries a chunk larger by the All-to-All factor, and the ring is correspondingly shorter — more compute per hop to hide the transfer behind, and fewer hops to accumulate latency and causal imbalance. On Cloud TPU the two map onto two axes of the slice's chip mesh.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

These strategies cover the attention variants in today's major models, from multi-head and grouped-query attention to DeepSeek-style multi-head latent attention.

Splitting the sequence only pays off if the attention compute on each chip stays efficient. All four strategies run on Splash Attention, the block-sparse flash attention kernel from Tokamax, Google's open-source library of TPU kernels. It skips fully masked blocks and handles packed sequences without padding. P2P calls it once per ring step, on the local queries and the KV chunk that has just arrived, while the transfer for the next step runs underneath, in the forward and backward pass alike. Each chip does the same fused, block-level work it would do without context parallelism, with the communication hidden behind it.

P2P (Ring Attention) is the primary workhorse for very long-context training, whereas All-Gather is the standard baseline for most other cases where the KV can fit. These are the primary CP strategies to use in practice. We compare how far each can push context length on a fixed 64-chip TPU 5p slice with Llama 3.1 8B (256 head dim, 16 query heads) , and measure their MFUs. In ideal settings where both strategies can have large enough chunks (S/CP) and local batch size - the two major variables driving MFU on TPU 5p, we observe that P2P can outperform All-Gather almost every context length from 64K to 14M tokens, where communication becomes mostly hidden with the Ring circular transport schedule.

TPU v7x (Ironwood) takes the same recipe further. With twice the HBM per chip, P2P training of 14M tokens on 64 TPU 7 chips reached 48.7% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) compared to 37.1% on TPU 5p. We observed that ring attention performs better than other context parallelism strategies -- e.g. Ulysses and USP –- on TPU 7 chips.

The choice of context parallelism strategy depends mostly on whether the gathered KV fits in HBM, and on how many KV heads the model has:

For all CP strategies, when you run low on memory: Offload before you recompute. MaxText can move saved activations, optimizer states, and parameters to host memory tensor by tensor. At long context lengths, these transfers are hidden behind compute, making offloading effectively free. Offload attention outputs first, followed by optimizer states, layer inputs, parameters, and finally remat as a last resort. MFU stays flat through the offload steps while all-gather falls off and eventually stops fitting. For more details, see the remat tuning guide.

Looking ahead, we see two natural extensions. The first is broader model coverage. Models that mix sliding-window and global layers, such as Gemma, would run the window layers with a short exchange between neighboring chips while the global layers use the ring. We also plan to support linear attention mechanisms such as Gated Delta Net (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). The second is performance, with further tuning of the Tokamax kernels and the P2P backward pass to narrow the remaining gap to all-gather at short context, and recipes by model and context length for TPU 5p and 7 chips.

Context parallelism is available today in MaxText v0.2.4 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Sample recipes are available here for TPU 7 and TPU 5p. Pick a strategy with context_parallel_strategy (ring, ulysses, or usp), set the context-parallel size, and keep the rest of your run the same. The sharding guide has the flags and constraints for each strategy, and the release notes link to the pull requests behind this work. We can't wait to see what you train.

#10387. 9to5Google’s Best of 2026

We reviewed dozens of products in 2026, and the one through line across all of them was cost. Because of RAMageddon, everything was more expensive, and it became somewhat easier to separate the products that were good because they were cheap from the ones that were good at any price.

This year was a lot of things, but it was primarily a reminder that the best products are created through careful iteration. Only one product on this list, the XREAL Aura, represents an emerging class. The rest, from the Fitbit Air to the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8, showcase years of work in optimizing for design, weight, battery life, and performance. Some take more risks than others, but to us they all represent the very best of their respective categories.

Price: $99.99/$129.99 (special editions)Black Friday deal: Save $30 on the Google Fitbit Air Champagne Gold & Obsidian Bundle. Ends 12/02.Release date: May 26, 2026

A screenless Google device outside of a smart speaker wasn’t something I thought I needed. Yet the Fitbit Air has become one of my favorite products to come out of Mountain View in years. Fitness tracking is something I want happening quietly in the background, not another reason to check a screen.

That’s what the Air gets right. It isn’t trying to reinvent fitness tracking; it packages it in a comfortable, lightweight band that stays out of the way. Health tracking data isn’t locked behind a paywall, and there’s no display demanding attention throughout the day.

Smart rings and full-featured watches have their place, but this slim wearable strikes a particularly appealing balance. It gives me useful information when I want it without becoming another device to manage. It’s comfortable, inexpensive, and easy to live with—and sometimes that’s exactly what makes a product great.

— Damien Wilde, Daniel Bader & Andrew Romero

Price: $2,499Black Friday price: $1,899Release date: November 1, 2026

I’m primarily a TV person, especially when it comes to gaming. But after spending time with XGIMI’s Horizon 30 Pro, I’ve been enjoying a very different setup. I loved the older Horizon Pro 4K and used it until this arrived; the 30 Pro feels like a substantial leap forward.

VRR support has made it possible to play my Steam Machine on a roughly 200-inch image in the bedroom, while the built-in speakers do an impressive job of filling the room. Going from Forza Horizon 5 at 120fps to Blue Planet on BBC iPlayer is effortless thanks to Google TV.

The swiveling base is more useful than it might sound. I can position the projector in the corner of the room and let XGIMI’s keystone correction handle the rest. It’s an expensive upgrade, but for a home cinema setup that doubles as a giant gaming display, it’s a beast.

I still wish the Steam Controller cost less. Beyond that, though, I have very little to complain about: this is the best controller I’ve used in years. It’s comfortable, gives Windows users a way to control their PC, and brings together some of the best ideas from my beloved Steam Deck.

Steam Input is the real selling point. The depth of customization makes this controller feel at home on a PC in a way few alternatives do. I haven’t connected with a new controller this quickly in a long time.

That doesn’t mean everyone needs one. Most people would be well served by an 8BitDo controller—or even a Stadia controller converted to Bluetooth. But if you play on PC and want the premium option, this is my pick.

Price: $1,899.99Black Friday price: $1,549.99Release date: August 7, 2026

The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is my favorite foldable of 2026. I’ve been enthralled with its design since reviewing it in July, and it won’t be leaving my daily rotation anytime soon.

The short, compact outer screen is refreshing to use, but it’s the inner display that changed the way I think about foldables. Its proportions feel more flexible than the square-shaped displays that have become so common across the category. Samsung’s motivations for adopting this form factor may be obvious, but the result works—and that’s what matters when I’m using it every day.

— Ben Schoon, Daniel Bader & Andrew Romero

Price: Ear (3): $179 / Ear (3a): $99Black Friday price: Ear (3): $94 / Ear (3a): $69Release date: Ear (3): Sept 18, 2025 / Ear (3a): July 7, 2026

Ear (3a) earns the nod here, but this is really a celebration of Nothing’s entire audio lineup. Its clean sound profiles, thoughtful designs, and compatibility with Android and the other devices I use have kept me listening all year.

Ear (3) remains my primary pair of earbuds, while Ear (3a) gets plenty of use when pocket space is at a premium. Ear (open) and Nothing Headphone (1) Pro round out the rest of my listening needs.

I still stand by calling Ear (3a) simply ‘the best earbuds.’ More broadly, Nothing has built an audio lineup worth paying attention to, especially when end-of-year discounts make it easier to try.

Price: ~$1,999Release date: April 21, 2026 (China) / Nov 2026 (Global)

If there’s one phone from 2026 I wish I could return to, it’s the Oppo Find X9 Ultra. ColorOS has quietly become one of the best Android experiences by getting the small details right, and the Ultra’s camera hardware and shooting experience remain standouts—even next to Oppo’s newer Find X10 Pro Max.

This is one of those increasingly rare smartphones that feels unwilling to compromise. I’ll be thinking about it until there’s a proper successor, and the prospect of not getting one in 2027 is disappointing.

I wish Oppo had sold this phone officially in the US. Importing comes with real complications, but the Find X9 Ultra is one of the few phones good enough to make me consider it.

Price: ~$1,720Release date: March 13, 2026

It’s been a remarkable year for foldables, but the Honor Magic V6 is my favorite of the bunch. Its combination of durability, battery life, and camera quality finally made me stop thinking about going back to a conventional slab phone.

The cameras are a big part of that. I used to rely entirely on my Panasonic GH5 for work photos; now I often reach for the Magic V6 instead. Its large 1/2-inch telephoto sensor helps it deliver the kind of dependable results I want when I need to capture something quickly.

It’s also held up to far more punishment than I’d normally put a foldable through. After a mud torture test and a trip through the dishwasher, I’m still using it months later. Battery life has been another major strength.

Honor’s software still has rough edges, but the improvements are substantial, and the seven-year update promise matters. The remaining problem is availability: I’d love to see Honor make its phones officially accessible across North America.

Android XR has had an awkward start, but the XREAL Aura feels like the kind of product the platform needed from the beginning. It puts a fresh idea into a form factor that’s easier to imagine wearing than a bulky headset.

Think of it as smart glasses that behave more like a VR headset. Open the app drawer with a pinch—or use the tethered compute puck—and your chosen app appears as a floating window you can move around. It’s like having a tablet suspended wherever you need it.

That makes remote work and entertainment feel surprisingly natural, especially when the glasses weigh less than a fifth of many VR headsets. There are compromises: hand gestures can be finicky, and while Bluetooth game controllers work, there isn’t a Meta Quest-style motion controller available for immersive gaming.

Even so, Aura makes a compelling case for Android XR. It’s an appealing option for people interested in immersive entertainment who don’t want the weight and bulk of a conventional VR headset.

DroidDeck has changed the way I think about gaming on Android. It isn’t the first project to bring PC games to a phone, but it feels like one of the most capable. The pitch is straightforward, even if the technology isn’t: run Steam on your Android device.

Rather than emulating an entire PC in the traditional sense, DroidDeck brings together translation layers using projects such as FEX and Proton. The technical details are complicated, but the practical result is that your phone can run games it wasn’t originally designed to understand.

Performance across my devices has been impressive—good enough that I’ve seen games run better than on my Steam Deck. The project is also free, community-driven, and highly customizable. The trade-off is that support for brand-new hardware can take time.

If you’ve been on the fence about mobile gaming, pairing DroidDeck with a controller such as my favorite, the GameSir G8 Galileo, is a great way to see how far it’s come.

Price: $179.99 / $219.99 (special editions)Release date: October 27, 2026

The Fitbit Air is a good fitness tracker and, to my eye, an attractive bracelet. But years of wearing watches have trained me to expect the time on my wrist. Even toward the end of my time with the Air, I kept glancing down for a clock that wasn’t there.

The Fitbit Edge fixes that central complaint. Its always-on display reduces battery life to roughly two or three days, but that’s a trade-off I’m happy to make. The software’s Pixel Watch-inspired look also helps it feel like part of a more cohesive lineup. For me, a small screen makes a meaningful difference.

Price: $29.99Release date: November 11, 2026

We waited so long for a first-party Google tracker that the Find My Device network had time to become Find Hub. The Pixel Tag makes the wait worthwhile with a more integrated experience: everything is managed in the Find Hub app, rather than spread across separate software.

‘Notify when left behind’ was overdue, and ‘Ring this phone’ has worked well for me. These are practical features, but they’re also reminders of how much first-party hardware can do to improve the software around it.

I also like the pill-shaped design. Google didn’t simply copy the AirTag; it made something that feels at home in the Pixel family. It’s a small thing, but it’s pleasant to hold—and surprisingly satisfying to fidget with.

Price: $349.99Black Friday price: $314.99Release date: July 6, 2026

A dock needs to do more than look good, but Keychron’s Thunderbolt 5 dock makes a strong first impression on a desk. Its machined aluminum case fits neatly into my setup, and the usefulness goes well beyond the finish.

I switched between devices more often this year. Moving my work from a Windows PC to the Mac mini M6 left the PC on gaming duty, while the Googlebook launch gave me another computer to plug into the same monitors and workstation.

The Keychron dock makes those changes simple: I connect the USB-C input cable and let it handle the display and data connections. Its own power supply, four USB-A ports, three USB-C ports, SD card reader, and wired Ethernet connection give me the flexibility I need. It might be the most useful addition I’ve made to my desk all year.

I don’t think I’ll stop talking about the Boox Picco anytime soon. It’s a wonderfully simple little e-reader with a straightforward operating system and battery life that stretches across days or weeks, depending on use.

You can attach it magnetically to the back of your phone or slip it into almost any pocket. But the bigger benefit is how easy it makes picking up a book, whether I’m out of the house or just moving from one room to another.

At around $100, it’s an appealingly focused device. In a year when so much attention has gone to retro gadgets and handhelds, the Picco captures a similar pleasure: a small piece of technology that does one thing well.

Price: $499.99Black Friday price: $399.99Release date: October 14, 2026

If one gadget came out of nowhere to steal the show for me in 2026, it was the Alexa Tablet 12 Pro. Amazon rebooted its tablet lineup, dropped the Fire branding, and embraced a much more familiar version of Android. Install a third-party launcher to remove the permanent Alexa search bar, and it feels like a regular mid-range Android tablet—with full Play Store support, no sideloading required.

The hardware is where it really wins me over. The base configuration pairs 8GB of RAM with 128GB of storage, which is more than enough for browsing, movies, YouTube, and casual games. But the display is the standout: this is a much better LCD than I expect from a tablet under $1,000, and the nanomatte finish looks and feels fantastic.

I’m as surprised as anyone that Amazon finally figured out its tablet lineup. A little help from the person behind Microsoft’s Surface line seems to have gone a long way. At this price—and especially on sale—I struggle to recommend anything else, including Apple’s base iPad. It’s that good.

Price: $99Black Friday price: $84Release date: July 21, 2026

I have always been a reader, but in my effort to spend less time on my phone this year, I did my utmost to return to e-paper displays for most long-form reading.

The XTEINK X4 Pro is a tiny 4-inch touch-capable e-paper display with a backlight and plenty of battery life. Its magnets help the device stick to the back of most phones (though it’s a bit tall for some smaller devices like the Pixel 11 Pro and iPhone 18 Pro), and it supports custom firmware through the Crosspoint project.

While it occasionally feels cramped to read on, the sheer number of formatting and font options makes it easy to adjust the reading experience to suit your tastes. And because it supports KOReader, reading progress syncs with most other devices, including Kobo e-readers and supported phone apps.

#10383. Motorola Signature 27 hands-on: Pure elegance on display

It’s hard to believe we finally got a proper premium phone from Motorola again, and it feels a bit like the return of the Moto X.

Motorola’s slow regeneration in North America from a mostly budget device-minded company to one offering the most expensive non-folding flagship phone might become the thing of legend. That’s not to say the company hasn’t shown true greatness in the past — the Moto X, Moto Z, and Razr lines are proof of that — but it’s always shied away from pulling out all the stops until the Motorola Signature 27 arrived.

Freshly unboxed and sitting in my hands, the textile-clad Capulet Olive Motorola Signature 27 colorway has impressed me in every way, shape, and form over the past 24 hours. It’s a much longer period of time than I usually get with hands-on opportunities and, yet, the newness of the experience has yet to wear off.

Part and parcel to that experience is the design. For whatever reason, this is the year Motorola seems to have truly figured out what makes a phone nice to hold. The textile back, ribbed metal frame, rounded corners, and curved edges on the front and back of the phone are purely blissful to hold. It’s a reminder that a phone can be as good looking as it feels instead of being a flat, boring, off-balance slab like too many phones are now.

The weight also surprised me. On paper this sits right in the middle of all the Ultra/Pro Max-named phones, yet it feels lighter for some intangible reason. The camera hump makes it slightly top-heavy, and it’s almost as wobble-free as a Pixel (there’s just one off-center camera hump).

The new 120Hz LTPO tandem OLED display is as top-notch as you’re going to get. I had all of maybe 2 hours of direct sunlight yesterday to test it with, and boy does it ever get bright. The rest of the time, I’ve been either inside or out in overcast weather, all of which it looked brilliant in. And it uses a combination of DC dimming above 20% brightness, and 2160Hz PWM dimming below 20% brightness, making it super comfortable for me.

Motorola’s software has evolved again with this release, and the verdict is still out on whether or not this is actually better than the company’s Android 16 designs. The build of Android 17 that ships with this phone utilizes some major design changes we’ve only seen on a few random low-end Motorola phones, and I’m not sure I care for the gentle gradients on the quick toggle icons and some other system UI elements. It feels like a weirdly cheap effect that clashes with Material Design’s flatter motif.

Despite that, this is the most Pixelly (is that a word?) a Motorola phone has ever felt. Features like Rambler, Sign to Text, and Create My Widget are here and work impeccably well. Gemini Intelligence is also on the way, and while there’s no specific ETA for that at the moment, it’s exciting to know that the most advanced Android features — which are currently only available on a very small number of devices — are coming to this phone.

With the additional $200 bonus, that leaves the cost at a whopping $1060

This may also be the fastest phone I’ve used to date. Everything is unbelievably snappy. Tap an icon and the thing opens instantly. Animations are zippy, and the new lock screen and customization options feel cohesive. Plus, all the favorite Moto actions and gestures are still here, which I greatly appreciate.

I’ve got some lofty expectations for this camera hardware, too, and it’s because Motorola is talking up such a big game. DXOMark’s camera review rated it the 2nd-best smartphone camera in the world, just under the Huawei Pura 80 Ultra and above the iPhone 18 Pro. Some people don’t take much stock in DXOMark, but it’s still worth noting that they’ve praised the heck out of the camera and Motorola is also pushing this as a winner in a way they’ve never done before.

The hype for the Motorola Signature 27 certainly seems to be realized, and now only time will truly tell if it can live up to the lofty expectations Motorola’s $1,599 price has set for it. If nothing, it’s certainly off to a good start and has made an excellent first impression.

#10382. What to Check Before Adding a Tripo Model to a Website

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A 3D model can look convincing in a still image and still be awkward on a website. The real questions appear when someone rotates it, loads it on a typical connection, or views it on a narrow phone screen. AI generators have made the first draft of a model cheap, which shifts the work toward the part developers and designers already know: budgets, testing and fallbacks.

This article walks through a web-readiness review for an AI-generated model, using an openwork ceramic vessel generated from a single reference image with Tripo as the worked example. The checks apply equally to output from other generators. It includes a small Node.js script that audits a GLB file against a size budget, and the markup for a lightweight viewer with a static fallback. The model shown is one candidate, so it cannot prove browser speed, mobile behavior, hidden geometry or export compatibility. Those are checks to run on your own asset.

Traditional 3D work involves modeling, polygon layout, materials, scale and export. AI generation can shorten the blank-workspace stage. A beginner may discover the category through a search such as AI 3D model generator free, but an initial candidate is useful only if it survives review.

For this vessel, start with the negative space, meaning the openings between the ribs. A flattering front view can hide a blocked cavity, a thin wall, or two surfaces passing through each other. Generation moves the bottleneck from making a first shape to deciding whether that shape is usable.

Image-to-3D tools use a visual reference to create a model candidate, while text-to-3D routes start from a written prompt. Tripo offers both, and other hosted options include Meshy, Rodin by Hyper3D and 3D AI Studio, all of which list GLB among their export formats, while open-source models such as TripoSR and Stable Fast 3D let you run generation yourself. Features, pricing, licensing and output quality differ and change often, so check each vendor’s current documentation. The image route suits this example because the folds, uneven rim and openings already exist in the source.

Whichever tool you use, it can provide a candidate for inspection. It cannot decide whether the source is licensed, whether the unseen rear structure is believable, or whether the exported asset suits a particular viewer. Those judgments belong to the person publishing the page.

Give the object one job. In a portfolio, this vessel might show how its shell and openings relate. That is narrower than calling it printable, manufacturable or dimensionally accurate. Note what must remain recognizable, and provide a still image if the interactive view fails.

Use an image you own or have permission to reuse. Busy backgrounds can distract an image-based workflow, so isolate the object when possible. One photograph also leaves the back and underside unknown. Treat those areas as review risks, not as details confirmed by the source.

After generation, rotate beyond the familiar front. Check the rear, top, underside and sides. Trace each large opening. Look for merged ribs, intersecting surfaces or an unstable base. A wireframe view, when available, can expose crowded geometry. A single front-facing candidate cannot answer these hidden-angle questions, so check them on your own model.

Record the download size, polygon count and texture dimensions. Polygons are the small flat faces that form the shape, and web viewers draw them as triangles. This matters because generator defaults are often tuned for quality, not for the web. In the generation screenshot for this vessel, the viewer reports roughly 1.95 million faces and the settings show 4K texture quality. That is a fine starting point for inspection but a heavy asset for a phone on a mobile connection. Curved ribs may need many triangles, but the count should still fit your page budget.

glTF is the open format most web viewers expect, and GLB is its single-file form that can bundle geometry, textures and materials. The format does not guarantee good performance, so measure the package. The script below reads a GLB with the open-source glTF Transform library, counts triangles, lists texture sizes, and compares them with a budget. The budget values are placeholders; set your own from your target devices.

Run it with node audit.mjs model.glb after installing @gltf-transform/core. It exits with a non-zero status when the asset is over budget, so you can run it in CI. A short test builds two GLBs with known triangle counts, one small and one with 200,000 triangles, and checks that the first passes and the second fails with the right message:

When an asset fails, glTF Transform also has a command line. gltf-transform inspect model.glb reports what is inside a file, and gltf-transform optimize input.glb output.glb --compress draco --texture-compress webp applies geometry and texture compression. Compression and mesh simplification can introduce artifacts, especially around thin ribs and openings, so reopen the result and re-check the angles from Gate 3 instead of accepting a smaller file blindly. Also check that your viewer supports the compression you choose.

Load the asset in the intended viewer at desktop and phone widths. Check framing, touch rotation, keyboard access, scrolling and a slower connection. The Google-backed model-viewer web component is one low-code option, and libraries such as three.js give you more control at the cost of more code. The markup below shows the pieces that matter for a first release: a poster image that loads first, lazy loading so the heavy file waits until needed, a text alternative, and visible controls.

The alt text, a poster and touch-action="pan-y" (so a vertical swipe still scrolls the page on a phone) address the common failures. Check the current model-viewer documentation for attribute names and the recommended script include, since they can change between versions. Also consider the WCAG guidance on non-text content: provide a useful description, and offer instructions plus a static image for people who cannot operate the viewer.

A single front-facing candidate is enough to discuss the method, not to certify a finished web asset. Call it a prototype until the exported file passes the checks required by its actual destination.

The same review pattern can be adapted to an architectural ornament, exhibition object, packaging concept or game prop, but each destination adds its own geometry, scale and delivery requirements. A portfolio may accept an inferred rear surface if that limitation is clear. A sales page, printable file or measured product needs stronger references and technical validation.

The audit script counts triangles, texture sizes and file size only. It does not detect self-intersections, thin walls, flipped normals or licensing problems, and passing it does not make an asset good. The budget numbers are illustrative, and real limits depend on your audience’s devices and connections. Tool features and terms change, so verify them with the vendor before you rely on them, and treat any speed or quality claims on vendor pages as the vendor’s own.

Inspect the angle you would normally crop out. Confirm input rights, measure the download, and test touch, keyboard and a slow connection. Reread the surrounding copy, because claims of fabrication accuracy or production readiness need supporting evidence. For this vessel, “exploratory web model” is an honest label, and more demanding claims require better references and a specialist review.

Decide what visitors should learn or do before adding a 3D viewer. A modest prototype with a useful fallback can beat a dramatic asset that loads slowly or hides limitations.

Do I need to code to create a model with an AI generator? No code is required to generate an initial model candidate in Tripo or similar tools. Publishing it interactively is separate work and usually involves a viewer, hosting, responsive testing and enough HTML or JavaScript knowledge to diagnose problems.

When should I use image-to-3D instead of text-to-3D? Use image-to-3D when a rights-cleared visual should guide the form, and text-to-3D when the idea begins as words. Do not treat the two routes as interchangeable evidence.

Is a generated model ready to publish as soon as it exports? No. Inspect the shape from several angles, measure the delivery package, test the intended viewer and prepare a fallback. Higher-stakes uses may also need specialist geometry, scale, material and rights review.

How can a beginner keep web 3D manageable? Give one object one clear job. Set a file-size target, test desktop and phone widths, keep interaction simple, and retain a still image for readers who cannot load or operate the viewer.

#10376. The Motorola Signature 27’s haptics finally feel more like a Pixel, and it’s about time

Motorola has been content with mid-grade haptics for years, but the Motorola Signature 27’s haptics rightfully upgrade the experience.

Two days ago, I unboxed the Motorola Signature 27 and talked about my experience a bit in my Motorola Signature 27 hands-on. It’s as gorgeous as the pictures make it look, and the texture on the back of my “Capulet Olive” green colorway is the stuff of dreams. But the thing that sealed the new ultra-high-quality design for me wasn’t the comfortable curved edges or the textile back: it was the haptic motors.

Now, don’t get me wrong. The Motorola Signature 27’s build and design are probably my favorite of the year. I’ll need to use it for just a bit longer to be 100% sure, but the pebble-like aesthetic of the curved front, back, and edges feels tangibly amazing to hold and use. It reminds me a bit of the Galaxy S3’s design, just without the nasty, slimy plastic back. It’s curved just enough to feel amazing, but not enough to be an annoyance like some waterfall display phones were in the past.

But a haptic motor is completely intangible to you as the reader. You can’t see it in pictures, and even if I describe it, you may not fully understand why it makes such a difference. If you’ve been using Google Pixel phones for a long time, you’ve probably forgotten how good a quality haptic motor can feel, but let me remind you for a moment just how important such an invisible thing can be to completing the experience.

Typing on the Signature 27 is pure bliss because of it. Tapping each key feels more like the press of a physical keyboard key. Unlocking the phone emits a satisfying light punch to my thumb, signalling a successful read. When I send a reply on X (Twitter), the button tap and subsequent animation feel like the message is being sent through the phone onto the web. It’s wonderful, and it’s one of the reasons people love the Pixel experience so much.

Unfortunately, the Motorola Signature 27’s haptic responses aren’t applied to as many parts of the UI as Pixel phones do, and that’s where the real miss comes in. Adjusting the brightness or volume doesn’t feel like a physically rotating vertical dial the way it does on Pixel, and I’d love to see Motorola add this in an update. But at least the upgraded haptic engine is a huge step in the right direction for Motorola and a good way to make the Signature 27 feel like a proper flagship phone.

#10370. What Is BIO Tagging and How to Use It for Document AI Training Data

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

If you've worked with named entity recognition (NER) in text, you've probably seen BIO tagging before. It's the standard way to mark where entities start, continue, and end inside a sequence of tokens. What you might not know is that the same tagging scheme has become the backbone of document AI -- the field where machine learning models extract structured data from invoices, receipts, contracts, and other business documents.

This guide explains how BIO tagging works, why it matters for document understanding, and how to build BIO-tagged training data that actually produces accurate models. We'll walk through the tagging logic, show real examples on financial documents, cover common mistakes that silently hurt model performance, and look at tools that speed up the annotation process.

BIO stands for Beginning, Inside, Outside. It's a labeling scheme that tells a model which tokens belong to an entity, which tokens continue that entity, and which tokens are just background noise.

● B (Beginning) -- the first token of a named entity

● I (Inside) -- any token that continues the same entity

● O (Outside) -- any token that doesn't belong to any entity

Take a simple line from an invoice: "Invoice #INV-2024-0891". If you're labeling invoice numbers, the BIO tags look like this:

Token          Label ─────          ───── Invoice        O #              O INV-2024-0891  B-invoice_number

Now consider a multi-word entity like a vendor name: "Acme Corporation Ltd". All three words are part of the same entity, so the tagging becomes:

Token          Label ─────          ───── Acme           B-vendor_name Corporation    I-vendor_name Ltd            I-vendor_name

The B prefix marks where the entity starts. The I prefix marks every following token that belongs to the same entity. Everything else gets O. This three-label structure is simple, but it gives models enough information to learn entity boundaries precisely -- even when two entities of the same type appear next to each other.

Traditional NER works on plain text -- a flat sequence of words. Document AI is harder because pages have two dimensions. A word's meaning depends not just on what comes before and after it in reading order, but also on where it sits on the page. The number "$4,250.00" could be an invoice total, a line item price, or a tax amount depending on its position relative to headers and columns.

Models like LayoutLMv3, LiLT, and BROS solve this by combining text tokens with their bounding box coordinates. Each token gets three inputs: the word itself, its position on the page (x, y, width, height), and its BIO label during training. The model learns to associate spatial patterns with entity types.

BIO tagging fits this architecture perfectly because it operates at the token level. Every word on the page gets exactly one label, and the model learns from thousands of these labeled tokens which spatial and textual patterns correspond to which fields.

If you're coming from a background in natural language processing in Python, the concept is identical -- BIO tagging works the same way whether your tokens come from a sentence or a document page. The difference is that document AI adds positional data to each token.

BIO isn't the only tagging scheme. You'll encounter several variants:

● BIO (IOB2) -- B marks the first token, I marks continuation tokens, O marks non-entities. The most widely used scheme.

● BIOES -- adds E (End) for the last token of a multi-word entity and S (Single) for single-token entities. More explicit boundary marking.

● BILOU -- adds L (Last) and U (Unit). Functionally identical to BIOES with different letters.

● IOB1 -- the original scheme where B is only used when two entities of the same type are adjacent. Rarely used today.

For document AI, standard BIO (IOB2) is the safest choice. Here's why:

● LayoutLMv3 and most pre-trained document models expect BIO format out of the box

● The FUNSD and CORD benchmark datasets use BIO, so your data format matches the academic baselines

● BIOES adds more label types, which means you need more training data to learn each one reliably

● Converting from BIO to BIOES is trivial if you later need it, but the reverse can lose information

Unless your model architecture specifically requires BIOES or BILOU, stick with BIO.

Before you tag a single token, you need a clear label taxonomy -- the list of entity types your model will learn to recognize. A poorly designed taxonomy creates confusion during annotation and cascades into model errors.

Ask yourself: what fields does the downstream system need? For invoice processing, a typical taxonomy looks like this:

vendor_name vendor_address invoice_number invoice_date due_date line_item_description line_item_quantity line_item_price subtotal tax total payment_method currency

**Rule 1 -- One label per semantic meaning. **Don't reuse amount for both line item prices and the invoice total. A model that learns "amount" means two different things depending on context will confuse them on novel layouts.

**Rule 2 -- Use hierarchical names. **Once your taxonomy exceeds 10 labels, flat names start colliding. line_item_price is better than price because it's self-documenting and prevents collisions with total_price.

**Rule 3 -- Include a catch-all label. **Call it other or O. Text that doesn't belong to any entity still needs a label for token classification. Without negative examples, the model can't learn what isn't a field.

**Rule 4 -- Match your model architecture. **Token classification models like LayoutLMv3 work best with fine-grained, word-level labels. Generative models like Donut output structured text, so they work with coarser labels. Choose your model first, then design labels to match.

Here's the practical workflow for turning raw PDF documents into BIO-tagged training data ready for model training.

Your model needs both the text and its coordinates on the page. For digitally generated PDFs, extract the text layer directly:

import pdfplumber def extract_words(pdf_path):     words = []     with pdfplumber.open(pdf_path) as pdf:         for page in pdf.pages:             for word in page.extract_words():                 words.append({                     'text': word['text'],                     'bbox': [word['x0'], word['top'],                              word['x1'], word['bottom']],                     'page': page.page_number                 })     return words

For scanned documents, you'll need OCR. Tesseract is the standard open-source option:

import pytesseract from pdf2image import convert_from_path def ocr_pdf(pdf_path, dpi=300):     images = convert_from_path(pdf_path, dpi=dpi)     all_words = []     for i, img in enumerate(images):         data = pytesseract.image_to_data(             img, output_type=pytesseract.Output.DICT         )         for j in range(len(data['text'])):             if data['text'][j].strip():                 all_words.append({                     'text': data['text'][j],                     'bbox': [data['left'][j], data['top'][j],                              data['left'][j] + data['width'][j],                              data['top'][j] + data['height'][j]],                     'page': i + 1                 })     return all_words

Models expect coordinates normalized to a 0--1000 range. This makes the model resolution-independent -- a word at the top-left corner is at (0, 0) whether the page is 72 DPI or 300 DPI.

def normalize_bbox(bbox, page_width, page_height):     return [         int(1000 bbox[0] / page_width), *        int(1000 *bbox[1] / page_height),         int(1000 bbox[2] / page_width), *        int(1000 *bbox[3] / page_height)     ]

If you've worked with bounding box annotation for document AI, you'll recognize this normalization step -- it's the same coordinate transformation used across all document understanding models.

This is the annotation step. For each word in the document, assign the appropriate BIO label based on what field it represents. Here's what a fully labeled invoice line looks like:

words = [     {'text': 'Invoice',      'label': 'O'},     {'text': 'Date:',        'label': 'O'},     {'text': 'March',        'label': 'B-invoice_date'},     {'text': '15,',          'label': 'I-invoice_date'},     {'text': '2024',         'label': 'I-invoice_date'},     {'text': 'Total:',       'label': 'O'},     {'text': '$4,250.00',    'label': 'B-total'},     {'text': 'Acme',         'label': 'B-vendor_name'},     {'text': 'Corporation',  'label': 'I-vendor_name'}, ]

Notice that "March 15, 2024" is a single entity spanning three tokens. The first token gets B-invoice_date, and the following tokens get I-invoice_date. The model learns that these tokens form one continuous entity.

LayoutLMv3 expects training data in a specific JSON structure. Each document becomes one training example with parallel arrays for words, bounding boxes, and labels:

{   "id": "invoice_001",   "words": ["Invoice", "Date:", "March", "15,", "2024",              "Total:", "$4,250.00", "Acme", "Corporation"],   "bboxes": [[84, 42, 145, 58], [84, 62, 120, 78],              [130, 62, 172, 78], [175, 62, 195, 78],              [198, 62, 230, 78], [84, 320, 125, 336],              [130, 320, 210, 336], [84, 22, 125, 38],              [130, 22, 210, 38]],   "labels": ["O", "O", "B-invoice_date", "I-invoice_date",              "I-invoice_date", "O", "B-total",              "B-vendor_name", "I-vendor_name"] }

If you're working with large volumes of documents, this guide on building JSON training datasets from PDFs covers how to automate much of this conversion pipeline.

Manually labeling every word on every page is slow. A single invoice might have 200-400 tokens, and you need hundreds of labeled documents for a usable model. Here are three approaches to speed things up.

Many financial fields follow predictable patterns. Dates, currency amounts, and invoice numbers can be pre-labeled with regex before a human annotator reviews them:

import re PATTERNS = {     'invoice_date': [         r'\b\d{1,2}[/-]\d{1,2}[/-]\d{2,4}\b',         r'\b(?:Jan|Feb|Mar|Apr|May|Jun|Jul|Aug|Sep|Oct|Nov|Dec)\w*\s+\d{1,2},?\s+\d{4}\b'     ],     'total': [         r'$[\d,]+.\d{2}\b'     ],     'invoice_number': [         r'\b(?:INV|inv)[- ]?\d{4,}\b'     ] } def pre_label(words):     for word in words:         word['label'] = 'O'  # default         for label, patterns in PATTERNS.items():             for pattern in patterns:                 if re.match(pattern, word['text']):                     word['label'] = f'B-{label}'                     break     return words

This won't catch everything -- vendor names, addresses, and line item descriptions need human judgment. But it can pre-fill 30-40% of the labels on a typical invoice, turning the annotator's job from labeling to reviewing.

Train a preliminary model on your first 50 labeled documents, then use it to predict labels on new documents. The annotator reviews and corrects the predictions instead of labeling from scratch. As the model improves, corrections become rarer and annotation speed increases.

The key is selecting documents where the model is least confident for the next round of human review. This focuses annotator time where it has the most impact on model accuracy.

If you're working with standard financial documents and want to skip the manual pipeline entirely, platforms like AI Asset Management can auto-generate structured labels from raw PDFs -- typically returning results in under 30 seconds per document. The annotator's role shifts from labeling to quality review, which is significantly faster than tagging every token by hand.

These are the errors that show up most often when teams start building document AI training data. Each one silently degrades model performance.

If "Acme Corporation" is labeled as one entity on page 1 but "Acme" and "Corporation" are labeled as separate entities on page 5, the model receives contradictory training signals. Pick a convention and enforce it across your entire dataset. For vendor names, include the full legal name ("Acme Corporation Ltd") every time.

A common beginner mistake is labeling every token of a multi-word entity with B instead of using B for the first token and I for the rest. "March 15, 2024" should be B-date, I-date, I-date -- not B-date, B-date, B-date. The B-B-B pattern tells the model these are three separate entities, which is wrong.

The word "Total:" is a header, not a value. Only the actual value ($4,250.00) should be tagged as B-total. Labeling "Total:" as part of the entity teaches the model to include headers in its extractions, which corrupts downstream processing.

Some OCR engines split punctuation into separate tokens. "$4,250.00" might become ["$", "4,250", ".", "00"]. If you label only "4,250" and skip the dollar sign and cents, the model learns incomplete extraction patterns. Make sure every token in the entity is covered by B or I tags.

If 95% of your tokens are labeled O and only 0.5% are B-invoice_number, the model will learn that predicting O is almost always correct. This is the class imbalance problem. It's normal for O to dominate, but watch for entity types that appear in fewer than 1% of tokens -- those need more training examples or the model won't learn them reliably.

Before you start training, run these checks on your labeled data. Catching errors here is cheaper than debugging a confused model later.

An I-vendor_name tag should never appear without a preceding B-vendor_name. If it does, either the B tag was missed or the entity boundaries are wrong.

def validate_bio_sequences(labels):     errors = []     for i, label in enumerate(labels):         if label.startswith('I-'):             entity_type = label[2:]             if i == 0:                 errors.append(f'I-{entity_type} at position 0 with no preceding B tag')             else:                 prev = labels[i - 1]                 if prev != f'B-{entity_type}' and prev != f'I-{entity_type}':                     errors.append(f'I-{entity_type} at position {i} follows {prev}')     return errors

Count how many times each label appears across your dataset. Flag any entity type that appears in fewer than 1% of total labels -- it likely needs more training examples.

from collections import Counter def check_distribution(all_labels):     counts = Counter(all_labels)     total = sum(counts.values())     for label, count in counts.most_common():         pct = 100 count / total *        flag = ' ** RARE **' if pct < 1.0 and label != 'O' else ''         print(f'  {label}: {count} ({pct:.1f}%){flag}')

Coordinates should fall within the page dimensions, and no bounding box should have zero or negative width or height:

def validate_bboxes(words, page_w=1000, page_h=1000):     issues = []     for w in words:         x0, y0, x1, y1 = w['bbox']         if x1 <= x0 or y1 <= y0:             issues.append(f"Zero/negative size: {w['text']}")         if x0 < 0 or y0 < 0 or x1 > page_w or y1 > page_h:             issues.append(f"Out of bounds: {w['text']}")     return issues

This depends on your document diversity and label count, but here are practical benchmarks based on fine-tuning LayoutLMv3 from a pre-trained checkpoint:

Single layout (e.g., one vendor's invoices)

These numbers assume fine-tuning from a pre-trained checkpoint, not training from scratch. Starting with a checkpoint like LayoutLMv3-base gives you document layout understanding for free -- your BIO-tagged data just teaches it which fields to extract from your specific document types.

Start small. Label 50 documents, train a model, check the error patterns, fix your taxonomy if needed, then scale up. Labeling 1,000 documents with a flawed taxonomy wastes more time than labeling 50 documents twice.

Once your data is tagged and validated, the training pipeline is straightforward. Here's the outline using Hugging Face Transformers:

from transformers import (     LayoutLMv3ForTokenClassification,     LayoutLMv3Processor,     TrainingArguments,     Trainer ) \

model = LayoutLMv3ForTokenClassification.from_pretrained(     'microsoft/layoutlmv3-base',     num_labels=len(label_list)  # your BIO labels ) \

args = TrainingArguments(     output_dir='./results',     num_train_epochs=30,     per_device_train_batch_size=4,     learning_rate=5e-5,     evaluation_strategy='epoch',     save_strategy='epoch',     load_best_model_at_end=True ) \

trainer = Trainer(     model=model,     args=args,     train_dataset=train_data,     eval_dataset=val_data ) trainer.train()

The key hyperparameters to tune are learning rate (start with 5e-5), batch size (4 or 8 depending on GPU memory), and number of epochs (30 is a good starting point for small datasets). Use an 80/10/10 train/validation/test split, and make sure document types are distributed across all three splits.

● BIO tagging assigns B (beginning), I (inside), or O (outside) to every token. It's the standard labeling scheme for token classification in document AI.

● Pair BIO labels with normalized bounding box coordinates so models learn both textual and spatial patterns.

● Design your label taxonomy before you start annotating. One label per semantic meaning, hierarchical names, and a catch-all O label.

● Validate early. Check BIO sequence integrity, label distribution, and bounding box coordinates before training.

● Start with 50-100 labeled documents, train a model, check errors, and scale up. Quality matters more than quantity.

● Use pre-labeling (regex or model-based) to speed up annotation. Manual labeling from scratch doesn't scale.

The annotation step is the foundation of every document AI pipeline. The model you train is only as good as the BIO labels you feed it. Take the time to build a clean taxonomy, label consistently, and validate before training -- it saves debugging time later and produces models that actually work in production.

#10364. Bark and Garmin have Black Friday kids’ smartwatch deals you won’t want to refuse

Garmin and Bark are competing for your money this year, and these sales make it easy to get something fun that’ll keep your kids safe and in touch.

If you’re anything like me, you didn’t give your kids a phone or tablet the second they were out of the womb. Instead, I’ve slowly introduced them to personal devices as I felt it made sense, and a smartwatch was one of the first easy ways for them to stay in touch while away from home without being introduced to the dangers of the internet. This year’s Black Friday sales on Bark and Garmin kids’ smartwatches slash up to $64 off the regular price, making them an easier decision than ever to pick up.

I’ve tested a lot of kids’ smartwatches over the years, and I can easily recommend the two in the picture above: the Bark Watch and the Garmin Bounce 2. Choosing between the two is easy enough. Garmin’s watch is simpler and comes in a slightly more durable case with fun colors. It’s slightly cheaper than Bark, but doesn’t have any health sensors on board. It mainly tracks steps, allows your kids to message or call you, and gives you geofencing and location tracking through a $9.99 per month LTE subscription plan. It’s got a 5ATM rating and a polycarbonate case, so it’s quite durable.

The Garmin Bounce 2 is preloaded with a few mini games and educational apps, plus the Garmin Jr. app has built-in unlockables and rewards parents can set for doing chores, completing schoolwork, etc. Battery life is roughly 2 days with normal use, but can be less if you’re in an area with poor LTE reception. The Garmin Bounce 2 is best for kids 10 and under.

The Bark Watch uses Samsung Galaxy Watch 8 hardware and a custom version of Wear OS. It’s a much more powerful watch and only gets a day of battery life because of it. This one feels like a midpoint between a kids’ watch and a standard Wear OS watch, as it has all the power of Wear OS but doesn’t feature the Google Play Store. It doesn’t have any games, social media, or browsers.

Since it uses Samsung’s hardware, it includes cutting-edge health sensors that your kids can use, plus great multitasking and a svelte, attractive design. Bark charges $15 per month for its plan, which includes a Bark App subscription for the whole family. I also use the Bark App on my son’s phone, which helps me monitor his usage and make sure he’s safe online. The extra $5 per month adds up, but it’s absolutely worth it if you want to add the Bark App to another phone or tablet.

The Bark Watch is fine enough for younger kids, but the design and lack of games feel more like an “older kids’ watch” to me, and my 12-year-old son agrees. Either way you go, these are fantastic choices that you’ll be happy with, especially with a nice little deal.

#10362. Jury finds Huawei stole US tech, laundered money, and broke sanctions

Criminal masterminds claimed to work for ‘Weihua’ or to have source code from ‘oscic’ instead of Cisco

A US jury has found Chinese tech giant Huawei stole tech from American companies, laundered money, and violated sanctions by building major internet infrastructure for Iran, but didn’t operate an illegal racket to get that done.

This case kicked off in 2020, when the US Department of Justice claimed entities Huawei operated in America violated federal law. The indictment led to a trial on a dozen criminal matters that has played out over the last three months in the Eastern District Court of New York.

The trial ended on Tuesday when a jury found Huawei and three subsidiary companies guilty of conspiracy to steal trade secrets, conspiracy to commit wire and bank fraud, wire fraud, bank fraud, conspiracy to defraud the United States, conspiracy to violate the International Emergency Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) and substantive violations of that law, plus money laundering conspiracy.

The Department of Justice’s celebration of the decision says Huawei was found to have stolen Cisco’s source code, after prosecutors showed evidence that “Huawei engineers left Cisco’s notes about the Cisco code, referred to Cisco using its name backwards ‘ocsic’ and described that portions of the code remained as they existed in the ‘ocsic’ code.”

Another item of evidence apparently showed a Huawei staffer photographing Fujitsu equipment while wearing a fake employee badge for a company called “Weihua” – cunningly reversing the two syllables of “Huawei.”

Another allegation the jury accepted involved Huawei staffers signing a non-disclosure agreement (NDA) ahead of a visit to T-Mobile to inspect a robot the telco created to test mobile devices.

Huawei staff ignored that NDA so thoroughly that they “sent photographs and technical details of the robot back to China” so the Chinese company could make its own robot. “When that data was not sufficient, a Huawei employee stole a part of the robot in his backpack and carried it out of T-Mobile’s controlled laboratory,” the DoJ says.

The feds say the Chinese company also “lied about its activities in Iran to banks to obtain billions of U.S. dollars and access to U.S.-dollar clearing services, which enabled Huawei to be a sophisticated participant in a global marketplace for telecommunications services — in part because of its lies.”

Huawei has issued a statement noting that the jury found it not guilty of racketeering and insisting the charges on which it was found guilty are “without legal or factual merit.”

“The five trade secret-related disputes cited by the government were all civil matters that were resolved over the past two decades,” the statement adds.

Sentencing will take place in September 2027.

The presiding judge will likely consider the fact that in 2017, US authorities fined Chinese telco equipment maker ZTE $900 million just for selling to Iran, then added an additional $1.4 billion penalty to lift a ban on doing business in the USA.

Washington has already banned Huawei from selling its telco products and personal devices in the USA. Lawmakers have little appetite to let the Chinese company return to the American market after spending billions to help American telcos replace the Huawei kit with locally made alternatives. ®

#10360. Samsung’s biggest Galaxy Z Fold 8 discounts yet are live, up to $900 for some

Samsung’s hit Galaxy Z Fold 8 saved its best deal for Black Friday, with a new low price on Amazon and a targeted Samsung.com offer that drops the price by up to $900 – here’s what you should know.

The Galaxy Z Fold 8 is usually available starting at $1,899, but discounts have been available to cut that down around Black Friday.

For everyone, Samsung’s Black Friday discount for the Galaxy Z Fold 8 is up to $450 off. That cuts the 512GB model down to $1649, while the base 256GB tier drops to $1,499. That discount is available through both Samsung.com and Amazon.

For some users, Samsung.com has a much bigger discount available. $500 off of the 512GB model, and then $900 off of the 1TB version of the Galaxy Z Fold 8 through a doubled-storage discount specifically on the “Lavender” color variant. It’s a huge discount, but it’s not showing for everyone. Samsung.com silently targets offers like this at certain users (logged in or not), so it’s essentially luck of the draw on whether or not you’ll see it. If you’re in the market, though, this is easily the best Fold 8 deal you’ll find. Let us know in the comments if you’re seeing this discount.

#10358. Every single pair of Xreal smart glasses is now on sale for Black Friday

Xreal offers an impressively wide range of smart display glasses that give you a massive virtual display without the massive price tag.

Xreal makes my favorite pairs of smart display glasses, and all of the company’s standalone pairs are on sale for the big weekend. Xreal glasses plug into any device that supports USB-C display out (your phone, handheld gaming system, laptop, etc.) and deliver a giant virtual screen only visible inside the glasses, giving you a personal home theater to enjoy your content on. Here’s the Black Friday sale in ascending price order:

Personally, I would recommend the least expensive of the bunch, the Xreal xbx a01+. It’s on sale for $249 ($50 off) and sports the most stylish looks of the three options, plus the lightest weight at 62g, the brightest display at 1600 nits, and class-leading 3,840Hz PWM dimming. It’s even got smooth tracking that’ll keep you from feeling sick when using them in a moving vehicle. I find these the most comfortable, by far, and have taken them with me on every flight since they launched a few months ago in the summer.

Choosing between the Xreal 1S and Xreal One Pro is a little harder, so let’s cover the pricing and differences that you’ll care most about. Xreal 1S is on sale for $379 ($70 off) and offers true native 3DoF spatial tracking, built-in real-time 3D conversion of 2D content, and a fully adjustable screen size and distance. Those are all upgrades over the xbx a01+ model.

Xreal One Pro is on sale for $499 ($100 off) and upgrades the Xreal 1S experience with prism lenses and a wider field of view. There are no other functional differences between One Pro and 1S other than the wider field of view and flatter lenses, so if those aren’t a big deal to you, the Xreal 1S is the “Goldilocks” pick because of its price and the range of features it offers.

Xreal 1 Pro+, on sale for the first time, upgrades the experience even further, offering a peak 1,200-nit brightness, a new AI HDR option, upgrading the 2D-to-3D conversion to 60FPS, adding extra viewing modes, and swapping out the nose pad for a more comfortable design.

Lastly, is Xreal Asus ROG R1 glasses, which are also on sale for the first time. You can choose between the normal style or the 20th anniversary style for the same $749 price. This one comes with a 240Hz display, an entire dock that connects to your PC or console of choice, more settings than any other Xreal glasses to date (making it feel like a proper monitor replacement), and that trademark Asus ROG style.

If you haven’t used a pair of smart display glasses from Xreal before, the setup is simple. Just plug the USB-C cable into your phone, laptop, or gaming handheld, and you’ll be greeted with a giant virtual screen that floats in front of your face. It’s like having a huge TV on the go, and it always looks great thanks to the micro-OLED image quality and tinted lenses. Only Xreal Aura differs from this setup, as it’s a completely standalone, VR headset-like experience in a significantly smaller form factor.
